 Path-Minimality of p p -Energy for Connected Graphs

 
 
 Yinchen Liu
 
 Address:  Institute for Interdisciplinary Information Sciences, Tsinghua University, Beijing 100084, P. R. China
 
 Email address: liuyinch23@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn 
 
 and 
 Quanyu Tang
 
 Address:  School of Mathematics and Statistics, Xi’an Jiaotong University, Xi’an 710049, P. R. China
 
 Email address: tangquanyu827@gmail.com 
 


 Abstract.

 
 Let G G be a simple connected graph on n n vertices, and let λ 1 ​ ( G ) , λ 2 ​ ( G ) , … , λ n ​ ( G ) \lambda_{1}(G),\lambda_{2}(G),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(G) be the eigenvalues of its adjacency matrix A ⁡ ( G ) A(G) . For p > 0 p>0 , define the p p -energy of G G by ℰ p ​ ( G ) = ∑ i = 1 n | λ i ​ ( G ) | p \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\lambda_{i}(G)|^{p} . We prove that, for every real number p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 and every simple connected graph G G on n n vertices,

 
 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) , \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}), 
 
 
 
 
 where P n P_{n} denotes the path on n n vertices. Moreover, for each fixed p > 2 p>2 , equality holds if and only if G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . Together with the previously known star-minimality results, this completes the solution of two questions of Nikiforov.

 
 The proof combines two different comparison principles. For 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , we use a bipartite reduction, a Mellin representation of fractional powers, and a determinant comparison involving matching generating polynomials and tree shifts. For p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , we prove a second-order stop-loss comparison for the squared singular values of bipartite graphs. This comparison is established by rank-one spectral-shift estimates, deletion-minimal counterexamples, and a finite certified analysis of the terminal sparse-sun configurations.

 


 
 Key words and phrases: 
graph energy, Schatten norm, spectral graph theory


 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 05C50; Secondary 05C35, 15A18

 1. Introduction


 
 1.1. Background and main result


 
 Throughout this paper, all graphs are finite, simple, undirected, and unweighted. Let G G be a graph with vertex set V ⁡ ( G ) = { v 1 , … , v n } V(G)=\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n}\} , and let A ⁡ ( G ) A(G) be its n × n n\times n adjacency matrix, whose ( i , j ) (i,j) -entry is 1 1 if v i ​ v j ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) v_{i}v_{j}\in E(G) and is 0 0 otherwise. We write

 

 
 
 λ 1 ​ ( G ) , λ 2 ​ ( G ) , … , λ n ​ ( G ) \lambda_{1}(G),\lambda_{2}(G),\ldots,\lambda_{n}(G) 
 
 
 
 for the eigenvalues of A ⁡ ( G ) A(G) , counted with multiplicity. For a real number x x , we write x + := max ⁡ { x , 0 } x_{+}:=\max\{x,0\} for its positive part. For p > 0 p>0 we define the p p -energy of G G by

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) := ∑ i = 1 n | λ i ​ ( G ) | p . \mathcal{E}_{p}(G):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\lambda_{i}(G)|^{p}. 
 
 
 
 For p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 , the Schatten p p -norm of a matrix M M is

 

 
 
 ‖ M ‖ S p := ( ∑ j s j ​ ( M ) p ) 1 / p , \left\lVert M\right\rVert_{S_{p}}:=\left(\sum_{j}s_{j}(M)^{p}\right)^{1/p}, 
 
 
 
 where s j ​ ( M ) s_{j}(M) are the singular values of M M . Since A ⁡ ( G ) A(G) is real symmetric, its singular values are | λ 1 ​ ( G ) | , … , | λ n ​ ( G ) | |\lambda_{1}(G)|,\ldots,|\lambda_{n}(G)| . Thus, for p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 , we have ℰ p ​ ( G ) = ‖ A ⁡ ( G ) ‖ S p p \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)=\left\lVert A(G)\right\rVert_{S_{p}}^{p} . Following Nikiforov’s notation, one may write ‖ G ‖ p \left\lVert G\right\rVert_{p} for ‖ A ⁡ ( G ) ‖ S p \left\lVert A(G)\right\rVert_{S_{p}} ; after taking p p th roots, inequalities for ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G) with p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 are exactly inequalities for these graph norms  [ 18 ] .



 
 Let P n P_{n} denote the path graph and S n S_{n} the star graph on n n vertices. Nikiforov asked whether paths and stars remain extremal for Schatten norms in the following sense  [ 18 ] :



 
 Question 1.1 ( [ 18 , Question 4.51] ) . 

 
 Let G G be a connected graph of order n n . 

 
 (a) 
 
 Is it true that ‖ G ‖ p ≥ ‖ P n ‖ p \|G\|_{p}\geq\|P_{n}\|_{p} for every p > 2 p>2 ? 




 (b) 
 
 Is it true that ‖ G ‖ p ≥ ‖ S n ‖ p \|G\|_{p}\geq\|S_{n}\|_{p} for every 1 < p < 2 1<p<2 ? 




 




 
 Question 1.2 ( [ 18 , Question 4.52] ) . 

 
 Let T T be a tree of order n n . 

 
 (a) 
 
 Is it true that ‖ S n ‖ p ≥ ‖ T ‖ p ≥ ‖ P n ‖ p \|S_{n}\|_{p}\geq\|T\|_{p}\geq\|P_{n}\|_{p} for every p > 2 p>2 ? 




 (b) 
 
 Is it true that ‖ P n ‖ p ≥ ‖ T ‖ p ≥ ‖ S n ‖ p \|P_{n}\|_{p}\geq\|T\|_{p}\geq\|S_{n}\|_{p} for every 1 < p < 2 1<p<2 ? 




 




 
 These questions extend the classical extremal behavior of paths and stars for
ordinary graph energy and for the spectral radius.



 
 Several parts of this picture were known. If p = 2 ​ k p=2k is a positive even integer, then

 

 
 
 ℰ 2 ​ k ​ ( G ) = tr ⁡ A ​ ( G ) 2 ​ k , \mathcal{E}_{2k}(G)=\tr A(G)^{2k}, 
 
 
 
 which counts closed walks of length 2 ​ k 2k . Csikvári proved that among connected graphs of fixed order the path minimizes the number of closed walks of every length; in Schatten-norm language this gives ‖ G ‖ 2 ​ k ≥ ‖ P n ‖ 2 ​ k \left\lVert G\right\rVert_{2k}\geq\left\lVert P_{n}\right\rVert_{2k} for every connected G G   [ 8 , 18 ] . On the tree side, Arizmendi and Arizmendi proved the star upper bound ℰ p ​ ( T ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( S n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(T)\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(S_{n}) for p > 2 p>2 by a bipartite Schatten-energy estimate [ 1 ] . For the opposite range, Arizmendi and Guerrero proved that for every tree T T on n n vertices and every 0 < p < 2 0<p<2 ,

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( S n ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( T ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) , \mathcal{E}_{p}(S_{n})\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(T)\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}), 
 
 
 
 using Coulson-type integral formulas and the tree quasi-order  [ 2 ] . Tang, Liu and Wang later settled the connected-graph star-minimality problem in the stronger form ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( S n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(S_{n}) for every connected G G and every 0 < p < 2 0<p<2 , with equality only for the star [ 22 ] .



 
 Thus the remaining issue was path-minimality for real exponents p > 2 p>2 . In the notation above, the connected-graph version asks whether ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) for every connected n n -vertex graph G G , while the tree version asks for the same inequality when G G is restricted to be a tree. The connected-graph version clearly implies the tree version. We shall refer to these two questions as Remaining Problem A and Remaining Problem B, respectively:



 
 Remaining Problem A. 
Let G G be a connected graph of order n n . Is it true that, for every real number p > 2 p>2 , ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) ?



 
 Remaining Problem B. 
Let T T be a tree of order n n . Is it true that, for every real number p > 2 p>2 , ℰ p ​ ( T ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(T)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) ?



 
 The limiting case p = ∞ p=\infty is consistent with this expectation: Lovász and Pelikán proved that the path minimizes the spectral radius among trees of fixed order, and the same conclusion among connected graphs follows by taking a spanning tree and using monotonicity of the spectral radius under edge addition  [ 16 ] .



 
 The purpose of this paper is to resolve both remaining problems. Our main result is the following theorem.



 
 Theorem 1.3 . 

 
 Let G G be a connected graph on n n vertices. Then for every real number
 p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 , 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) . \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, for each fixed p > 2 p>2 , equality holds if and only if G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . 





 
 Theorem  1.3 proves Remaining Problem A, and therefore also proves Remaining Problem B. We emphasize that the proof is not a direct interpolation from even exponents. Instead, it is based on two comparison principles: an integrated resolvent comparison for the range 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , and an admissible-function comparison for the range p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , the latter being proved via a second-order stop-loss comparison for the squared singular values. These two principles are introduced in the proof outline below.





 
 1.2. Proof outline


 
 We first provide a standard reduction to the bipartite case, and then give an outline of the two comparison principles and their proofs.



 
 Reduction to bipartite graphs.  We reduce the problem from arbitrary connected graphs to connected
bipartite spanning subgraphs. Given a spanning tree of G G , its bipartition
defines a cut. Keeping only the edges crossing this cut gives a connected
bipartite spanning subgraph H H . At the matrix level, A ⁡ ( H ) A(H) is the
arithmetic mean of A ⁡ ( G ) A(G) and − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D -DA(G)D for a signature matrix D D .
Hence, for every even convex function F F , the trace spectral functional
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( λ i ) \sum_{i}F(\lambda_{i}) decreases under this operation. In particular,
 ℰ p ​ ( H ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(G) for p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 . It is therefore enough
to prove the required lower bound for connected bipartite graphs.



 
 For a connected bipartite graph G G , write μ i ​ ( G ) \mu_{i}(G) for the squared
singular values of a biadjacency matrix, so that
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) = 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) p / 2 \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)=2\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{p/2} . The two ranges 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 and
 p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 are handled by different comparison principles.



 
 1.2.1. The case 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 


 
 We set α = p / 2 ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha=p/2\in(1,2) and introduce the integrated resolvent

 

 
 
 r 1 ​ ( y ) := ∫ 0 y s 1 + s ​ 𝑑 s = y − log ⁡ ( 1 + y ) , R 1 ​ ( G , x ) := ∑ i r 1 ​ ( x ​ μ i ​ ( G ) ) . r_{1}(y):=\int_{0}^{y}\frac{s}{1+s}\,ds=y-\log(1+y),\qquad R_{1}(G;x):=\sum_{i}r_{1}(x\mu_{i}(G)). 
 
 
 
 The Mellin identity in Lemma  3.1 expresses u α u^{\alpha} as a
positive integral of r 1 ​ ( x ​ u ) r_{1}(xu) over x > 0 x>0 , so a pointwise comparison

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) ( x > 0 ) R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x)\qquad(x>0) 
 
 
 
 implies ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) for every p ∈ ( 2 , 4 ) p\in(2,4) .
The advantage of passing to R 1 R_{1} is that it admits a clean determinant
expression. Given the bipartition V ⁡ ( G ) = X ⊔ Y V(G)=X\sqcup Y , the
 biadjacency matrix B G B_{G} is the | X | × | Y | |X|\times|Y| 0-1 matrix
whose ( i , j ) (i,j) -entry equals 1 if x i ​ y j ∈ E ⁡ ( G ) x_{i}y_{j}\in E(G) and 0 otherwise.
With this notation,

 

 
 
 R 1 ( G ; x ) = x | E ( G ) | − log det ( I + x B G ⊤ B G ) . R_{1}(G;x)=x|E(G)|-\log\det(I+xB_{G}^{\top}B_{G}). 
 
 
 
 For a tree T T , it can be shown that the matrix I + x ​ B T ⊤ ​ B T I+xB_{T}^{\top}B_{T} has determinant equal to the
matching generating polynomial M T ​ ( x ) := ∑ k ≥ 0 m k ​ ( T ) ​ x k M_{T}(x):=\sum_{k\geq 0}m_{k}(T)\,x^{k} , where
 m k ​ ( T ) m_{k}(T) counts k k -matchings of T T . Thus the R 1 R_{1} -comparison for
trees is equivalent to a pointwise comparison of matching generating polynomials:

 

 
 
 M T ​ ( x ) ≤ M P n ​ ( x ) ( x ≥ 0 ) . M_{T}(x)\leq M_{P_{n}}(x)\qquad(x\geq 0). 
 
 
 


 
 The one-branch tree shift.  The matching-polynomial comparison for trees is proved by using the inverse
direction of a one-branch special case of Csikvári’s generalized tree
shift  [ 8 ] . Csikvári’s generalized tree shift induces a
poset on trees of fixed order; in that poset the path is the unique minimal
element and the star is the unique maximal element, and a proper generalized
tree shift increases the number of closed walks of every length. Here we use
the same local geometry in the opposite, path-directed direction.



 
 For the present argument, the required point is the following coefficientwise matching-polynomial monotonicity. Suppose that a tree T T contains a vertex v v with two pendant path arms

 

 
 
 v u 1 ⋯ u a and v w 1 ⋯ w b , a , b ≥ 1 . vu_{1}\cdots u_{a}\qquad\text{and}\qquad vw_{1}\cdots w_{b},\qquad a,b\geq 1. 
 
 
 
 Let T ′ T^{\prime} be obtained from T T by deleting the edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and adding the edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} . Thus the entire arm w 1 ⋯ w b w_{1}\cdots w_{b} is reattached to the far end u a u_{a} of the first arm, and the two pendant arms are replaced by the single pendant path v u 1 ⋯ u a w 1 ⋯ w b vu_{1}\cdots u_{a}w_{1}\cdots w_{b} ; see Figure  1 . Then

 

 
 
 M T ′ ​ ( x ) = M T ​ ( x ) + x 2 ​ Q ​ ( x ) ≥ M T ​ ( x ) ( x ≥ 0 ) , M_{T^{\prime}}(x)=M_{T}(x)+x^{2}Q(x)\geq M_{T}(x)\qquad(x\geq 0), 
 
 
 
 where Q Q is a polynomial with non-negative coefficients.



 
 Every non-path tree admits such a path-directed shift: choose a branching
vertex of maximal distance from a fixed root leaf, and choose two pendant path
components emanating from it away from the root. This shift reduces the number
of leaves by one. Iterating the operation therefore terminates at the unique
tree with no branching vertex, namely P n P_{n} . This yields M T ​ ( x ) ≤ M P n ​ ( x ) M_{T}(x)\leq M_{P_{n}}(x) , and hence R 1 ​ ( T , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) R_{1}(T;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x) for every tree T T .



 
 Extension to general bipartite graphs.  The tree inequality extends to all connected bipartite graphs by induction on
 | V ⁡ ( G ) | |V(G)| , with trees as the base. Suppose G G is not a tree; choose a
vertex v v on a cycle and let G − v = G 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ G q G-v=G_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup G_{q} with
 n i = | V ⁡ ( G i ) | n_{i}=|V(G_{i})| and n = 1 + ∑ i n i n=1+\sum_{i}n_{i} . Since v v lies on a cycle, two of
its neighbors remain connected after its removal, so q ≤ d − 1 q\leq d-1 where
 d = d G ​ ( v ) d=d_{G}(v) . Applying the inductive hypothesis to each component and using
additivity of R 1 R_{1} over disjoint unions gives

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G − v , x ) = ∑ i R 1 ​ ( G i , x ) ≥ ∑ i R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) . R_{1}(G-v;x)=\sum_{i}R_{1}(G_{i};x)\geq\sum_{i}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x). 
 
 
 
 It therefore suffices to show that the gain ,

 

 
 
 gain ⁡ ( v , G , x ) := R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( G − v , x ) , \mathrm{gain}(v,G;x):=R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(G-v;x), 
 
 
 
 is at least the path deficit R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) − ∑ i R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) R_{1}(P_{n};x)-\sum_{i}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x) .
Let b ∈ { 0 , 1 } | Y | b\in\{0,1\}^{|Y|} be the indicator vector of N G ​ ( v ) N_{G}(v) on the right
side of the bipartition and set M = B G − v ⊤ ​ B G − v M=B_{G-v}^{\top}B_{G-v} . The matrix
determinant lemma applied to the rank-one update B G ⊤ ​ B G = M + b ​ b ⊤ B_{G}^{\top}B_{G}=M+bb^{\top} 
yields the gain formula

 

 
 
 gain ⁡ ( v , G , x ) = x ​ d − log ⁡ ( 1 + x ​ b ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ​ b ) . \mathrm{gain}(v,G;x)=xd-\log\bigl(1+xb^{\top}(I+xM)^{-1}b\bigr). 
 
 
 
 Since M ⪰ 0 M\succeq 0 implies ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ⪯ I (I+xM)^{-1}\preceq I , we have
 b ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ​ b ≤ b ⊤ ​ b = d b^{\top}(I+xM)^{-1}b\leq b^{\top}b=d , so
 gain ⁡ ( v , G , x ) ≥ x ​ d − log ⁡ ( 1 + x ​ d ) = r 1 ​ ( d ​ x ) \mathrm{gain}(v,G;x)\geq xd-\log(1+xd)=r_{1}(dx) . Since r 1 r_{1} is
increasing and d ≥ q + 1 d\geq q+1 , this yields gain ⁡ ( v , G , x ) ≥ r 1 ​ ( ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) \mathrm{gain}(v,G;x)\geq r_{1}((q+1)x) .



 
 For the path deficit: a similar rank-one update argument together with the star-of-paths tree—the
tree formed by connecting v v to one endpoint of each P n i P_{n_{i}} as a
hub—shows R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) ≤ ∑ i R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) + r 1 ​ ( ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) R_{1}(P_{n};x)\leq\sum_{i}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x)+r_{1}((q+1)x) . Combining
the two estimates gives R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x) , completing the induction.





 
 1.2.2. The case p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 


 
 For p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , we prove a stronger comparison for a whole cone of test
functions, with the power functions as special cases. More precisely, we work
with functions F : [ 0 , ∞ ) → ℝ F\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R} satisfying F ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 F(0)=0 , F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime}(0)\geq 0 and F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq 0 in the sense made precise later: F ∈ C 2 ​ ( [ 0 , ∞ ) ) F\in C^{2}([0,\infty)) , F ′′ F^{\prime\prime} is locally
absolutely continuous, and F ′′′ ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq 0 almost everywhere on ( 0 , ∞ ) (0,\infty) .
We call such functions admissible third-order convex . The comparison
proved in this range is

 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( G ) 2 ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( P n ) 2 ) \sum_{i=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{i}(G)^{2}\right)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{i}(P_{n})^{2}\right) 
 
 
 
 for every connected graph G G on n n vertices and every admissible
third-order convex function F F . Taking F ⁡ ( u ) = u p / 2 F(u)=u^{p/2} gives ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) for every p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 .



 
 Reduction to second-order stop-loss.  Fix an admissible third-order convex function F F . For bipartite graphs, the comparison for F F is reduced to a second-order stop-loss comparison for squared singular values. If μ i ​ ( G ) \mu_{i}(G) denotes the squared singular values of a biadjacency matrix of G G , set

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) := ∑ i ( μ i ​ ( G ) − t ) + 2 . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G):=\sum_{i}(\mu_{i}(G)-t)_{+}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Taylor’s formula with integral remainder gives, for u ≥ 0 u\geq 0 ,

 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( u ) = F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ​ u + F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) 2 ​ u 2 + 1 2 ​ ∫ 0 ∞ F ′′′ ​ ( t ) ​ ( u − t ) + 2 ​ 𝑑 t . F(u)=F^{\prime}(0)u+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}u^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)(u-t)_{+}^{2}\,dt. 
 
 
 
 Here F ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 F(0)=0 has been used, and F ′′′ F^{\prime\prime\prime} is understood in the almost-everywhere sense specified in the definition of admissibility. Applying this identity to the squared singular values of G G and P n P_{n} , and then subtracting, gives

 

 
 
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( G ) ) − ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( P n ) ) \displaystyle\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(G))-\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(P_{n})) 
 = F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ​ ( | E ⁡ ( G ) | − ( n − 1 ) ) \displaystyle=F^{\prime}(0)\bigl(|E(G)|-(n-1)\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 
 + F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) 2 ​ ( 𝖲 0 ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 0 ​ ( P n ) ) \displaystyle+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}\bigl(\mathsf{S}_{0}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{0}(P_{n})\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 
 + 1 2 ∫ 0 ∞ F ′ ′ ′ ( t ) ( 𝖲 t ( G ) − 𝖲 t ( P n ) ) d t . \displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)\bigl(\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})\bigr)\,dt. 
 
 
 
 In the first term we used ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) = | E ⁡ ( G ) | \sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)=|E(G)| and ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) = n − 1 \sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})=n-1 . Since G G is connected, | E ⁡ ( G ) | ≥ n − 1 |E(G)|\geq n-1 . Thus the first term is
non-negative because F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime}(0)\geq 0 . If the stop-loss domination

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) ( t ≥ 0 ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})\qquad(t\geq 0) 
 
 
 
 holds, then the second term is non-negative because F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq 0 , and the
integral term is non-negative because F ′′′ ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq 0 a.e. Therefore the
admissible-function comparison follows from this stop-loss domination.



 
 This domination is proved below first for trees and then, by a deletion
argument, for all connected bipartite graphs.



 
 The tree case.  The tree case of the stop-loss comparison again uses the one-branch tree
shift, but the argument is subtler than the corresponding R 1 R_{1} comparison.
For R 1 R_{1} , coefficientwise monotonicity of the matching generating
polynomial is enough. For the second-order stop-loss quantities, one has to
control how the roots of the matching polynomial move under the shift.



 
 Let T ′ T^{\prime} be obtained from T T by deleting the old edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and adding
the new edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} . For 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 , let G θ G_{\theta} be the
non-negatively weighted graph in which the old edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} has weight
 1 − θ 1-\theta , the new edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} has weight θ \theta , and all other
edges have weight 1 1 . Let M θ := M G θ M_{\theta}:=M_{G_{\theta}} be its weighted matching generating polynomial. Thus M 0 = M T M_{0}=M_{T} and M 1 = M T ′ M_{1}=M_{T^{\prime}} . A direct matching calculation gives ∂ θ M θ ​ ( x ) = x 2 ​ Q ​ ( x ) \partial_{\theta}M_{\theta}(x)=x^{2}Q(x) , where Q Q is an explicit polynomial with non-negative coefficients.



 
 The point of introducing M θ M_{\theta} is that its roots can be followed
continuously. By the Heilmann–Lieb theorem  [ 11 ] , M θ M_{\theta} has only real
negative zeros for every 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 . Bronshtein’s theorem on
hyperbolic polynomial families  [ 4 ] then allows us to label the corresponding
non-negative root parameters by locally Lipschitz functions
 μ j ​ ( θ ) \mu_{j}(\theta) , after adding zero parameters if necessary, so that

 

 
 
 M θ ​ ( x ) = ∏ j ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) ) . M_{\theta}(x)=\prod_{j}(1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)). 
 
 
 
 For almost every θ \theta , differentiating this factorization gives

 

 
 
 ∑ j x ​ μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) = x 2 ​ Q ​ ( x ) M θ ​ ( x ) . \sum_{j}\frac{x\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)}{1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)}=\frac{x^{2}Q(x)}{M_{\theta}(x)}. 
 
 
 
 Equivalently, if ν ˙ θ := ∑ j μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) ​ δ μ j ​ ( θ ) \dot{\nu}_{\theta}:=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)\,\delta_{\mu_{j}(\theta)} is the signed speed measure of the roots, then its Stieltjes transform is 𝒮 ν ˙ θ ​ ( x ) = x ​ Q ​ ( x ) / M θ ​ ( x ) \mathcal{S}_{\dot{\nu}_{\theta}}(x)=xQ(x)/M_{\theta}(x) .



 
 It remains to convert the Stieltjes-transform identity into the sign of the
stop-loss derivative. We use the Stieltjes cones introduced below: briefly,
 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} is the cone generated by finite non-negative linear
combinations of products of at most k k factors

 

 
 
 ( 1 + α ​ x ) − 1 , α ≥ 0 , (1+\alpha x)^{-1},\qquad\alpha\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 and 𝒞 ≤ k 0 \mathcal{C}_{\leq k}^{0} denotes the subcone with O ⁡ ( x − 1 ) O(x^{-1}) decay at
infinity. The deletion identities for the tree-shift interpolation give the
key membership

 

 
 
 Q M θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 . \frac{Q}{M_{\theta}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0}. 
 
 
 


 
 For almost every θ \theta , set ν ˙ θ := ∑ j μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) ​ δ μ j ​ ( θ ) \dot{\nu}_{\theta}:=\sum_{j}\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)\,\delta_{\mu_{j}(\theta)} . Then 𝒮 ν ˙ θ ​ ( x ) = x ​ Q ​ ( x ) / M θ ​ ( x ) \mathcal{S}_{\dot{\nu}_{\theta}}(x)=xQ(x)/M_{\theta}(x) . The analytic cone lemma proved below states that if a finite signed atomic
measure σ \sigma satisfies

 

 
 
 𝒮 σ ​ ( x ) = x ​ g ​ ( x ) , g ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 , \mathcal{S}_{\sigma}(x)=xg(x),\qquad g\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0}, 
 
 
 
 then ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( λ ) ≤ 0 \int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}\,d\sigma(\lambda)\leq 0 for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Applying this lemma to σ = ν ˙ θ \sigma=\dot{\nu}_{\theta} and
 g = Q / M θ g=Q/M_{\theta} , we obtain

 

 
 
 d d ​ θ ​ 𝖲 t ​ ( θ ) = d d ​ θ ​ ∑ j ( μ j ​ ( θ ) − t ) + 2 = 2 ​ ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + ​ d ​ ν ˙ θ ​ ( λ ) ≤ 0 . \frac{d}{d\theta}\mathsf{S}_{t}(\theta)=\frac{d}{d\theta}\sum_{j}(\mu_{j}(\theta)-t)_{+}^{2}=2\int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}\,d\dot{\nu}_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq 0. 
 
 
 
 Thus 𝖲 t ​ ( θ ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(\theta) is non-increasing along the interpolation.



 
 At the endpoints, M 0 = M T M_{0}=M_{T} and M 1 = M T ′ M_{1}=M_{T^{\prime}} are matching polynomials of
trees, so the forest determinant identity identifies their root parameters
with the squared singular values of T T and T ′ T^{\prime} , respectively. Therefore 𝖲 t ​ ( T ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( T ′ ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(T)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(T^{\prime}) for t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Iterating along the finite sequence of one-branch shifts from T T to P n P_{n} yields 𝖲 t ​ ( T ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(T)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}) for every tree T T on n n vertices.



 
 Deletion for general bipartite graphs.  The non-tree case uses a deletion-minimal counterexample argument. When a
vertex v v is reinserted, the rank-one trace formula and Jensen’s inequality
turn the spectral update into a one-dimensional interval integral. We call
 v v deletable if the componentwise inductive hypothesis

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) = ∑ i 𝖲 t ​ ( G i ) ≥ ∑ i 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) , G − v = G 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ G q , \mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)=\sum_{i}\mathsf{S}_{t}(G_{i})\geq\sum_{i}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}}),\qquad G-v=G_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup G_{q}, 
 
 
 
 together with the rank-one spectral-shift bound for reinserting v v , already
implies 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}) . Comparing this local gain with the corresponding path-splicing cost shows that
 v v is deletable whenever d G ​ ( v ) − κ ⁡ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 d_{G}(v)-\kappa(G-v)\geq 2 , where κ ⁡ ( G − v ) \kappa(G-v) is the number of components of G − v G-v . A sharper two-arm
estimate handles the borderline case.



 
 Reduction to terminal configurations.  These deletion criteria force any minimal counterexample into a rigid terminal
family. Every 2 2 -connected block must be an even cycle, reducing the graph
to a sun graph , that is, an even cycle with pendant leaves attached to
some cycle vertices. The sun must moreover be sparse, meaning that at most one
pendant leaf is attached to each cycle vertex. Its loaded cycle vertices,
namely the cycle vertices carrying pendant leaves, must be 3 3 -separated
along the cycle: any two of them have cyclic distance at least 3 3 . The pure
even-cycle case is excluded by a trigonometric pair comparison and a
weak-majorization argument.



 
 Excluding terminal sparse-sun configurations.  It remains to exclude 3 3 -separated sparse suns. Choose a loaded cycle vertex
and delete one of its unloaded cycle neighbors, namely a neighboring cycle
vertex carrying no pendant leaf. The rank-one spectral-shift formula reduces
the required deletability check to a comparison between the local interval
contribution of this deletion and the corresponding endpoint contribution for
the path extension P n − 1 ↝ P n P_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow P_{n} . This local comparison is
proved in three ranges. For 0 ≤ t ≤ 3 0\leq t\leq 3 , it follows from a Jensen lower
envelope; for 3 ≤ t ≤ 4 3\leq t\leq 4 , from a fifth-moment lower envelope and exact
Bernstein certificates recorded in the appendix; and for t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 , from the
fact that the path endpoint contribution vanishes. Hence the chosen vertex is
deletable, contradicting minimality. This eliminates the terminal sparse-sun
case and completes the stop-loss comparison for connected bipartite graphs.



 
 Combining the R 1 R_{1} -comparison for 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , the admissible-function comparison for p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , and the identity ℰ 2 ​ ( G ) = 2 ​ | E ⁡ ( G ) | \mathcal{E}_{2}(G)=2|E(G)| , we obtain the p p -energy inequality for connected bipartite graphs for every p ≥ 2 p\geq 2 . The bipartite reduction then extends the result to all connected graphs.





 
 1.2.3. Equality


 
 The case p = 4 p=4 is the anchor:
the closed-walk formula for tr ⁡ A 4 \operatorname{tr}A^{4} shows that equality at
 p = 4 p=4 holds only for P n P_{n} .



 
 We first discuss equality for connected bipartite graphs. For 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , equality in the Mellin representation forces
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) = R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) R_{1}(G;x)=R_{1}(P_{n};x) for all x > 0 x>0 . Expanding at x = 0 x=0 then gives
equality at p = 4 p=4 , hence G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . For p > 4 p>4 , take F ⁡ ( u ) = u p / 2 F(u)=u^{p/2} in the stop-loss decomposition above. Since
 F ′ ​ ( 0 ) = F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) = 0 F^{\prime}(0)=F^{\prime\prime}(0)=0 and F ′′′ ​ ( t ) > 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)>0 for t > 0 t>0 , equality forces 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) = 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)=\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}) for all t > 0 t>0 , and
letting t ↓ 0 t\downarrow 0 gives equality at p = 4 p=4 . Thus G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} in the
bipartite case.



 
 For a general connected graph, equality in the bipartite reduction forces no
edges to be lost when passing to the bipartite spanning subgraph. Hence the
graph itself is the bipartite path. Therefore, for every p > 2 p>2 , equality
holds if and only if G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} .







 
 1.3. Scope of the paper


 
 The present paper is devoted to the proof of Theorem  1.3 . Some of the comparison principles developed below also have consequences for positive adjacency p p -energies, Laplacian-type spectral functionals, and line graphs. These applications are developed in the companion paper [ 14 ] . We do not include them here, so as to keep the present paper focused on Nikiforov’s path-minimality problem.





 
 1.4. Paper organization


 
 Section  2 collects the preliminary material used
throughout the paper. We first fix the spectral notation for general and
bipartite graphs, prove the reduction from connected graphs to connected
bipartite spanning subgraphs, and then record the matching-polynomial,
tree-shift, and Stieltjes-transform tools needed later.



 
 Section  3 treats the range 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 . The main object
there is the integrated resolvent functional R 1 R_{1} . A Mellin representation
reduces fractional power sums to a pointwise R 1 R_{1} -comparison, which is first
proved for trees by matching-polynomial monotonicity and then extended to all
connected bipartite graphs by a vertex-deletion induction.



 
 Sections  4 – 6 
develop the second-order stop-loss comparison needed for the range p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 .
Section  4 introduces the stop-loss framework, proves the
tree-shift and path-splicing inputs, and establishes the pure even-cycle
comparison. Section  5 develops the deletion theory and
reduces any minimal counterexample to a sparse-sun configuration whose loaded
cycle vertices are 3 3 -separated. Section  6 
excludes the remaining sparse-sun case with pendant leaves by a
support-neighbor deletion argument and a finite certified comparison.



 
 Section  7 combines the R 1 R_{1} -comparison for 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , the
second-order stop-loss comparison for p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , and the bipartite reduction to
prove Theorem  1.3 , including the equality case.



 
 The appendices contain the finite certified and elementary verifications used
in the proof. Appendix  A gives the rigorous interval
certificate for the low-range path-splicing estimate. Appendix  B 
proves the interval domination inequalities used in the deletion step, and
Appendix  C records the exact polynomial certificates used
in the terminal sparse-sun argument.







 
 2. Preliminaries


 
 This section collects the auxiliary material used in the proof. We first fix
the spectral notation and prove the bipartite reduction. We then record the
matching-polynomial and tree-shift tools used in the R 1 R_{1} -comparison, and
finally recall the Stieltjes-transform and cone-closure facts needed for the
second-order stop-loss framework.



 
 We shall use the following elementary notation. For a real number x x , we write ⌊ x ⌋ \lfloor x\rfloor for the floor of x x , and { x } := x − ⌊ x ⌋ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \{x\}:=x-\lfloor x\rfloor\in[0,1) for its fractional part.



 
 2.1. Spectral notation and bipartite reduction


 
 We first record the spectral notation used in the main proof. For general
background on spectral graph theory, see, for example, [ 7 ] . We
use the adjacency-eigenvalue and p p -energy notation introduced in
Section  1.1 .



 
 For real symmetric matrices A A and B B of the same size, we write
 A ⪰ B A\succeq B if A − B A-B is positive semidefinite. Let M M be a real
symmetric matrix. By the spectral theorem, we may write

 

 
 
 M = U ​ diag ⁡ ( λ 1 , … , λ n ) ​ U ⊤ , M=U\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})U^{\top}, 
 
 
 
 where U U is orthogonal and λ 1 , … , λ n \lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n} are the eigenvalues
of M M , counted with multiplicity. We write spec ⁡ ( M ) \spec(M) for the spectrum of
 M M . If f f is a real function defined on spec ⁡ ( M ) \spec(M) , we define

 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( M ) := U ​ diag ⁡ ( f ⁡ ( λ 1 ) , … , f ⁡ ( λ n ) ) ​ U ⊤ . f(M):=U\operatorname{diag}\bigl(f(\lambda_{1}),\ldots,f(\lambda_{n})\bigr)U^{\top}. 
 
 
 
 In particular, for t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} , we write

 

 
 
 ( M − t ​ I ) + := U ​ diag ⁡ ( ( λ 1 − t ) + , … , ( λ n − t ) + ) ​ U ⊤ . (M-tI)_{+}:=U\operatorname{diag}\bigl((\lambda_{1}-t)_{+},\ldots,(\lambda_{n}-t)_{+}\bigr)U^{\top}. 
 
 
 


 
 Now assume that G G is bipartite with a fixed bipartition V ⁡ ( G ) = X ⊔ Y V(G)=X\sqcup Y ,
and let B G B_{G} be the | X | × | Y | |X|\times|Y| biadjacency matrix. Then

 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( G ) = ( 0 B G B G ⊤ 0 ) . A(G)=\begin{pmatrix}0&B_{G}\\
B_{G}^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the adjacency spectrum is symmetric about the origin. Its nonzero
eigenvalues occur in opposite pairs

 

 
 
 ± σ 1 ​ ( G ) , … , ± σ m ​ ( G ) , \pm\sigma_{1}(G),\ldots,\pm\sigma_{m}(G), 
 
 
 
 where σ 1 ​ ( G ) , … , σ m ​ ( G ) \sigma_{1}(G),\ldots,\sigma_{m}(G) are the positive singular values of
 B G B_{G} . We write

 

 
 
 μ i ​ ( G ) := σ i ​ ( G ) 2 . \mu_{i}(G):=\sigma_{i}(G)^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Equivalently, the μ i ​ ( G ) \mu_{i}(G) are the nonzero eigenvalues of both
 B G ​ B G ⊤ B_{G}B_{G}^{\top} and B G ⊤ ​ B G B_{G}^{\top}B_{G} . Hence, for every p > 0 p>0 ,

 

 
 (1) 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) = 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) p / 2 , ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) = tr ⁡ ( B G ⊤ ​ B G ) = | E ⁡ ( G ) | . \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)=2\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{p/2},\qquad\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)=\tr(B_{G}^{\top}B_{G})=|E(G)|. 
 
 
 
 If G = ⨆ ℓ = 1 r G ℓ G=\bigsqcup_{\ell=1}^{r}G_{\ell} is a disjoint union of bipartite graphs,
then the multiset of squared singular values of G G is the union of the
corresponding multisets for the components. Hence quantities defined as sums
over the μ i ​ ( G ) \mu_{i}(G) are additive over disjoint unions.



 
 Next, we state a structural reduction that allows us to work entirely in the
bipartite setting for even convex test functions.



 
 Lemma 2.1 . 

 
 Let G G be a connected graph on n n vertices. Then there exists a connected bipartite spanning subgraph H H of G G such that 

 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( H ) ) ≤ ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( G ) ) \sum_{i=1}^{n}F(\lambda_{i}(H))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}F(\lambda_{i}(G)) 
 
 
 
 for every even convex function F : ℝ → ℝ F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} . In particular, for every p ≥ 1 p\geq 1 , we have ℰ p ​ ( H ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(G) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let T T be a spanning tree of G G , and let V ⁡ ( G ) = X ⊔ Y V(G)=X\sqcup Y be the bipartition
of T T . Let H H be the spanning subgraph of G G consisting of all edges of
 G G that cross the cut ( X , Y ) (X,Y) . Since T ⊆ H ⊆ G T\subseteq H\subseteq G , the graph
 H H is connected and bipartite. 



 
 Order the vertices so that the vertices of X X come first. Then 

 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( G ) = ( A X B B ⊤ A Y ) , A ⁡ ( H ) = ( 0 B B ⊤ 0 ) . A(G)=\begin{pmatrix}A_{X}&B\\
B^{\top}&A_{Y}\end{pmatrix},\qquad A(H)=\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
B^{\top}&0\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 With D = diag ⁡ ( I | X | , − I | Y | ) D=\operatorname{diag}(I_{|X|},-I_{|Y|}) , we have 

 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( H ) = A ⁡ ( G ) + ( − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ) 2 . A(H)=\frac{A(G)+(-DA(G)D)}{2}. 
 
 
 


 
 For the given even convex function F : ℝ → ℝ F:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R} , and for any real symmetric matrix M M , write 

 

 
 
 Φ F ​ ( M ) := tr ⁡ F ⁡ ( M ) = ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( M ) ) . \Phi_{F}(M):=\tr F(M)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}F(\lambda_{i}(M)). 
 
 
 
 Since F F is convex, the trace spectral functional Φ F ​ ( M ) \Phi_{F}(M) is convex on the space of real symmetric matrices. This is a standard consequence of the Davis theorem on convex invariant functions of Hermitian matrices and its real-symmetric corollary; see [ 9 ] . Therefore 

 

 
 
 Φ F ​ ( A ⁡ ( H ) ) = Φ F ​ ( A ⁡ ( G ) + ( − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ) 2 ) ≤ Φ F ​ ( A ⁡ ( G ) ) + Φ F ​ ( − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ) 2 . \Phi_{F}(A(H))=\Phi_{F}\left(\frac{A(G)+(-DA(G)D)}{2}\right)\leq\frac{\Phi_{F}(A(G))+\Phi_{F}(-DA(G)D)}{2}. 
 
 
 
 Since D D is orthogonal and D − 1 = D D^{-1}=D , the matrix − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D = D ⁡ ( − A ⁡ ( G ) ) ​ D -DA(G)D=D(-A(G))D is orthogonally similar to − A ⁡ ( G ) -A(G) . Thus 

 

 
 
 Φ F ​ ( − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ) = Φ F ​ ( − A ⁡ ( G ) ) . \Phi_{F}(-DA(G)D)=\Phi_{F}(-A(G)). 
 
 
 
 Since F F is even, F ⁡ ( − t ) = F ⁡ ( t ) F(-t)=F(t) for every t ∈ ℝ t\in\mathbb{R} , and hence 

 

 
 
 Φ F ​ ( − A ⁡ ( G ) ) = Φ F ​ ( A ⁡ ( G ) ) . \Phi_{F}(-A(G))=\Phi_{F}(A(G)). 
 
 
 
 Thus Φ F ​ ( A ⁡ ( H ) ) ≤ Φ F ​ ( A ⁡ ( G ) ) \Phi_{F}(A(H))\leq\Phi_{F}(A(G)) , which is exactly the stated inequality. Taking F ⁡ ( t ) = | t | p F(t)=|t|^{p} gives ℰ p ​ ( H ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(G) .
∎ 





 
 Consequently, any lower bound for an even convex spectral functional over connected bipartite graphs of order n n immediately extends to all connected graphs of order n n . Thus the rest of the proof of Theorem  1.3 may be carried out entirely in the connected bipartite setting.





 
 2.2. Matching generating polynomials


 
 We shall later compare trees by translating a spectral determinant into a matching generating polynomial. This subsection records the matching-polynomial facts needed for that comparison.



 
 Definition 2.2 (Weighted matching generating polynomial) . 

 
 Let G G be a finite graph whose edges carry nonnegative weights ω e \omega_{e} . The weighted matching generating polynomial of G G is

 

 
 
 M G ​ ( x ) = ∑ M ( ∏ e ∈ M ω e ) ​ x | M | , M_{G}(x)=\sum_{M}\left(\prod_{e\in M}\omega_{e}\right)x^{|M|}, 
 
 
 
 where the sum is over all matchings M M of G G .





 
 Remark 2.3 . 

 
 If G G is unweighted, we take ω e = 1 \omega_{e}=1 for every edge and recover the usual matching generating polynomial

 

 
 
 M G ​ ( x ) = ∑ j ≥ 0 m j ​ ( G ) ​ x j , M_{G}(x)=\sum_{j\geq 0}m_{j}(G)x^{j}, 
 
 
 
 where m j ​ ( G ) m_{j}(G) is the number of j j -matchings in G G .





 
 This is the standard monomer–dimer partition-function normalization; see Heilmann–Lieb  [ 11 ] . For general background on matching polynomials, see Godsil–Gutman  [ 10 ] . We call a ratio of the form M G − S ​ ( x ) / M G ​ ( x ) M_{G-S}(x)/M_{G}(x) , where S S is a deleted vertex set, a deletion ratio .



 
 The next theorem supplies the real-rootedness input for weighted graphs.



 
 Theorem 2.4 (Heilmann–Lieb zero theorem) . 

 
 Let G G be a finite graph with nonnegative edge weights ω e \omega_{e} , and let M G ​ ( x ) M_{G}(x) be the corresponding weighted matching generating polynomial. Then every zero of M G ​ ( x ) M_{G}(x) is real and negative. 





 
 Proof.

 
 Let 

 

 
 
 P ⁡ ( G , z ) := ∑ M ( ∏ e ∈ M ω e ) ​ z | V ⁡ ( G ) | − 2 ​ | M | , P(G;z):=\sum_{M}\left(\prod_{e\in M}\omega_{e}\right)z^{|V(G)|-2|M|}, 
 
 
 
 where the sum runs over all matchings M M of G G . This is exactly the weighted monomer–dimer partition polynomial considered by Heilmann and Lieb  [ 11 , Eq. (2.5a)] . For graphs with nonnegative edge weights, they proved that all zeros of P ⁡ ( G , z ) P(G;z) lie on the imaginary axis  [ 11 , Remark 4.1] . 



 
 By the definition of M G ​ ( x ) M_{G}(x) , for every z ≠ 0 z\neq 0 we have 

 

 
 
 P ⁡ ( G , z ) = ∑ M ( ∏ e ∈ M ω e ) ​ z | V ⁡ ( G ) | − 2 ​ | M | = z | V ⁡ ( G ) | ​ ∑ M ( ∏ e ∈ M ω e ) ​ ( z − 2 ) | M | = z | V ⁡ ( G ) | ​ M G ​ ( z − 2 ) . P(G;z)=\sum_{M}\left(\prod_{e\in M}\omega_{e}\right)z^{|V(G)|-2|M|}=z^{|V(G)|}\sum_{M}\left(\prod_{e\in M}\omega_{e}\right)(z^{-2})^{|M|}=z^{|V(G)|}M_{G}(z^{-2}). 
 
 
 
 Now let ξ \xi be a zero of M G ​ ( x ) M_{G}(x) . Since the empty matching contributes the constant term 1 1 , we have M G ​ ( 0 ) = 1 M_{G}(0)=1 , and hence ξ ≠ 0 \xi\neq 0 . Choose z ∈ ℂ ∖ { 0 } z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\{0\} such that z − 2 = ξ z^{-2}=\xi . Then 

 

 
 
 P ⁡ ( G , z ) = z | V ⁡ ( G ) | ​ M G ​ ( ξ ) = 0 . P(G;z)=z^{|V(G)|}M_{G}(\xi)=0. 
 
 
 
 Since every zero of P ⁡ ( G , z ) P(G;z) is purely imaginary, we know that z ∈ i ​ ℝ z\in i\mathbb{R} . Therefore 

 

 
 
 ξ = z − 2 ∈ ( − ∞ , 0 ) . \xi=z^{-2}\in(-\infty,0). 
 
 
 
 Thus every zero of M G ​ ( x ) M_{G}(x) is real and negative.
∎ 





 
 Example 2.5 (Paths) . 

 
 For the unweighted path P m P_{m} we write

 

 
 
 h m ​ ( x ) := M P m ​ ( x ) = ∑ j m j ​ ( P m ) ​ x j ( m ≥ 0 ) , h_{m}(x):=M_{P_{m}}(x)=\sum_{j}m_{j}(P_{m})x^{j}\qquad(m\geq 0), 
 
 
 
 and we use the convention h − 1 ​ ( x ) = 0 h_{-1}(x)=0 . Thus h 0 ​ ( x ) = h 1 ​ ( x ) = 1 h_{0}(x)=h_{1}(x)=1 . The matching recurrence for the last edge gives

 

 
 (2) 
 
 h m ​ ( x ) = h m − 1 ​ ( x ) + x ​ h m − 2 ​ ( x ) ( m ≥ 1 ) . h_{m}(x)=h_{m-1}(x)+xh_{m-2}(x)\qquad(m\geq 1). 
 
 
 
 More generally, concatenating two paths at an endpoint and conditioning on whether the connecting edge is used gives the path-concatenation identity 

 

 
 (3) 
 
 h a + b ​ ( x ) = h a ​ ( x ) ​ h b ​ ( x ) + x ​ h a − 1 ​ ( x ) ​ h b − 1 ​ ( x ) ( a , b ≥ 0 ) , h_{a+b}(x)=h_{a}(x)h_{b}(x)+xh_{a-1}(x)h_{b-1}(x)\qquad(a,b\geq 0), 
 
 
 
 again with the convention h − 1 ​ ( x ) = 0 h_{-1}(x)=0 .





 
 The next lemma is the bridge between weighted matching generating polynomials and the determinant identities used later.



 
 Lemma 2.6 . 

 
 Let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a weighted forest, and fix a bipartition V ⁡ ( ℱ ) = L ⊔ R V(\mathcal{F})=L\sqcup R .
Define the weighted biadjacency matrix B ℱ B_{\mathcal{F}} by 

 

 
 
 B ℱ = ( ( B ℱ ) u ​ v ) u ∈ L , v ∈ R , ( B ℱ ) u ​ v = { ω u ​ v , u ​ v ∈ E ⁡ ( ℱ ) , 0 , u ​ v ∉ E ⁡ ( ℱ ) . B_{\mathcal{F}}=\bigl((B_{\mathcal{F}})_{uv}\bigr)_{u\in L,\,v\in R},\qquad(B_{\mathcal{F}})_{uv}=\begin{cases}\sqrt{\omega_{uv}},&uv\in E(\mathcal{F}),\\
0,&uv\notin E(\mathcal{F}).\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 Then 

 

 
 (4) 
 
 det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ​ B ℱ ⊤ ) = det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ ) = M ℱ ​ ( x ) . \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top})=\det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}})=M_{\mathcal{F}}(x). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 By expanding det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ ) \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}}) into principal minors and then applying Cauchy–Binet, 

 

 
 
 det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ ) = ∑ J ⊆ R x | J | ​ det ( ( B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ ) J , J ) = ∑ I ⊆ L , J ⊆ R , | I | = | J | x | J | ​ det ( B ℱ ​ [ I , J ] ) 2 . \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}})=\sum_{J\subseteq R}x^{|J|}\det\bigl((B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}})_{J,J}\bigr)=\sum_{I\subseteq L,\;J\subseteq R,\;|I|=|J|}x^{|J|}\det(B_{\mathcal{F}}[I,J])^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Using det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ ) = det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ​ B ℱ ⊤ ) \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}})=\det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}) , we also obtain 

 

 
 
 det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ​ B ℱ ⊤ ) = ∑ I ⊆ L , J ⊆ R , | I | = | J | x | J | ​ det ( B ℱ ​ [ I , J ] ) 2 . \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top})=\sum_{I\subseteq L,\;J\subseteq R,\;|I|=|J|}x^{|J|}\det(B_{\mathcal{F}}[I,J])^{2}. 
 
 
 


 
 Now fix I ⊆ L I\subseteq L and J ⊆ R J\subseteq R with | I | = | J | |I|=|J| . Since ℱ \mathcal{F} is a forest,
the induced subgraph ℱ ⁡ [ I ∪ J ] \mathcal{F}[I\cup J] is again a forest, and hence it has at most one
perfect matching. If ℱ ⁡ [ I ∪ J ] \mathcal{F}[I\cup J] has no perfect matching, then det ( B ℱ ​ [ I , J ] ) = 0 \det(B_{\mathcal{F}}[I,J])=0 .
If ℱ ⁡ [ I ∪ J ] \mathcal{F}[I\cup J] has a perfect matching, then it is unique, and
 det ( B ℱ ​ [ I , J ] ) 2 \det(B_{\mathcal{F}}[I,J])^{2} is exactly the product of the weights of the edges in that matching.
Therefore the contribution of all pairs ( I , J ) (I,J) to the above sum is precisely the
weighted matching generating polynomial M ℱ ​ ( x ) M_{\mathcal{F}}(x) . This proves 

 

 
 
 det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ​ B ℱ ⊤ ) = det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ ) = M ℱ ​ ( x ) . ∎ \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top})=\det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}})=M_{\mathcal{F}}(x).\qed 
 
 
 






 
 2.3. Tree shifts


 
 With the matching-polynomial framework in place, we now introduce the tree shift that moves an arbitrary tree toward the path.



 
 The following tree-shift operation is the key to obtaining the main spectral results for trees. It is a one-branch special case of Csikvári’s generalized tree shift; see [ 8 ] . Figure  1 illustrates this operation.



 
 Figure 1. The one-branch tree shift used in Lemma  2.7 : delete the edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and reattach the pendant path w 1 ⋯ w b w_{1}\cdots w_{b} at u a u_{a} . 
 
 
 Lemma 2.7 . 

 
 Let T T be a tree containing a vertex v v with two pendant paths 

 

 
 
 v u 1 u 2 ⋯ u a , v w 1 w 2 ⋯ w b , vu_{1}u_{2}\cdots u_{a},\qquad vw_{1}w_{2}\cdots w_{b}, 
 
 
 
 where a , b ≥ 1 a,b\geq 1 . Let T ′ T^{\prime} be the tree obtained from T T by deleting the edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and adding the edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} . Then there is a polynomial Q ⁡ ( x ) Q(x) with nonnegative coefficients such that 

 

 
 
 M T ′ ​ ( x ) = M T ​ ( x ) + x 2 ​ Q ​ ( x ) . M_{T^{\prime}}(x)=M_{T}(x)+x^{2}Q(x). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Let C C be the subtree obtained from T T by deleting the vertices
 u 1 , … , u a , w 1 , … , w b u_{1},\ldots,u_{a},w_{1},\ldots,w_{b} ; thus v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) v\in V(C) . We shall use the path-concatenation ( 3 ). 



 
 Decomposing matchings according to whether the edge incident to v v on each
displayed path is used, we obtain 

 

 
 
 M T = h a ​ h b ​ M C + x ⁡ ( h a − 1 ​ h b + h a ​ h b − 1 ) ​ M C − v . M_{T}=h_{a}h_{b}M_{C}+x\bigl(h_{a-1}h_{b}+h_{a}h_{b-1}\bigr)M_{C-v}. 
 
 
 
 After the shift, the two pendant paths form a single path on a + b a+b vertices
attached to v v , so 

 

 
 
 M T ′ = h a + b ​ M C + x ​ h a + b − 1 ​ M C − v . M_{T^{\prime}}=h_{a+b}M_{C}+xh_{a+b-1}M_{C-v}. 
 
 
 
 Using the two identities 

 

 
 
 h a + b = h a ​ h b + x ​ h a − 1 ​ h b − 1 h_{a+b}=h_{a}h_{b}+xh_{a-1}h_{b-1} 
 
 
 
 and 

 

 
 
 h a + b − 1 = h a − 1 ​ h b + h a ​ h b − 1 − h a − 1 ​ h b − 1 , h_{a+b-1}=h_{a-1}h_{b}+h_{a}h_{b-1}-h_{a-1}h_{b-1}, 
 
 
 
 we get 

 

 
 
 M T ′ − M T = x ​ h a − 1 ​ h b − 1 ​ ( M C − M C − v ) . M_{T^{\prime}}-M_{T}=xh_{a-1}h_{b-1}\bigl(M_{C}-M_{C-v}\bigr). 
 
 
 
 Applying the matching recurrence at v v inside C C , 

 

 
 (5) 
 
 M C − M C − v = x ∑ z ∈ C : v ∼ z M C − v − z , M_{C}-M_{C-v}=x\sum_{z\in C:v\sim z}M_{C-v-z}, 
 
 
 
 where the sum is interpreted as 0 0 if v v is isolated in C C . Hence 

 

 
 
 M T ′ − M T = x 2 h a − 1 h b − 1 ∑ z ∈ C : v ∼ z M C − v − z . M_{T^{\prime}}-M_{T}=x^{2}h_{a-1}h_{b-1}\sum_{z\in C:v\sim z}M_{C-v-z}. 
 
 
 
 The right-hand side is a polynomial with nonnegative coefficients, which proves the claim.
∎ 





 
 Lemma 2.8 . 

 
 Every tree T T on n n vertices can be transformed into P n P_{n} by a
finite sequence of the tree shifts described in Lemma  2.7 . 





 
 Proof.

 
 We call a vertex branching if it has degree at least 3 3 . If a
tree has no branching vertex, then its maximum degree is at most 2 2 ,
and hence, being connected, it is a path. 



 
 Assume that the current tree is not a path. Root it at an arbitrary leaf,
and choose a branching vertex v v of maximal distance from the root. Let
 π ⁡ ( v ) \pi(v) be the parent of v v . Every component of T − v T-v not containing
 π ⁡ ( v ) \pi(v) contains no branching vertex; otherwise such a branching vertex
would be farther from the root than v v . Hence each such component is a
path, attached to v v at one of its endpoints. Since v v is branching
and has exactly one parent edge, there are at least two such pendant paths.
Choose two of them, 

 

 
 
 v u 1 ⋯ u a and v w 1 ⋯ w b . vu_{1}\cdots u_{a}\qquad\text{and}\qquad vw_{1}\cdots w_{b}. 
 
 
 


 
 Now delete the edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and add the edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} . This reconnects
the separated w w -path to the rest of the tree, and therefore produces
again a tree on the same vertex set. Under this operation the leaf
 u a u_{a} ceases to be a leaf, while w b w_{b} remains a leaf; the vertex
 v v still has degree at least 2 2 , since it remains adjacent to
 π ⁡ ( v ) \pi(v) and u 1 u_{1} . No other vertex changes its leaf status. Thus the
number of leaves decreases by exactly 1 1 . 



 
 Repeating the operation whenever the current tree is not a path must stop
after finitely many steps, because the number of leaves is a positive
integer and strictly decreases at each step. The terminal tree has no
branching vertex, and hence is a path. Since the number of vertices is
preserved throughout, the terminal tree is P n P_{n} .
∎ 







 
 2.4. Stieltjes transforms and cones


 
 For the second-order stop-loss part we use Stieltjes transforms of finite signed measures and, in particular, finite atomic measures, the cones 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} , and the auxiliary closure lemma (Lemma  2.11 ). The proof of that lemma uses one standard complete-Bernstein argument; see, for example, [ 20 , Chapter 7] .



 
 Definition 2.9 (Stieltjes transform) . 

 
 If τ \tau is a finite signed Borel measure on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , its
 Stieltjes transform is

 

 
 
 𝒮 τ ​ ( x ) := ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) 1 1 + x ​ λ ​ 𝑑 τ ​ ( λ ) ( x > 0 ) . \mathcal{S}_{\tau}(x):=\int_{[0,\infty)}\frac{1}{1+x\lambda}\,d\tau(\lambda)\qquad(x>0). 
 
 
 
 In the finite atomic case

 

 
 
 τ = m 0 ​ δ 0 + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ ​ δ s ℓ , m 0 , m ℓ ∈ ℝ , s ℓ > 0 , \tau=m_{0}\delta_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}m_{\ell}\delta_{s_{\ell}},\qquad m_{0},m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R},\ s_{\ell}>0, 
 
 
 
 where δ s \delta_{s} denotes the Dirac measure at s s , this becomes

 

 
 
 𝒮 τ ​ ( x ) = m 0 + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ 1 + s ℓ ​ x . \mathcal{S}_{\tau}(x)=m_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\frac{m_{\ell}}{1+s_{\ell}x}. 
 
 
 




 
 Definition 2.10 (Stieltjes cones) . 

 
 For k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , let 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} denote the convex cone generated by the functions

 

 
 
 ∏ j = 1 m ( 1 + s j ​ x ) − 1 , 0 ≤ m ≤ k , s j > 0 , \prod_{j=1}^{m}(1+s_{j}x)^{-1},\qquad 0\leq m\leq k,\qquad s_{j}>0, 
 
 
 
 where the parameters s j s_{j} are allowed to repeat. In other words, 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} consists of all finite nonnegative linear combinations of such functions. The case m = 0 m=0 is interpreted as the constant function 1 1 . We also put

 

 
 
 𝒞 ≤ k 0 := { g ∈ 𝒞 ≤ k : g ⁡ ( x ) = O ⁡ ( x − 1 ) ​  as  ​ x → ∞ } . \mathcal{C}_{\leq k}^{0}:=\{g\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq k}:g(x)=O(x^{-1})\text{ as }x\to\infty\}. 
 
 
 




 
 It is immediate from the definition that 𝒞 ≤ k ⋅ 𝒞 ≤ ℓ ⊆ 𝒞 ≤ k + ℓ \mathcal{C}_{\leq k}\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\leq\ell}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\leq k+\ell} . Moreover, 𝒞 ≤ k 0 \mathcal{C}_{\leq k}^{0} is the subcone of 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} consisting of functions that satisfy g ⁡ ( x ) = O ⁡ ( x − 1 ) g(x)=O(x^{-1}) as x → ∞ x\to\infty . This distinction is important: deletion ratios may have a nonzero limit at infinity, and therefore naturally lie in 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} , not necessarily in 𝒞 ≤ k 0 \mathcal{C}_{\leq k}^{0} .



 
 By definition, every element of 𝒞 ≤ 1 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} is the Stieltjes
transform of a finite atomic nonnegative measure on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , and
 𝒞 ≤ 1 0 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 1}^{0} consists exactly of those such functions with zero
constant term.



 
 We shall also use the following standard terminology. A nonnegative function h : ( 0 , ∞ ) → [ 0 , ∞ ) h:(0,\infty)\to[0,\infty) is called a Stieltjes function if it admits a representation

 

 
 
 h ⁡ ( x ) = a x + b + ∫ ( 0 , ∞ ) 1 x + t ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( t ) , a , b ≥ 0 , h(x)=\frac{a}{x}+b+\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{1}{x+t}\,d\mu(t),\qquad a,b\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 where μ \mu is a positive measure satisfying ∫ ( 0 , ∞ ) ( 1 + t ) − 1 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( t ) < ∞ \int_{(0,\infty)}(1+t)^{-1}\,d\mu(t)<\infty ; see [ 20 , Definition 2.1] . A function is called rational Stieltjes if it has the form

 

 
 
 f ⁡ ( x ) = a x + 𝒮 τ ​ ( x ) = a x + m 0 + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ 1 + s ℓ ​ x , a , m ℓ ≥ 0 , s ℓ > 0 , f(x)=\frac{a}{x}+\mathcal{S}_{\tau}(x)=\frac{a}{x}+m_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\frac{m_{\ell}}{1+s_{\ell}x},\qquad a,m_{\ell}\geq 0,\quad s_{\ell}>0, 
 
 
 
 for some finite atomic nonnegative measure τ = m 0 ​ δ 0 + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ ​ δ s ℓ \tau=m_{0}\delta_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}m_{\ell}\delta_{s_{\ell}} on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) . In particular, every element of 𝒞 ≤ 1 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} is a rational Stieltjes function with no pole at 0 0 .



 
 We now recall the standard notion of a complete Bernstein function . We shall use the following consequence of [ 20 , Definition 6.1 and Theorem 6.2(vi)] : if φ \varphi is a complete Bernstein function, then its restriction to ( 0 , ∞ ) (0,\infty) admits a representation

 

 
 
 φ ⁡ ( x ) = a + b ​ x + ∫ ( 0 , ∞ ) x x + t ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( t ) , a , b ≥ 0 , \varphi(x)=a+bx+\int_{(0,\infty)}\frac{x}{x+t}\,d\mu(t),\qquad a,b\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 where μ \mu is a positive measure satisfying ∫ ( 0 , ∞ ) ( 1 + t ) − 1 ​ 𝑑 μ ​ ( t ) < ∞ \int_{(0,\infty)}(1+t)^{-1}\,d\mu(t)<\infty .



 
 We shall use the following standard facts. If h h is a nonzero Stieltjes function, then 1 / h 1/h is a complete Bernstein function, and conversely the reciprocal of a nonzero complete Bernstein function is a Stieltjes function [ 20 , Theorem 7.3] . If h h is a Stieltjes function, then x ​ h ​ ( x ) xh(x) is a complete Bernstein function [ 20 , Theorem 6.2(ii)] . Finally, complete Bernstein functions are closed under addition, which is immediate from the integral representation in [ 20 , Theorem 6.2(vi)] .



 
 Lemma 2.11 . 

 
 If c ⁡ ( x ) , d ⁡ ( x ) ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 c(x),d(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} , then 

 

 
 
 c ⁡ ( x ) 1 + x ​ c ​ ( x ) ​ d ​ ( x ) ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 . \frac{c(x)}{1+xc(x)d(x)}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 If c ≡ 0 c\equiv 0 , then the assertion is immediate. Hence we may assume c ≢ 0 c\not\equiv 0 . Since c , d ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 c,d\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} , both c c and d d are rational Stieltjes functions with no pole at 0 0 . Moreover, c ⁡ ( x ) > 0 c(x)>0 for every x > 0 x>0 . 



 
 In particular, 1 / c 1/c and x ​ d xd are complete Bernstein functions, so 1 / c ⁡ ( x ) + x ​ d ​ ( x ) 1/c(x)+xd(x) is again a complete Bernstein function. Its reciprocal is therefore a Stieltjes function; since c c and d d are rational, this reciprocal is rational as well. Thus c ⁡ ( x ) 1 + x ​ c ​ ( x ) ​ d ​ ( x ) \frac{c(x)}{1+xc(x)d(x)} is a rational Stieltjes function. 



 
 It remains to check that this function lies in the cone 𝒞 ≤ 1 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} . Since c c and d d are regular at 0 0 , so is c ⁡ ( x ) 1 + x ​ c ​ ( x ) ​ d ​ ( x ) \frac{c(x)}{1+xc(x)d(x)} . Hence its a / x a/x -term vanishes, and therefore it is a nonnegative linear combination of the constant function 1 1 and ( 1 + s ​ x ) − 1 (1+sx)^{-1} . This completes the proof.
∎ 









 
 3. The R 1 R_{1} -comparison for 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 


 
 In this section we prove the path-minimality inequality throughout the interval 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 . The central device is the functional R 1 R_{1} . The Mellin representation below expresses the fractional power sums appearing in ℰ p \mathcal{E}_{p} as positive integrals of R 1 R_{1} . We then use a determinant identity, together with the bipartite spectral notation from Section  2.1 , the matching-polynomial preliminaries from Section  2.2 , and the tree-transform discussion in Section  2.3 , to prove the tree case. Finally, a deletion induction gives the pointwise R 1 R_{1} -comparison for all connected bipartite graphs.



 
 3.1. From R 1 R_{1} to p p -energy


 
 Recall from Section  2.1 that, for a bipartite graph G G , the squared singular values μ i ​ ( G ) \mu_{i}(G) satisfy ( 1 ). Thus a lower bound for ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G) against a path is equivalent to a lower bound for a fractional power sum of the squared singular values:

 

 
 
 ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) α ≥ ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) α , α = p 2 . \sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{\alpha}\geq\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{\alpha},\qquad\alpha=\frac{p}{2}. 
 
 
 
 The point of the resolvent functional below is to replace fractional powers by a positive integral of the single test function r 1 ​ ( x ​ t ) r_{1}(xt) .



 
 Define

 

 
 
 r 1 ​ ( y ) := ∫ 0 y s 1 + s ​ 𝑑 s = y − log ⁡ ( 1 + y ) , y ≥ 0 . r_{1}(y):=\int_{0}^{y}\frac{s}{1+s}\,ds=y-\log(1+y),\qquad y\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 For a bipartite graph G G and x > 0 x>0 , define

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) := ∑ i r 1 ​ ( x ​ μ i ​ ( G ) ) . R_{1}(G;x):=\sum_{i}r_{1}(x\mu_{i}(G)). 
 
 
 
 Equivalently, R 1 ​ ( G , x ) = tr ⁡ r 1 ​ ( x ​ B G ⊤ ​ B G ) R_{1}(G;x)=\tr r_{1}\bigl(xB_{G}^{\top}B_{G}\bigr) , where zero eigenvalues cause no contribution because r 1 ​ ( 0 ) = 0 r_{1}(0)=0 .



 
 By the disjoint-union observation in Section  2.1 , the functional R 1 R_{1} is additive over disjoint unions.



 
 The relevance to fractional powers in the range 1 < α < 2 1<\alpha<2 is the
following Mellin identity.



 
 Lemma 3.1 . 

 
 Let α ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha\in(1,2) . Then for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 (6) 
 
 t α = c α ​ ∫ 0 ∞ r 1 ​ ( x ​ t ) ​ x − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 x , t^{\alpha}=c_{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}r_{1}(xt)x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx, 
 
 
 
 where c α = α ​ sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( α − 1 ) ) / π > 0 c_{\alpha}=\alpha\sin\left(\pi(\alpha-1)\right)/\pi>0 . 





 
 Proof.

 
 The identity is trivial at t = 0 t=0 , so assume t > 0 t>0 . By the change of variables u = x ​ t u=xt , 

 

 
 
 ∫ 0 ∞ r 1 ​ ( x ​ t ) ​ x − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 x = t α ​ ∫ 0 ∞ r 1 ​ ( u ) ​ u − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 u . \int_{0}^{\infty}r_{1}(xt)x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx=t^{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}r_{1}(u)u^{-\alpha-1}\,du. 
 
 
 
 It remains to compute the constant. The boundary terms in the following integration by parts vanish. Indeed, as u → 0 + u\to 0^{+} , we have r 1 ​ ( u ) = u 2 2 + O ⁡ ( u 3 ) r_{1}(u)=\frac{u^{2}}{2}+O(u^{3}) , and hence 

 

 
 
 r 1 ​ ( u ) ​ u − α = O ⁡ ( u 2 − α ) → 0 ( α < 2 ) . r_{1}(u)u^{-\alpha}=O(u^{2-\alpha})\to 0\qquad(\alpha<2). 
 
 
 
 As u → ∞ u\to\infty , we have r 1 ​ ( u ) = O ​ ( u ) r_{1}(u)=O(u) , and hence 

 

 
 
 r 1 ​ ( u ) ​ u − α = O ⁡ ( u 1 − α ) → 0 ( α > 1 ) . r_{1}(u)u^{-\alpha}=O(u^{1-\alpha})\to 0\qquad(\alpha>1). 
 
 
 
 The same estimates also show that
 r 1 ​ ( u ) ​ u − α − 1 r_{1}(u)u^{-\alpha-1} is integrable at both endpoints. Therefore
integration by parts gives 

 

 
 
 ∫ 0 ∞ r 1 ​ ( u ) ​ u − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 u = 1 α ​ ∫ 0 ∞ u − α ​ r 1 ′ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u = 1 α ​ ∫ 0 ∞ u 1 − α 1 + u ​ 𝑑 u . \int_{0}^{\infty}r_{1}(u)u^{-\alpha-1}\,du=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}u^{-\alpha}r_{1}^{\prime}(u)\,du=\frac{1}{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{1-\alpha}}{1+u}\,du. 
 
 
 
 Put s = 2 − α ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) s=2-\alpha\in(0,1) . Then 

 

 
 
 ∫ 0 ∞ u 1 − α 1 + u ​ 𝑑 u = ∫ 0 ∞ u s − 1 1 + u ​ 𝑑 u = π sin ⁡ ( π ​ s ) = π sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( α − 1 ) ) . \int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{1-\alpha}}{1+u}\,du=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{u^{s-1}}{1+u}\,du=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi s)}=\frac{\pi}{\sin(\pi(\alpha-1))}. 
 
 
 
 Thus 

 

 
 
 ∫ 0 ∞ r 1 ​ ( x ​ t ) ​ x − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 x = t α ​ π α ​ sin ⁡ ( π ⁡ ( α − 1 ) ) , \int_{0}^{\infty}r_{1}(xt)x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx=t^{\alpha}\frac{\pi}{\alpha\sin(\pi(\alpha-1))}, 
 
 
 
 which proves the formula.
∎ 





 
 Theorem 3.2 . 

 
 Let G G and H H be bipartite graphs. Suppose 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( H , x ) for every  ​ x > 0 . R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(H;x)\qquad\text{for every }x>0. 
 
 
 
 Then for every p ∈ ( 2 , 4 ) p\in(2,4) , we have ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( H ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(H) . 





 
 Proof.

 
 Set α = p / 2 \alpha=p/2 , so α ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha\in(1,2) . Zero squared singular values may be included in the sums below, since both sides of ( 6 ) vanish when t = 0 t=0 . Since each graph is finite, there are only finitely many
squared singular values, and ( 6 ) may be summed over them. The resulting integrals are finite by the endpoint estimates in the proof of Lemma  3.1 .
Hence 

 

 
 
 1 2 ​ ℰ p ​ ( G ) = ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) α = c α ​ ∫ 0 ∞ R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ​ x − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 x . \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{E}_{p}(G)=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{\alpha}=c_{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}R_{1}(G;x)x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx. 
 
 
 
 The same identity holds for H H . The weight
 c α ​ x − α − 1 c_{\alpha}x^{-\alpha-1} is strictly positive for x > 0 x>0 . Therefore the
pointwise comparison 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( H , x ) ( x > 0 ) R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(H;x)\qquad(x>0) 
 
 
 
 implies ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( H ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(H) .
∎ 





 
 Thus, by Theorem  3.2 , in the range 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , it remains to prove the pointwise comparison

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) ( x > 0 ) R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x)\qquad(x>0) 
 
 
 
 for every connected bipartite graph G G on n n vertices.





 
 3.2. Determinant identity and the tree case


 
 Recall that, for a bipartite graph G G , we write Q G := B G ⊤ ​ B G Q_{G}:=B_{G}^{\top}B_{G} and R 1 ​ ( G , x ) = tr ⁡ r 1 ​ ( x ​ Q G ) R_{1}(G;x)=\tr r_{1}(xQ_{G}) for x > 0 x>0 . Since r 1 ​ ( y ) = y − log ⁡ ( 1 + y ) r_{1}(y)=y-\log(1+y) and tr ⁡ Q G = | E ⁡ ( G ) | \tr Q_{G}=|E(G)| , functional calculus gives the determinant identity

 

 
 (7) 
 
 R 1 ( G ; x ) = tr r 1 ( x Q G ) = x tr Q G − tr log ( I + x Q G ) = x | E ( G ) | − log det ( I + x Q G ) . R_{1}(G;x)=\tr r_{1}(xQ_{G})=x\tr Q_{G}-\tr\log(I+xQ_{G})=x|E(G)|-\log\det(I+xQ_{G}). 
 
 
 
 Here we used the standard identity tr log M = log det M \tr\log M=\log\det M for positive definite matrices M M , applied to M = I + x ​ Q G M=I+xQ_{G} . The same determinant is obtained from B G ​ B G ⊤ B_{G}B_{G}^{\top} , because B G ​ B G ⊤ B_{G}B_{G}^{\top} and B G ⊤ ​ B G B_{G}^{\top}B_{G} have the same nonzero eigenvalues. The functional R 1 R_{1} is additive over disjoint unions.



 
 Corollary 3.3 . 

 
 Let T T be a tree on n n vertices. Then, for every x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( T , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) . R_{1}(T;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Since | E ⁡ ( T ) | = | E ⁡ ( P n ) | = n − 1 |E(T)|=|E(P_{n})|=n-1 , Lemma  2.6 together with ( 7 ) gives 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( T , x ) − R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) = log ⁡ M P n ​ ( x ) − log ⁡ M T ​ ( x ) . R_{1}(T;x)-R_{1}(P_{n};x)=\log M_{P_{n}}(x)-\log M_{T}(x). 
 
 
 
 By Lemma  2.8 , there is a sequence of trees 

 

 
 
 T = T 0 , T 1 , … , T m = P n T=T_{0},T_{1},\ldots,T_{m}=P_{n} 
 
 
 
 such that each T j + 1 T_{j+1} is obtained from T j T_{j} by one operation of
Lemma  2.7 . Since Lemma  2.7 gives
 M T j + 1 ​ ( x ) − M T j ​ ( x ) = x 2 ​ Q j ​ ( x ) M_{T_{j+1}}(x)-M_{T_{j}}(x)=x^{2}Q_{j}(x) with nonnegative coefficients, we have
 M T j ​ ( x ) ≤ M T j + 1 ​ ( x ) M_{T_{j}}(x)\leq M_{T_{j+1}}(x) for every x ≥ 0 x\geq 0 . Iterating along the
sequence yields 

 

 
 
 M T ​ ( x ) ≤ M P n ​ ( x ) ( x ≥ 0 ) . M_{T}(x)\leq M_{P_{n}}(x)\qquad(x\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 Therefore log ⁡ M P n ​ ( x ) − log ⁡ M T ​ ( x ) ≥ 0 \log M_{P_{n}}(x)-\log M_{T}(x)\geq 0 , which completes the proof.
∎ 







 
 3.3. Rank-one gain and path-deficit estimates


 
 We first record a rank-one vertex-addition estimate for R 1 R_{1} . Equivalently, it gives a lower bound for the amount of R 1 R_{1} lost when a vertex of degree d d is deleted.



 
 Lemma 3.4 . 

 
 Let H H be a bipartite graph and let G G be obtained from H H by adding one
new vertex v v of degree d d . Then, for every x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( H , x ) ≥ r 1 ​ ( d ​ x ) . R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(H;x)\geq r_{1}(dx). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Assume v v is added on the left side of the bipartition. Let B H B_{H} be the biadjacency matrix for H H and let b b be the 0 0 - 1 1 column vector encoding the neighbors of v v on the right side. Put M = B H ⊤ ​ B H M=B_{H}^{\top}B_{H} . Then Q G = M + b ​ b ⊤ Q_{G}=M+bb^{\top} and b ⊤ ​ b = d b^{\top}b=d . By the matrix determinant lemma, 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( H , x ) \displaystyle R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(H;x) 
 = x ​ d − log ⁡ det ( I + x ⁡ ( M + b ​ b ⊤ ) ) det ( I + x ​ M ) \displaystyle=xd-\log\frac{\det(I+x(M+bb^{\top}))}{\det(I+xM)} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = x ​ d − log ⁡ ( 1 + x ​ b ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ​ b ) . \displaystyle=xd-\log\left(1+xb^{\top}(I+xM)^{-1}b\right). 
 
 
 
 Since M ⪰ 0 M\succeq 0 , we have ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ⪯ I (I+xM)^{-1}\preceq I . Hence 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ​ b ≤ b ⊤ ​ b = d . b^{\top}(I+xM)^{-1}b\leq b^{\top}b=d. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( H , x ) ≥ x ​ d − log ⁡ ( 1 + x ​ d ) = r 1 ​ ( d ​ x ) . R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(H;x)\geq xd-\log(1+xd)=r_{1}(dx). 
 
 
 
 The case in which v v is added on the right side is identical, using
 B G ​ B G ⊤ B_{G}B_{G}^{\top} instead of B G ⊤ ​ B G B_{G}^{\top}B_{G} .
∎ 





 
 The second estimate controls the path comparator that appears when a vertex
is deleted and several components remain. It is the matching upper bound for the preceding vertex-gain estimate.



 
 Lemma 3.5 . 

 
 Let q ≥ 1 q\geq 1 and let n 1 , … , n q ≥ 1 n_{1},\ldots,n_{q}\geq 1 . Put N = 1 + n 1 + ⋯ + n q N=1+n_{1}+\cdots+n_{q} . Then for every x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 (8) 
 
 R 1 ​ ( P N , x ) − ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) ≤ r 1 ​ ( ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) . R_{1}(P_{N};x)-\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x)\leq r_{1}((q+1)x). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Construct a tree T T as follows. Take the disjoint union
 P n 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P n q P_{n_{1}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{n_{q}} , choose one endpoint in each path, and
add a new vertex adjacent to all chosen endpoints. Then T T has N N vertices.
By Corollary  3.3 , we know that R 1 ​ ( P N , x ) ≤ R 1 ​ ( T , x ) R_{1}(P_{N};x)\leq R_{1}(T;x) . Thus it remains to estimate 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( T , x ) − ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) . R_{1}(T;x)-\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x). 
 
 
 
 Choose the bipartition of each path independently so that the selected endpoint lies on the right side, and place the new center on the left side. With this choice, adding the new center to the disjoint union of the q q paths is a rank-one update of degree q q . Let B i B_{i} be the biadjacency matrix of P n i P_{n_{i}} with the chosen endpoint on the right side, and let e i e_{i} be the standard basis vector corresponding to this endpoint. Put M i = B i ⊤ ​ B i M_{i}=B_{i}^{\top}B_{i} . For the disjoint union ℱ = P n 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ P n q \mathcal{F}=P_{n_{1}}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup P_{n_{q}} , the matrix B ℱ ⊤ ​ B ℱ B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}B_{\mathcal{F}} is block diagonal with blocks M 1 , … , M q M_{1},\ldots,M_{q} . Adding the new center contributes the rank-one vector b = ( e 1 , … , e q ) ⊤ b=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{q})^{\top} .
Thus 

 

 
 
 B T ⊤ ​ B T = M + b ​ b ⊤ , M = diag ⁡ ( M 1 , … , M q ) . B_{T}^{\top}B_{T}=M+bb^{\top},\qquad M=\operatorname{diag}(M_{1},\ldots,M_{q}). 
 
 
 
 Using ( 7 ) and | E ⁡ ( T ) | − | E ⁡ ( ℱ ) | = q |E(T)|-|E(\mathcal{F})|=q , we get 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( T , x ) − R 1 ​ ( ℱ , x ) = q ​ x − log ⁡ det ( I + x ⁡ ( M + b ​ b ⊤ ) ) det ( I + x ​ M ) . R_{1}(T;x)-R_{1}(\mathcal{F};x)=qx-\log\frac{\det(I+x(M+bb^{\top}))}{\det(I+xM)}. 
 
 
 
 By the matrix determinant lemma, 

 

 
 
 det ( I + x ⁡ ( M + b ​ b ⊤ ) ) det ( I + x ​ M ) = 1 + x ​ b ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ​ b . \frac{\det(I+x(M+bb^{\top}))}{\det(I+xM)}=1+xb^{\top}(I+xM)^{-1}b. 
 
 
 
 Since M M is block diagonal, b ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M ) − 1 ​ b = ∑ i = 1 q e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i b^{\top}(I+xM)^{-1}b=\sum_{i=1}^{q}e_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i} . Therefore 

 

 
 (9) 
 
 R 1 ​ ( T , x ) − ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) = q ​ x − log ⁡ ( 1 + x ​ ∑ i = 1 q e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i ) . R_{1}(T;x)-\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x)=qx-\log\left(1+x\sum_{i=1}^{q}e_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i}\right). 
 
 
 
 We claim that 

 

 
 (10) 
 
 e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i ≥ 1 1 + x . e_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i}\geq\frac{1}{1+x}. 
 
 
 
 Indeed, adding one new leaf to the chosen endpoint of P n i P_{n_{i}} produces
 P n i + 1 P_{n_{i}+1} . Applying the determinant lemma to this operation gives 

 

 
 
 h n i + 1 ​ ( x ) = det ( I + x ⁡ ( M i + e i ​ e i ⊤ ) ) = h n i ​ ( x ) ​ ( 1 + x ​ e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i ) . h_{n_{i}+1}(x)=\det(I+x(M_{i}+e_{i}e_{i}^{\top}))=h_{n_{i}}(x)(1+xe_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i}). 
 
 
 
 Using the path recurrence ( 2 ), we obtain 

 

 
 
 e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i = h n i − 1 ​ ( x ) h n i ​ ( x ) . e_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i}=\frac{h_{n_{i}-1}(x)}{h_{n_{i}}(x)}. 
 
 
 
 If n i = 1 n_{i}=1 , this gives e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i = h 0 / h 1 = 1 ≥ ( 1 + x ) − 1 e_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i}=h_{0}/h_{1}=1\geq(1+x)^{-1} . If
 n i ≥ 2 n_{i}\geq 2 , then 

 

 
 
 h n i ​ ( x ) = h n i − 1 ​ ( x ) + x ​ h n i − 2 ​ ( x ) ≤ ( 1 + x ) ​ h n i − 1 ​ ( x ) , h_{n_{i}}(x)=h_{n_{i}-1}(x)+xh_{n_{i}-2}(x)\leq(1+x)h_{n_{i}-1}(x), 
 
 
 
 because the path polynomials have non-negative coefficients and
 h n i − 2 ​ ( x ) ≤ h n i − 1 ​ ( x ) h_{n_{i}-2}(x)\leq h_{n_{i}-1}(x) for x > 0 x>0 . Hence
( 10 ) follows in all cases. 



 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 q e i ⊤ ​ ( I + x ​ M i ) − 1 ​ e i ≥ q 1 + x . \sum_{i=1}^{q}e_{i}^{\top}(I+xM_{i})^{-1}e_{i}\geq\frac{q}{1+x}. 
 
 
 
 Substituting this into ( 9 ), 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( T , x ) − ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) \displaystyle R_{1}(T;x)-\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x) 
 ≤ q ​ x − log ⁡ ( 1 + q ​ x 1 + x ) \displaystyle\leq qx-\log\left(1+\frac{qx}{1+x}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = q ​ x + log ⁡ ( 1 + x ) − log ⁡ ( 1 + ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) \displaystyle=qx+\log(1+x)-\log(1+(q+1)x) 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ ( q + 1 ) ​ x − log ⁡ ( 1 + ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) \displaystyle\leq(q+1)x-\log(1+(q+1)x) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = r 1 ​ ( ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) , \displaystyle=r_{1}((q+1)x), 
 
 
 
 where we used log ⁡ ( 1 + x ) ≤ x \log(1+x)\leq x . This proves
( 8 ).
∎ 







 
 3.4. The R 1 R_{1} -comparison for all connected bipartite graphs


 
 The preceding deletion estimates now yield the pointwise R 1 R_{1} -comparison needed for the range 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 .



 
 Theorem 3.6 . 

 
 Let G G be a connected bipartite graph on n n vertices. Then, for every
 x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 (11) 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) . R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 We argue by induction on n n . The cases n = 1 , 2 n=1,2 are immediate. 



 
 If G G is a tree, then ( 11 ) follows from Corollary  3.3 . 



 
 Assume now that G G is not a tree. Then G G contains a cycle. Choose a
vertex v v lying on a cycle. Let 

 

 
 
 d = d G ​ ( v ) , G − v = G 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ G q , n i = | V ⁡ ( G i ) | . d=d_{G}(v),\qquad G-v=G_{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup G_{q},\qquad n_{i}=|V(G_{i})|. 
 
 
 
 Since two neighbors of v v on the chosen cycle remain connected after v v is
removed, the number q q of components of G − v G-v is at most d − 1 d-1 : 

 

 
 
 q ≤ d − 1 . q\leq d-1. 
 
 
 


 
 By the induction hypothesis applied to each connected component G i G_{i} , 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G i , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) . R_{1}(G_{i};x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x). 
 
 
 
 Using additivity over disjoint unions, 

 

 
 (12) 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G − v , x ) = ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( G i , x ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) . R_{1}(G-v;x)=\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(G_{i};x)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x). 
 
 
 
 By Lemma  3.4 , adding v v back gives R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( G − v , x ) ≥ r 1 ​ ( d ​ x ) R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(G-v;x)\geq r_{1}(dx) . Since r 1 r_{1} is increasing and d ≥ q + 1 d\geq q+1 , 

 

 
 (13) 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( G − v , x ) ≥ r 1 ​ ( ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) . R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(G-v;x)\geq r_{1}((q+1)x). 
 
 
 
 On the other hand, Lemma  3.5 gives 

 

 
 (14) 
 
 R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) ≤ ∑ i = 1 q R 1 ​ ( P n i , x ) + r 1 ​ ( ( q + 1 ) ​ x ) , R_{1}(P_{n};x)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{q}R_{1}(P_{n_{i}};x)+r_{1}((q+1)x), 
 
 
 
 where n = 1 + ∑ i n i n=1+\sum_{i}n_{i} . Combining ( 12 ), ( 13 ), and ( 14 ), we obtain 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) . R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x). 
 
 
 
 This completes the induction.
∎ 









 
 4. The second-order stop-loss framework for p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 


 
 Section  3 handled the whole range 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 through the
kernel R 1 R_{1} . For the range p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , we prove a stronger statement:
not only power functions, but a whole cone of test functions can be compared.
This comparison is based on the second-order stop-loss quantities of the
squared singular values.



 
 For a bipartite graph G G define the atomic measure of its squared singular values by

 

 
 
 ν G := ∑ i δ μ i ​ ( G ) . \nu_{G}:=\sum_{i}\delta_{\mu_{i}(G)}. 
 
 
 
 The sum may be taken over the positive squared singular values only. Equivalently, one may include additional zero squared singular values. This convention makes no difference for any of the quantities 𝖲 t \mathsf{S}_{t} , since
 ( 0 − t ) + 2 = 0 (0-t)_{+}^{2}=0 for t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 .



 
 Definition 4.1 (Second-order stop-loss quantities) . 

 
 For t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , define the second-order stop-loss quantity of G G at level t t by

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) := ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + 2 ​ d ​ ν G ​ ( λ ) = ∑ i ( μ i ​ ( G ) − t ) + 2 . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G):=\int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}^{2}\,d\nu_{G}(\lambda)=\sum_{i}(\mu_{i}(G)-t)_{+}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 We say that G G dominates H H in the second-order stop-loss sense if

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 




 
 Definition 4.2 (Admissible third-order convex functions) . 

 
 A function F : [ 0 , ∞ ) → ℝ F:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R} is called
 admissible third-order convex if

 

 
 
 F ∈ C 2 ​ ( [ 0 , ∞ ) ) , F ′′ ∈ A ​ C loc ​ ( [ 0 , ∞ ) ) , F\in C^{2}([0,\infty)),\qquad F^{\prime\prime}\in AC_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty)), 
 
 
 
 where A ​ C loc ​ ( [ 0 , ∞ ) ) AC_{\mathrm{loc}}([0,\infty)) means absolute continuity on every compact subinterval of [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , and

 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 , F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 , F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 , F(0)=0,\qquad F^{\prime}(0)\geq 0,\qquad F^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 while the a.e. derivative F ′′′ F^{\prime\prime\prime} satisfies

 

 
 
 F ′′′ ​ ( t ) ≥ 0 for a.e.  ​ t > 0 . F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)\geq 0\qquad\text{for a.e. }t>0. 
 
 
 




 
 For every admissible third-order convex function F F , Taylor’s formula with
integral remainder gives

 

 
 
 F ⁡ ( λ ) = F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ​ λ + F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) 2 ​ λ 2 + 1 2 ​ ∫ 0 ∞ ( λ − t ) + 2 ​ F ′′′ ​ ( t ) ​ 𝑑 t ( λ ≥ 0 ) . F(\lambda)=F^{\prime}(0)\lambda+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}\lambda^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}(\lambda-t)_{+}^{2}F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)\,dt\qquad(\lambda\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 Indeed, the integral over [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) is the same as the integral over
 [ 0 , λ ] [0,\lambda] , since ( λ − t ) + = 0 (\lambda-t)_{+}=0 for t ≥ λ t\geq\lambda .



 
 The next lemma is the basic comparison principle that converts second-order stop-loss domination, together with the first-moment comparison, into domination for admissible third-order convex test functions.



 
 Lemma 4.3 . 

 
 Let G G and H H be bipartite graphs. Suppose that ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) ≥ ∑ i μ i ​ ( H ) \sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)\geq\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(H) and that 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(H) for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Then, for every admissible third-order convex F F , 

 

 
 
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( G ) ) ≥ ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( H ) ) . \sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(G))\geq\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(H)). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Applying the Taylor representation above to each squared singular value of G G and H H , and then subtracting, we obtain 

 

 
 
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( G ) ) − ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( H ) ) \displaystyle\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(G))-\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(H)) 
 = F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ​ ( ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) − ∑ i μ i ​ ( H ) ) \displaystyle=F^{\prime}(0)\left(\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(H)\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 + F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) 2 ​ ( 𝖲 0 ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 0 ​ ( H ) ) \displaystyle+\frac{F^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}\left(\mathsf{S}_{0}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{0}(H)\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 + 1 2 ∫ 0 ∞ ( 𝖲 t ( G ) − 𝖲 t ( H ) ) F ′ ′ ′ ( t ) d t . \displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)\right)F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)\,dt. 
 
 
 
 The first term is non-negative by the first-moment hypothesis and
 F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime}(0)\geq 0 . The second term is non-negative because the stop-loss
comparison at t = 0 t=0 gives 𝖲 0 ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 0 ​ ( H ) \mathsf{S}_{0}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{0}(H) , and
 F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq 0 . The integral term is non-negative because
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(H) for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 and
 F ′′′ ​ ( t ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}(t)\geq 0 almost everywhere. This completes the proof.
∎ 





 
 Remark 4.4 . 

 
 For p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 , the function F ⁡ ( λ ) = λ p / 2 F(\lambda)=\lambda^{p/2} is admissible third-order convex. Indeed, with α = p / 2 ≥ 2 \alpha=p/2\geq 2 , one has
 F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime}(0)\geq 0 , F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq 0 , and

 

 
 
 F ′′′ ​ ( λ ) = α ⁡ ( α − 1 ) ​ ( α − 2 ) ​ λ α − 3 ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}(\lambda)=\alpha(\alpha-1)(\alpha-2)\lambda^{\alpha-3}\geq 0 
 
 
 
 for λ > 0 \lambda>0 , with the case α = 2 \alpha=2 giving F ′′′ ≡ 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}\equiv 0 .





 
 The main bipartite theorem in the range p ≥ 4 p\geq 4 is the following.



 
 Theorem 4.5 . 

 
 Let G G be a connected bipartite graph on n n vertices. Then 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 




 
 Combining this stop-loss comparison with the first-moment inequality and the bipartite reduction gives the following comparison, valid for all connected graphs.



 
 Corollary 4.6 . 

 
 Let G G be a connected graph on n n vertices. Then, for every admissible third-order convex function F F , 

 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( G ) 2 ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( P n ) 2 ) . \sum_{i=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{i}(G)^{2}\right)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{i}(P_{n})^{2}\right). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 First suppose that G G is connected and bipartite. By Theorem  4.5 , 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}) for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . Moreover, 

 

 
 
 ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) = | E ⁡ ( G ) | ≥ n − 1 = ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) . \sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)=|E(G)|\geq n-1=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 
 Lemma  4.3 , applied with H = P n H=P_{n} , gives 

 

 
 
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( G ) ) ≥ ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( P n ) ) . \sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(G))\geq\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(P_{n})). 
 
 
 
 The adjacency eigenvalues of a bipartite graph are ± μ i \pm\sqrt{\mu_{i}} , together with possible additional zeros. Since F ⁡ ( 0 ) = 0 F(0)=0 , we have ∑ j = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( G ) 2 ) = 2 ​ ∑ i F ⁡ ( μ i ​ ( G ) ) \sum_{j=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{j}(G)^{2}\right)=2\sum_{i}F(\mu_{i}(G)) , and the same identity holds for P n P_{n} . Therefore 

 

 
 
 ∑ j = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( G ) 2 ) ≥ ∑ j = 1 n F ⁡ ( λ j ​ ( P n ) 2 ) . \sum_{j=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{j}(G)^{2}\right)\geq\sum_{j=1}^{n}F\left(\lambda_{j}(P_{n})^{2}\right). 
 
 
 


 
 Now let G G be an arbitrary connected graph. Since F F is admissible, F ′′′ ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime\prime}\geq 0 a.e., F ′′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime}(0)\geq 0 , and F ′ ​ ( 0 ) ≥ 0 F^{\prime}(0)\geq 0 . Hence F ′′ ≥ 0 F^{\prime\prime}\geq 0 and F ′ ≥ 0 F^{\prime}\geq 0 on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) . It follows that F ~ ​ ( x ) := F ​ ( x 2 ) \widetilde{F}(x):=F(x^{2}) is an even convex function on ℝ \mathbb{R} . By Lemma  2.1 , there exists a connected bipartite spanning subgraph H H of G G such that 

 

 
 
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( H ) 2 ) = ∑ i F ~ ​ ( λ i ​ ( H ) ) ≤ ∑ i F ~ ​ ( λ i ​ ( G ) ) = ∑ i F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( G ) 2 ) . \sum_{i}F\left(\lambda_{i}(H)^{2}\right)=\sum_{i}\widetilde{F}(\lambda_{i}(H))\leq\sum_{i}\widetilde{F}(\lambda_{i}(G))=\sum_{i}F\left(\lambda_{i}(G)^{2}\right). 
 
 
 
 Applying the bipartite case to H H gives 

 

 
 
 ∑ i F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( H ) 2 ) ≥ ∑ i F ⁡ ( λ i ​ ( P n ) 2 ) . \sum_{i}F\left(\lambda_{i}(H)^{2}\right)\geq\sum_{i}F\left(\lambda_{i}(P_{n})^{2}\right). 
 
 
 
 Combining the last two inequalities proves the claim.
∎ 





 
 The remainder of this section and the next two sections are devoted to the proof of Theorem  4.5 . The argument follows the same deletion-induction strategy as the R 1 R_{1} comparison, but with the analytic inputs replaced by second-order stop-loss estimates.



 
 4.1. Tree shifts at the second-order stop-loss level


 
 Recall from Section  2.4 the cones 𝒞 ≤ k \mathcal{C}_{\leq k} , their decaying subcones 𝒞 ≤ k 0 \mathcal{C}_{\leq k}^{0} , and the Stieltjes transform 𝒮 τ \mathcal{S}_{\tau} .
