The deletion ratios used below naturally lie in the cones 𝒞 ≤ 1 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} and 𝒞 ≤ 2 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 2} , while the final combined quantity will lie in the decaying cone 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0} . The following lemma is the analytic device that converts this cone membership into the needed stop-loss inequality.



 
 Lemma 4.7 . 

 
 Let g ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 g\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0} . Suppose that a finite signed atomic measure 

 

 
 
 σ = m 0 ​ δ 0 + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ ​ δ r ℓ , m 0 , m ℓ ∈ ℝ , r ℓ > 0 , \sigma=m_{0}\delta_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}m_{\ell}\delta_{r_{\ell}},\qquad m_{0},m_{\ell}\in\mathbb{R},\quad r_{\ell}>0, 
 
 
 
 satisfies 𝒮 σ ​ ( x ) = x ​ g ​ ( x ) \mathcal{S}_{\sigma}(x)=xg(x) for every x > 0 x>0 . Then, for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( λ ) ≤ 0 . \int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}\,d\sigma(\lambda)\leq 0. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Fix t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . For ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 , put 

 

 
 
 h ε , t ​ ( λ ) := ε ​ log ⁡ ( 1 + exp ⁡ ( ( λ − t ) / ε ) ) , λ ≥ 0 . h_{\varepsilon,t}(\lambda):=\varepsilon\log\bigl(1+\exp((\lambda-t)/\varepsilon)\bigr),\qquad\lambda\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 Then h ε , t h_{\varepsilon,t} is smooth, non-decreasing and convex on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , and h ε , t ​ ( λ ) ⟶ ( λ − t ) + h_{\varepsilon,t}(\lambda)\longrightarrow(\lambda-t)_{+} locally uniformly as ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 . We shall also use ϕ ε , t ​ ( u ) := u ​ h ε , t ​ ( u ) \phi_{\varepsilon,t}(u):=uh_{\varepsilon,t}(u) . Since 

 

 
 
 ϕ ε , t ′′ ​ ( u ) = 2 ​ h ε , t ′ ​ ( u ) + u ​ h ε , t ′′ ​ ( u ) ≥ 0 ( u ≥ 0 ) , \phi_{\varepsilon,t}^{\prime\prime}(u)=2h_{\varepsilon,t}^{\prime}(u)+uh_{\varepsilon,t}^{\prime\prime}(u)\geq 0\qquad(u\geq 0), 
 
 
 
 the function ϕ ε , t \phi_{\varepsilon,t} is convex on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) . 



 
 Since g ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 g\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0} , it can be written as a finite nonnegative linear combination of nonconstant generators: 

 

 
 
 g ⁡ ( x ) = ∑ ν = 1 M α ν ​ q ν ​ ( x ) , α ν ≥ 0 , g(x)=\sum_{\nu=1}^{M}\alpha_{\nu}q_{\nu}(x),\qquad\alpha_{\nu}\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 where 

 

 
 
 q ν ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 m ν 1 1 + s ν , j ​ x , s ν , j > 0 , 1 ≤ m ν ≤ 3 . q_{\nu}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{m_{\nu}}\frac{1}{1+s_{\nu,j}x},\qquad s_{\nu,j}>0,\quad 1\leq m_{\nu}\leq 3. 
 
 
 
 For a smooth function F F , the first divided difference [ a , b ] ​ F [a,b]F is the
slope of the secant line through ( a , F ⁡ ( a ) ) (a,F(a)) and ( b , F ⁡ ( b ) ) (b,F(b)) : 

 

 
 
 [ a , b ] ​ F := F ⁡ ( b ) − F ⁡ ( a ) b − a . [a,b]F:=\frac{F(b)-F(a)}{b-a}. 
 
 
 
 The second divided difference measures the change of these secant slopes: 

 

 
 
 [ a , b , c ] ​ F := [ b , c ] ​ F − [ a , b ] ​ F c − a . [a,b,c]F:=\frac{[b,c]F-[a,b]F}{c-a}. 
 
 
 
 When some nodes coincide, we use the standard confluent extension. Thus 

 

 
 
 [ a , a ] ​ F = F ′ ​ ( a ) , [ a , a , c ] ​ F = [ a , c ] ​ F − F ′ ​ ( a ) c − a , [ a , a , a ] ​ F = 1 2 ​ F ′′ ​ ( a ) . [a,a]F=F^{\prime}(a),\qquad[a,a,c]F=\frac{[a,c]F-F^{\prime}(a)}{c-a},\qquad[a,a,a]F=\frac{1}{2}F^{\prime\prime}(a). 
 
 
 
 For a generator 

 

 
 
 q ⁡ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 m 1 1 + s j ​ x , 1 ≤ m ≤ 3 , q(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{1+s_{j}x},\qquad 1\leq m\leq 3, 
 
 
 
 with the available parameters ordered as s 1 ≤ s 2 ≤ ⋯ ≤ s m s_{1}\leq s_{2}\leq\cdots\leq s_{m} , define a linear functional on smooth functions h h by 

 

 
 
 Λ q ​ ( h ) := { h ⁡ ( 0 ) − h ⁡ ( s 1 ) s 1 , m = 1 , − [ s 1 , s 2 ] ​ h , m = 2 , − [ s 1 , s 2 , s 3 ] ​ ( u ↦ u ​ h ​ ( u ) ) , m = 3 . \Lambda_{q}(h):=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{h(0)-h(s_{1})}{s_{1}},&m=1,\\[8.61108pt]
\displaystyle-[s_{1},s_{2}]h,&m=2,\\[5.16663pt]
\displaystyle-[s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}]\bigl(u\mapsto uh(u)\bigr),&m=3.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 This is well-defined also for repeated parameters by the confluent
interpretation above. 



 
 Let 

 

 
 
 k x ​ ( λ ) := 1 1 + x ​ λ , x > 0 . k_{x}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{1+x\lambda},\qquad x>0. 
 
 
 
 A direct divided-difference calculation gives 

 

 
 (15) 
 
 Λ q ​ ( k x ) = x ​ q ​ ( x ) ( x > 0 ) . \Lambda_{q}(k_{x})=xq(x)\qquad(x>0). 
 
 
 
 We next prove the sign of each generator on h ε , t h_{\varepsilon,t} . If
 m = 1 m=1 , then 

 

 
 
 Λ q ​ ( h ε , t ) = h ε , t ​ ( 0 ) − h ε , t ​ ( s 1 ) s 1 ≤ 0 \Lambda_{q}(h_{\varepsilon,t})=\frac{h_{\varepsilon,t}(0)-h_{\varepsilon,t}(s_{1})}{s_{1}}\leq 0 
 
 
 
 because h ε , t h_{\varepsilon,t} is non-decreasing. If m = 2 m=2 , then 

 

 
 
 Λ q ​ ( h ε , t ) = − [ s 1 , s 2 ] ​ h ε , t ≤ 0 , \Lambda_{q}(h_{\varepsilon,t})=-[s_{1},s_{2}]h_{\varepsilon,t}\leq 0, 
 
 
 
 again because first divided differences of a non-decreasing function are
nonnegative, including the confluent case. If m = 3 m=3 , then 

 

 
 
 Λ q ​ ( h ε , t ) = − [ s 1 , s 2 , s 3 ] ​ ϕ ε , t . \Lambda_{q}(h_{\varepsilon,t})=-[s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}]\phi_{\varepsilon,t}. 
 
 
 
 If s 1 < s 2 < s 3 s_{1}<s_{2}<s_{3} , then convexity of ϕ ε , t \phi_{\varepsilon,t} means that its
secant slopes are non-decreasing, so
 [ s 1 , s 2 ] ​ ϕ ε , t ≤ [ s 2 , s 3 ] ​ ϕ ε , t . [s_{1},s_{2}]\phi_{\varepsilon,t}\leq[s_{2},s_{3}]\phi_{\varepsilon,t}. Hence 

 

 
 
 [ s 1 , s 2 , s 3 ] ​ ϕ ε , t = [ s 2 , s 3 ] ​ ϕ ε , t − [ s 1 , s 2 ] ​ ϕ ε , t s 3 − s 1 ≥ 0 , [s_{1},s_{2},s_{3}]\phi_{\varepsilon,t}=\frac{[s_{2},s_{3}]\phi_{\varepsilon,t}-[s_{1},s_{2}]\phi_{\varepsilon,t}}{s_{3}-s_{1}}\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 and therefore Λ q ​ ( h ε , t ) ≤ 0 \Lambda_{q}(h_{\varepsilon,t})\leq 0 . For repeated nodes, the result follows from the same confluent interpretation of divided differences. 



 
 Finally, we define 

 

 
 
 Λ g ​ ( h ) := ∑ ν = 1 M α ν ​ Λ q ν ​ ( h ) . \Lambda_{g}(h):=\sum_{\nu=1}^{M}\alpha_{\nu}\Lambda_{q_{\nu}}(h). 
 
 
 
 Then 

 

 
 (16) 
 
 Λ g ​ ( h ε , t ) ≤ 0 \Lambda_{g}(h_{\varepsilon,t})\leq 0 
 
 
 
 and, by ( 15 ), 

 

 
 (17) 
 
 Λ g ​ ( k x ) = x ​ g ​ ( x ) ( x > 0 ) . \Lambda_{g}(k_{x})=xg(x)\qquad(x>0). 
 
 
 


 
 It remains to identify Λ g \Lambda_{g} with integration against the given
atomic measure σ \sigma . This is the only point where one has to be careful
about repeated parameters and higher-order poles. 



 
 Let A ⊂ ( 0 , ∞ ) A\subset(0,\infty) be the finite set of all s ν , j s_{\nu,j} among the generators q ν q_{\nu} . Since Λ g \Lambda_{g} is built from divided
differences of order at most two at these s ν , j s_{\nu,j} ’s, there exist real coefficients b 0 b_{0} and
 b a , j b_{a,j} , a ∈ A a\in A , 0 ≤ j ≤ 2 0\leq j\leq 2 , such that for every smooth h h , 

 

 
 
 Λ g ​ ( h ) = b 0 ​ h ​ ( 0 ) + ∑ a ∈ A ∑ j = 0 2 b a , j ​ h ( j ) ​ ( a ) . \Lambda_{g}(h)=b_{0}h(0)+\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{j=0}^{2}b_{a,j}h^{(j)}(a). 
 
 
 
 Applying this to k x k_{x} gives the rational function 

 

 
 (18) 
 
 Λ g ​ ( k x ) = b 0 + ∑ a ∈ A ∑ j = 0 2 b a , j ​ ( − 1 ) j ​ j ! ​ x j ( 1 + a ​ x ) j + 1 . \Lambda_{g}(k_{x})=b_{0}+\sum_{a\in A}\sum_{j=0}^{2}b_{a,j}\,\frac{(-1)^{j}j!x^{j}}{(1+ax)^{j+1}}. 
 
 
 
 After merging equal support points of σ \sigma , write 

 

 
 
 σ = m 0 ​ δ 0 + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ ​ δ r ℓ , r ℓ > 0 . \sigma=m_{0}\delta_{0}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}m_{\ell}\delta_{r_{\ell}},\qquad r_{\ell}>0. 
 
 
 
 Then

 By assumption and ( 17 ), the rational functions in
( 18 ) and ( 19 ) agree
for all x > 0 x>0 . Comparing their limits as x → ∞ x\to\infty gives b 0 = m 0 b_{0}=m_{0} .
Since the poles of 𝒮 σ \mathcal{S}_{\sigma} are simple, we also have
 b a , j = 0 b_{a,j}=0 for every j ≥ 1 j\geq 1 . 



 
 Comparing the remaining simple pole coefficients gives b r ℓ , 0 = m ℓ b_{r_{\ell},0}=m_{\ell} , and all other coefficients b a , 0 b_{a,0} for a ∈ A ∖ { r 1 , … , r N } a\in A\setminus\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{N}\} vanish. Thus we obtain for every smooth h h , 

 

 
 
 Λ g ​ ( h ) = m 0 ​ h ​ ( 0 ) + ∑ ℓ = 1 N m ℓ ​ h ​ ( r ℓ ) = ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) h ⁡ ( λ ) ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( λ ) . \Lambda_{g}(h)=m_{0}h(0)+\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}m_{\ell}h(r_{\ell})=\int_{[0,\infty)}h(\lambda)\,d\sigma(\lambda). 
 
 
 
 Applying this to h = h ε , t h=h_{\varepsilon,t} and using ( 16 ), we get 

 

 
 
 ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) h ε , t ​ ( λ ) ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( λ ) = Λ g ​ ( h ε , t ) ≤ 0 . \int_{[0,\infty)}h_{\varepsilon,t}(\lambda)\,d\sigma(\lambda)=\Lambda_{g}(h_{\varepsilon,t})\leq 0. 
 
 
 
 Finally let ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 . Since σ \sigma has finite support and h ε , t → ( λ − t ) + h_{\varepsilon,t}\to(\lambda-t)_{+} uniformly on finite sets, 

 

 
 
 ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( λ ) = lim ε ↓ 0 ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) h ε , t ​ ( λ ) ​ 𝑑 σ ​ ( λ ) ≤ 0 . \int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}\,d\sigma(\lambda)=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\int_{[0,\infty)}h_{\varepsilon,t}(\lambda)\,d\sigma(\lambda)\leq 0. 
 
 
 
 This proves the lemma.
∎ 





 
 The following lemma gives the cone membership for deletion ratios of weighted forests. In the applications below we shall only need the cases | U | = 1 |U|=1 and | U | = 2 |U|=2 , but the same argument proves the general statement.



 
 Lemma 4.8 . 

 
 Let Y Y be a weighted forest with non-negative edge weights, and let
 U U be a set of vertices contained in one side of a bipartition of
 Y Y . Then 

 

 
 
 M Y − U ​ ( x ) M Y ​ ( x ) ∈ 𝒞 ≤ | U | . \frac{M_{Y-U}(x)}{M_{Y}(x)}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq|U|}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 It suffices to consider the case where U U is contained in the left side of the bipartition; the case where U U is contained in the right side is obtained by replacing B B with B ⊤ B^{\top} . Let L L be the left vertex set, and let B = B Y B=B_{Y} be the weighted biadjacency matrix from Lemma  2.6 . Set
 A := B ​ B ⊤ ⪰ 0 . A:=BB^{\top}\succeq 0. 
By ( 4 ), 

 

 
 
 M Y ​ ( x ) = det ( I + x ​ A ) . M_{Y}(x)=\det(I+xA). 
 
 
 
 Write U c := L ∖ U U^{c}:=L\setminus U . Deleting the vertices in U U corresponds to deleting the corresponding rows of B B . Thus the weighted biadjacency matrix of Y − U Y-U , with respect to the bipartition U c ⊔ R U^{c}\sqcup R , is B ⁡ [ U c , R ] B[U^{c},R] . Hence 

 

 
 
 M Y − U ​ ( x ) = det ( I + x ​ B ​ [ U c , R ] ​ B ​ [ U c , R ] ⊤ ) = det ( ( I + x ​ A ) U c , U c ) . M_{Y-U}(x)=\det\bigl(I+xB[U^{c},R]B[U^{c},R]^{\top}\bigr)=\det\bigl((I+xA)_{U^{c},U^{c}}\bigr). 
 
 
 
 Applying the principal-minor case of Jacobi’s identity for complementary minors [ 5 , Eq. (12)] to M = I + x ​ A M=I+xA , we obtain, for x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 
 M Y − U ​ ( x ) M Y ​ ( x ) = det ( ( I + x ​ A ) U c , U c ) det ( I + x ​ A ) = det ( [ ( I + x ​ A ) − 1 ] U , U ) . \frac{M_{Y-U}(x)}{M_{Y}(x)}=\frac{\det((I+xA)_{U^{c},U^{c}})}{\det(I+xA)}=\det\bigl([(I+xA)^{-1}]_{U,U}\bigr). 
 
 
 
 It remains to expand this determinant spectrally. Let 

 

 
 
 A = ∑ α = 1 | L | s α ​ q α ​ q α ⊤ , s α ≥ 0 , A=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{|L|}s_{\alpha}q_{\alpha}q_{\alpha}^{\top},\qquad s_{\alpha}\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 be an orthonormal spectral decomposition of A A . Set T x := ( I + x ​ A ) − 1 T_{x}:=(I+xA)^{-1} , so T x ​ q α = 1 1 + s α ​ x ​ q α T_{x}q_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{1+s_{\alpha}x}q_{\alpha} . 



 
 If U = { u 1 , … , u r } U=\{u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}\} , write 

 

 
 
 e U := e u 1 ∧ ⋯ ∧ e u r . e_{U}:=e_{u_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge e_{u_{r}}. 
 
 
 
 Then the principal minor has the exterior-power interpretation 

 

 
 
 det ( [ T x ] U , U ) = ⟨ e U , ∧ r T x ( e U ) ⟩ . \det([T_{x}]_{U,U})=\langle e_{U},\wedge^{r}T_{x}(e_{U})\rangle. 
 
 
 
 Since the vectors 

 

 
 
 q α 1 ∧ ⋯ ∧ q α r , α 1 < ⋯ < α r , q_{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge q_{\alpha_{r}},\qquad\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{r}, 
 
 
 
 form an orthonormal eigenbasis for ∧ r T x \wedge^{r}T_{x} , with eigenvalues
 ∏ ℓ = 1 r 1 1 + s α ℓ ​ x , \prod_{\ell=1}^{r}\frac{1}{1+s_{\alpha_{\ell}}x}, 
we get 

 

 
 
 det ( [ T x ] U , U ) \displaystyle\det([T_{x}]_{U,U}) 
 = ∑ α 1 < ⋯ < α r ⟨ e U , q α 1 ∧ ⋯ ∧ q α r ⟩ 2 ∏ ℓ = 1 r ( 1 + s α ℓ ​ x ) . \displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{r}}\frac{\bigl\langle e_{U},\,q_{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge q_{\alpha_{r}}\bigr\rangle^{2}}{\prod_{\ell=1}^{r}(1+s_{\alpha_{\ell}}x)}. 
 
 
 
 All coefficients are squares, hence non-negative. Therefore
 M Y − U ​ ( x ) M Y ​ ( x ) \frac{M_{Y-U}(x)}{M_{Y}(x)} 
is a non-negative linear combination of products of at most | U | |U| 
first-order resolvent factors. Thus it lies in 𝒞 ≤ | U | \mathcal{C}_{\leq|U|} .
∎ 





 
 We shall need to differentiate real-rooted polynomial families through their root parameters. The needed root regularity is supplied by Bronshtein’s theorem on hyperbolic polynomials; we record the short consequence needed below. Here and below, locally Lipschitz means Lipschitz on every compact subinterval.



 
 Lemma 4.9 . 

 
 Let I ⊂ ℝ I\subset\mathbb{R} be an open interval and let 

 

 
 
 P θ ​ ( x ) = ∑ k = 0 D a k ​ ( θ ) ​ x k , a 0 ​ ( θ ) ≡ 1 , P_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{k=0}^{D}a_{k}(\theta)x^{k},\qquad a_{0}(\theta)\equiv 1, 
 
 
 
 be a family of polynomials of degree at most D D , where each coefficient function a k : I → ℝ a_{k}:I\to\mathbb{R} is of class C D C^{D} . Assume that, for every θ ∈ I \theta\in I , all zeros of P θ P_{\theta} are real and non-positive. Then one can choose locally Lipschitz functions μ 1 , … , μ D : I → [ 0 , ∞ ) \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{D}:I\to[0,\infty) such that 

 

 
 
 P θ ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) ) . P_{\theta}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}\left(1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)\right). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, there is a set E ⊂ I E\subset I such that I ∖ E I\setminus E has Lebesgue measure zero and, for every θ ∈ E \theta\in E , all derivatives μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) \mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta) exist and, for every x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 
 ∂ θ P θ ​ ( x ) P θ ​ ( x ) = ∑ j = 1 D x ​ μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) . \frac{\partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x)}{P_{\theta}(x)}=\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{x\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)}{1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Define the reversed polynomial 

 

 
 
 P ~ θ ( y ) := y D P θ ( − 1 / y ) . \widetilde{P}_{\theta}(y):=y^{D}P_{\theta}(-1/y). 
 
 
 
 Since a 0 ​ ( θ ) ≡ 1 a_{0}(\theta)\equiv 1 , this is a monic polynomial of degree D D whose
coefficients are C D C^{D} functions of θ \theta . Its roots are real and
non-negative; possible drops in the degree of P θ P_{\theta} contribute additional
zero roots of P ~ θ \widetilde{P}_{\theta} . 



 
 Recall that a univariate polynomial is called hyperbolic if all its roots are real. Here C m , 1 C^{m,1} means C m C^{m} with Lipschitz m m -th derivative. Since the coefficients of P ~ θ \widetilde{P}_{\theta} are C D C^{D} , they are C D − 1 , 1 C^{D-1,1} on every compact subinterval of I I . By Bronshtein’s theorem for hyperbolic polynomials  [ 4 ] , in the form stated in [ 19 , Theorem 2.1] , the roots of P ~ θ \widetilde{P}_{\theta} can be labeled by locally Lipschitz functions μ 1 , … , μ D : I → [ 0 , ∞ ) \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{D}:I\to[0,\infty) . Thus P ~ θ ​ ( y ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( y − μ j ​ ( θ ) ) \widetilde{P}_{\theta}(y)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}(y-\mu_{j}(\theta)) . Substituting y = − 1 / x y=-1/x gives 

 

 
 
 P θ ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) ) . P_{\theta}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}(1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)). 
 
 
 


 
 By Rademacher’s theorem, each locally Lipschitz function μ j \mu_{j} is differentiable almost everywhere on I I . Since there are only finitely many functions μ 1 , … , μ D \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{D} , there exists a set E ⊂ I E\subset I such that I ∖ E I\setminus E has Lebesgue measure zero and all derivatives μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) \mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta) exist for every θ ∈ E \theta\in E . For θ ∈ E \theta\in E , differentiating the displayed product identity with respect to θ \theta gives 

 

 
 
 ∂ θ P θ ​ ( x ) = ∑ j = 1 D x ​ μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) ​ ∏ ℓ ≠ j ( 1 + x ​ μ ℓ ​ ( θ ) ) . \partial_{\theta}P_{\theta}(x)=\sum_{j=1}^{D}x\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)\prod_{\ell\neq j}(1+x\mu_{\ell}(\theta)). 
 
 
 
 Since x > 0 x>0 and μ j ​ ( θ ) ≥ 0 \mu_{j}(\theta)\geq 0 , all factors are positive, and division by P θ ​ ( x ) P_{\theta}(x) gives the claimed logarithmic derivative identity.
∎ 





 
 Corollary 4.10 . 

 
 With the notation of Lemma  4.9 , fix t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 and define 

 

 
 
 F t ​ ( θ ) := ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( θ ) − t ) + 2 . F_{t}(\theta):=\sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(\theta)-t)_{+}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Then F t F_{t} is locally Lipschitz on I I , and for almost every
 θ ∈ I \theta\in I , 

 

 
 
 F t ′ ​ ( θ ) = 2 ​ ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( θ ) − t ) + ​ μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) . F_{t}^{\prime}(\theta)=2\sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(\theta)-t)_{+}\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 The function ϕ t ​ ( λ ) := ( λ − t ) + 2 \phi_{t}(\lambda):=(\lambda-t)_{+}^{2} is C 1 C^{1} , with ϕ t ′ ​ ( λ ) = 2 ​ ( λ − t ) + \phi_{t}^{\prime}(\lambda)=2(\lambda-t)_{+} . Since each μ j \mu_{j} is locally Lipschitz, so is ϕ t ∘ μ j \phi_{t}\circ\mu_{j} . The chain rule for Lipschitz functions gives 

 

 
 
 d d ​ θ ​ ϕ t ​ ( μ j ​ ( θ ) ) = 2 ​ ( μ j ​ ( θ ) − t ) + ​ μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) \frac{d}{d\theta}\phi_{t}(\mu_{j}(\theta))=2(\mu_{j}(\theta)-t)_{+}\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta) 
 
 
 
 for almost every θ \theta . Summing over j j gives the result.
∎ 





 
 We now prove that the one-branch tree shift is monotone for the
second-order stop-loss quantities.



 
 Theorem 4.11 . 

 
 Let T T contain two pendant paths 

 

 
 
 v u 1 ⋯ u a , v w 1 ⋯ w b , a , b ≥ 1 , vu_{1}\cdots u_{a},\qquad vw_{1}\cdots w_{b},\qquad a,b\geq 1, 
 
 
 
 and let T ′ T^{\prime} be obtained from T T by deleting v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and adding
 u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} , exactly as in Lemma  2.7 . Then 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( T ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( T ′ ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(T)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(T^{\prime})\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 By Lemma  2.6 , for a forest ℱ \mathcal{F} , we can identify the matching polynomial M ℱ M_{\mathcal{F}} with the determinant det ( I + x ​ B ℱ ​ B ℱ ⊤ ) \det(I+xB_{\mathcal{F}}B_{\mathcal{F}}^{\top}) , where B ℱ B_{\mathcal{F}} is the weighted biadjacency matrix of ℱ \mathcal{F} . Hence for trees T T and T ′ T^{\prime} , if we can factorize M T ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( 0 ) ) M_{T}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}(1+x\mu_{j}(0)) and M T ′ ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( 1 ) ) M_{T^{\prime}}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}(1+x\mu_{j}(1)) , for some D D , possibly padded with zero μ j \mu_{j} ’s, then 𝖲 t ​ ( T ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( T ′ ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(T)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(T^{\prime}) is equivalent to ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( 0 ) − t ) + 2 ≥ ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( 1 ) − t ) + 2 \sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(0)-t)_{+}^{2}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(1)-t)_{+}^{2} for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . We will construct a family of polynomials M θ M_{\theta} interpolating between M T M_{T} and M T ′ M_{T^{\prime}} , and apply Lemma  4.9 to obtain the desired stop-loss inequality. 



 
 Use the notation from Lemma  2.7 . Let C C be the subtree obtained
from T T by deleting the vertices u 1 , … , u a , w 1 , … , w b u_{1},\ldots,u_{a},w_{1},\ldots,w_{b} , and put 

 

 
 
 R := M C − v , W := M C − M C − v x . R:=M_{C-v},\qquad W:=\frac{M_{C}-M_{C-v}}{x}. 
 
 
 
 If C 1 , … , C s C_{1},\ldots,C_{s} are the connected components of C − v C-v and
 r i ∈ C i r_{i}\in C_{i} is adjacent to v v , then by ( 5 ) in the proof of Lemma  2.7 , together with multiplicativity of matching polynomials over disjoint unions, 

 

 
 
 W = M C − M C − v x = ∑ i = 1 s M C − v − r i = ∑ i = 1 s M C i − r i ​ ∏ ℓ ≠ i M C ℓ . W=\frac{M_{C}-M_{C-v}}{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}M_{C-v-r_{i}}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}M_{C_{i}-r_{i}}\prod_{\ell\neq i}M_{C_{\ell}}. 
 
 
 
 Hence, we know that 

 

 
 
 c := W R = ∑ i = 1 s M C i − r i M C i , c:=\frac{W}{R}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}\frac{M_{C_{i}-r_{i}}}{M_{C_{i}}}, 
 
 
 
 with the right-hand side interpreted as 0 0 when s = 0 s=0 . Each summand is a one-vertex deletion ratio. Hence, by Lemma  4.8 and the convexity of the cone 𝒞 ≤ 1 \mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} , we have c ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 c\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1} . 



 
 Let h m := M P m h_{m}:=M_{P_{m}} , with h 0 = 1 h_{0}=1 , and set 

 

 
 
 H := h a + b + 1 , D 0 := h a ​ h b , D 1 := h a + b , E := h a − 1 ​ h b − 1 . H:=h_{a+b+1},\qquad D_{0}:=h_{a}h_{b},\qquad D_{1}:=h_{a+b},\qquad E:=h_{a-1}h_{b-1}. 
 
 
 
 The path-concatenation identity ( 3 ) gives D 1 = D 0 + x ​ E D_{1}=D_{0}+xE . For 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 , put 

 

 
 
 D θ := ( 1 − θ ) ​ D 0 + θ ​ D 1 = D 0 + θ ​ x ​ E . D_{\theta}:=(1-\theta)D_{0}+\theta D_{1}=D_{0}+\theta xE. 
 
 
 
 Label the vertices of P a + b + 1 P_{a+b+1} as z 0 , z 1 , … , z a + b z_{0},z_{1},\ldots,z_{a+b} . Deleting z a z_{a} leaves P a ⊔ P b P_{a}\sqcup P_{b} , while deleting the endpoint z 0 z_{0} leaves P a + b P_{a+b} . Hence 

 

 
 
 D 0 H = h a ​ h b h a + b + 1 , D 1 H = h a + b h a + b + 1 \frac{D_{0}}{H}=\frac{h_{a}h_{b}}{h_{a+b+1}},\qquad\frac{D_{1}}{H}=\frac{h_{a+b}}{h_{a+b+1}} 
 
 
 
 are one-vertex deletion ratios in P a + b + 1 P_{a+b+1} . Therefore again by Lemma  4.8 , 

 

 
 
 d θ := D θ H = ( 1 − θ ) ​ D 0 H + θ ​ D 1 H ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 . d_{\theta}:=\frac{D_{\theta}}{H}=(1-\theta)\frac{D_{0}}{H}+\theta\frac{D_{1}}{H}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1}. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, deleting z a − 1 z_{a-1} and z a + 1 z_{a+1} leaves P a − 1 ⊔ P 1 ⊔ P b − 1 P_{a-1}\sqcup P_{1}\sqcup P_{b-1} . The vertices z a − 1 z_{a-1} and z a + 1 z_{a+1} have the same parity, hence lie on the same side of the bipartition. Since h 1 = 1 h_{1}=1 , by Lemma  4.8 , the two-vertex same-side deletion ratio is 

 

 
 
 e := E H = h a − 1 ​ h b − 1 h a + b + 1 ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 2 . e:=\frac{E}{H}=\frac{h_{a-1}h_{b-1}}{h_{a+b+1}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}. 
 
 
 
 We now define the interpolation, as illustrated in Figure  2 . 



 
 Figure 2. The interpolation from T T to T ′ T^{\prime} : the old edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} carries weight 1 − θ 1-\theta , the new edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} carries weight θ \theta , and all other edges have weight 1 1 . 
 
 
 Let G θ G_{\theta} be the non-negatively weighted graph obtained by assigning weight 1 − θ 1-\theta to the old edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} , weight θ \theta to the new edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} , and weight 1 1 to all other edges. Let 

 

 
 
 M θ := M G θ . M_{\theta}:=M_{G_{\theta}}. 
 
 
 
 Thus M 0 = M T M_{0}=M_{T} and M 1 = M T ′ M_{1}=M_{T^{\prime}} . 



 
 Let H θ H_{\theta} denote the weighted branch on the vertices 

 

 
 
 v , u 1 , … , u a , w 1 , … , w b v,u_{1},\ldots,u_{a},w_{1},\ldots,w_{b} 
 
 
 
 with the same edge weights as above. The two special edges v ​ w 1 vw_{1} and
 u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} share the vertex w 1 w_{1} , so no matching can use both. Matchings using v ​ w 1 vw_{1} contribute
 ( 1 − θ ) ​ x ​ h a ​ h b − 1 , (1-\theta)xh_{a}h_{b-1}, 
whereas matchings using u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} contribute
 θ ​ x ​ h a ​ h b − 1 . \theta xh_{a}h_{b-1}. 
All matchings using neither special edge are independent of θ \theta . Therefore 

 

 
 
 M H θ = M H 0 = h a + b + 1 = H . M_{H_{\theta}}=M_{H_{0}}=h_{a+b+1}=H. 
 
 
 
 On the other hand, after deleting v v , the old edge v ​ w 1 vw_{1} disappears, while the new edge u a ​ w 1 u_{a}w_{1} remains with weight θ \theta . Hence, 

 

 
 
 M H θ − v = h a ​ h b + θ ​ x ​ h a − 1 ​ h b − 1 = D θ . M_{H_{\theta}-v}=h_{a}h_{b}+\theta xh_{a-1}h_{b-1}=D_{\theta}. 
 
 
 
 Conditioning on whether v v is matched to an edge lying in C C , we get 

 

 
 
 M θ = R ​ M H θ + x ​ W ​ M H θ − v = R ​ H + x ​ W ​ D θ . M_{\theta}=R\,M_{H_{\theta}}+xW\,M_{H_{\theta}-v}=RH+xWD_{\theta}. 
 
 
 
 Consequently, ∂ θ M θ = x 2 ​ W ​ E \partial_{\theta}M_{\theta}=x^{2}WE . 



 
 Set Q := W ​ E Q:=WE . We next prove that 

 

 
 
 Q M θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 . \frac{Q}{M_{\theta}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0}. 
 
 
 
 Indeed, we have 

 

 
 
 Q M θ = W ​ E R ​ H + x ​ W ​ D θ = ( W / R ) ​ ( E / H ) 1 + x ⁡ ( W / R ) ​ ( D θ / H ) = e ​ c 1 + x ​ c ​ d θ . \frac{Q}{M_{\theta}}=\frac{WE}{RH+xWD_{\theta}}=\frac{(W/R)(E/H)}{1+x(W/R)(D_{\theta}/H)}=e\,\frac{c}{1+xcd_{\theta}}. 
 
 
 
 We already proved 

 

 
 
 c ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 , d θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 , e ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 2 . c\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1},\qquad d_{\theta}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1},\qquad e\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}. 
 
 
 
 By Lemma  2.11 , 

 

 
 
 c 1 + x ​ c ​ d θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 1 . \frac{c}{1+xcd_{\theta}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore, using
 𝒞 ≤ 2 ⋅ 𝒞 ≤ 1 ⊆ 𝒞 ≤ 3 , \mathcal{C}_{\leq 2}\cdot\mathcal{C}_{\leq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}, 
we obtain Q / M θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 Q/M_{\theta}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3} . It remains to check the decay at infinity. If Q = 0 Q=0 , then Q / M θ = 0 ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 Q/M_{\theta}=0\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0} , and there is nothing to prove. Assume Q ≠ 0 Q\neq 0 . Then W ≠ 0 W\neq 0 , and 

 

 
 
 M θ = R ​ H + x ​ W ​ ( D 0 + θ ​ x ​ E ) , Q = W ​ E . M_{\theta}=RH+xW(D_{0}+\theta xE),\qquad Q=WE. 
 
 
 
 Since
 deg ⁡ D 0 ≥ deg ⁡ E , \deg D_{0}\geq\deg E, 
the summand x ​ W ​ D 0 xWD_{0} has degree at least
 1 + deg ⁡ W + deg ⁡ E = 1 + deg ⁡ Q . 1+\deg W+\deg E=1+\deg Q. 
All coefficients are non-negative, so no leading-term cancellation is
possible in M θ M_{\theta} . Hence
 deg ⁡ M θ ≥ deg ⁡ Q + 1 , \deg M_{\theta}\geq\deg Q+1, 
and therefore 

 

 
 
 Q M θ = O ⁡ ( x − 1 ) ( x → ∞ ) . \frac{Q}{M_{\theta}}=O(x^{-1})\qquad(x\to\infty). 
 
 
 
 Thus Q / M θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 Q/M_{\theta}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0} . 



 
 Now Theorem  2.4 , applied to G θ G_{\theta} , shows that M θ M_{\theta} has only real negative zeros for
every 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 . Apply Lemma  4.9 on the open interval ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1) . We write 

 

 
 
 M θ ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) ) , M_{\theta}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}(1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)), 
 
 
 
 where the root parameters are non-negative and zero parameters are allowed in
order to keep the number of factors independent of θ \theta . For almost
every θ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \theta\in(0,1) , all derivatives μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) \mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta) exist and, for
every x > 0 x>0 , 

 

 
 
 ∑ j = 1 D x ​ μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) = ∂ θ M θ ​ ( x ) M θ ​ ( x ) = x 2 ​ Q ​ ( x ) M θ ​ ( x ) . \sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{x\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)}{1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)}=\frac{\partial_{\theta}M_{\theta}(x)}{M_{\theta}(x)}=\frac{x^{2}Q(x)}{M_{\theta}(x)}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the finite signed atomic measure ν ˙ θ := ∑ j = 1 D μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) ​ δ μ j ​ ( θ ) \dot{\nu}_{\theta}:=\sum_{j=1}^{D}\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)\delta_{\mu_{j}(\theta)} has Stieltjes transform 

 

 
 
 𝒮 ν ˙ θ ​ ( x ) = ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) 1 1 + x ​ λ ​ d ​ ν ˙ θ ​ ( λ ) = ∑ j = 1 D μ j ′ ​ ( θ ) 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( θ ) = x ​ Q ⁡ ( x ) M θ ​ ( x ) . \mathcal{S}_{\dot{\nu}_{\theta}}(x)=\int_{[0,\infty)}\frac{1}{1+x\lambda}\,d\dot{\nu}_{\theta}(\lambda)=\sum_{j=1}^{D}\frac{\mu_{j}^{\prime}(\theta)}{1+x\mu_{j}(\theta)}=x\frac{Q(x)}{M_{\theta}(x)}. 
 
 
 
 Since Q / M θ ∈ 𝒞 ≤ 3 0 Q/M_{\theta}\in\mathcal{C}_{\leq 3}^{0} , Lemma  4.7 yields 

 

 
 
 ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + ​ d ​ ν ˙ θ ​ ( λ ) ≤ 0 ( t ≥ 0 ) \int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}\,d\dot{\nu}_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq 0\qquad(t\geq 0) 
 
 
 
 for almost every θ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \theta\in(0,1) . By Corollary  4.10 , for almost every θ ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \theta\in(0,1) , 

 

 
 
 d d ​ θ ​ ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( θ ) − t ) + 2 = 2 ​ ∫ [ 0 , ∞ ) ( λ − t ) + ​ d ​ ν ˙ θ ​ ( λ ) ≤ 0 . \frac{d}{d\theta}\sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(\theta)-t)_{+}^{2}=2\int_{[0,\infty)}(\lambda-t)_{+}\,d\dot{\nu}_{\theta}(\lambda)\leq 0. 
 
 
 
 Integrating over [ ε , 1 − ε ] [\varepsilon,1-\varepsilon] and then letting
 ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 , using continuity of the root parameters at the
endpoints, gives 

 

 
 
 ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( 0 ) − t ) + 2 ≥ ∑ j = 1 D ( μ j ​ ( 1 ) − t ) + 2 . \sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(0)-t)_{+}^{2}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{D}(\mu_{j}(1)-t)_{+}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Since M 0 = M T M_{0}=M_{T} , Lemma  2.6 gives 

 

 
 
 M 0 ​ ( x ) = M T ​ ( x ) = det ( I + x ​ B T ​ B T ⊤ ) . M_{0}(x)=M_{T}(x)=\det(I+xB_{T}B_{T}^{\top}). 
 
 
 
 By the spectral theorem, this determinant factors as det ( I + x ​ B T ​ B T ⊤ ) = ∏ i ( 1 + x ​ λ i ) \det(I+xB_{T}B_{T}^{\top})=\prod_{i}(1+x\lambda_{i}) , where the λ i \lambda_{i} ’s are the eigenvalues of B T ​ B T ⊤ B_{T}B_{T}^{\top} , equivalently the squared singular values of B T B_{T} . Comparing this factorization with M 0 ​ ( x ) = ∏ j = 1 D ( 1 + x ​ μ j ​ ( 0 ) ) M_{0}(x)=\prod_{j=1}^{D}(1+x\mu_{j}(0)) , we see that the nonzero parameters μ j ​ ( 0 ) \mu_{j}(0) are precisely the squared singular values of T T , with harmless zero padding. The same argument applies to M 1 = M T ′ M_{1}=M_{T^{\prime}} . Hence the endpoint inequality is exactly 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( T ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( T ′ ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . ∎ \mathsf{S}_{t}(T)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(T^{\prime})\qquad(t\geq 0).\qed 
 
 
 




 
 Corollary 4.12 . 

 
 For every tree T T on n n vertices, 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( T ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(T)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 By Lemma  2.8 , every tree T T can be transformed into P n P_{n} by a finite sequence of the one-branch tree shifts. Applying Theorem  4.11 at each step and telescoping gives the result.
∎ 







 
 4.2. Path splicing at the second-order stop-loss level


 
 We first prove a path-specific splicing estimate at the level of the
second-order stop-loss quantities. The proof uses the explicit spectrum of
paths and a uniform bound for a sawtooth error term.



 
 The derivative of the threshold kernel will produce the following weight. For 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 0\leq\rho\leq 1/2 , put

 

 
 
 w ρ ​ ( u ) = 16 ​ π ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ​ ( cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ) , 0 ≤ u ≤ ρ . w_{\rho}(u)=16\pi\sin(2\pi u)\bigl(\cos(2\pi u)-\cos(2\pi\rho)\bigr),\qquad 0\leq u\leq\rho. 
 
 
 
 We also write ψ ⁡ ( x ) := { x } − 1 2 \psi(x):=\{x\}-\frac{1}{2} , where { x } = x − ⌊ x ⌋ \{x\}=x-\lfloor x\rfloor is the fractional part, and define

 

 
 
 J n ​ ( ρ ) = ∫ 0 ρ ψ ⁡ ( n ​ u ) ​ w ρ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . J_{n}(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\psi(nu)w_{\rho}(u)\,du. 
 
 
 


 
 The following estimate is a standard periodic-Bernoulli integration-by-parts
bound. We include the proof in order to keep the constant explicit.



 
 Lemma 4.13 . 

 
 For every ρ ∈ [ 1 / 6 , 1 / 2 ] \rho\in[1/6,1/2] and every integer n ≥ 1 n\geq 1 , 

 

 
 
 | J n ​ ( ρ ) | ≤ 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ​ n 2 . |J_{n}(\rho)|\leq\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27n^{2}}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Let 

 

 
 
 B 2 ( x ) = x 2 − x + 1 6 , B 3 ( x ) = x 3 − 3 2 x 2 + 1 2 x ( 0 ≤ x ≤ 1 ) , B_{2}(x)=x^{2}-x+\frac{1}{6},\qquad B_{3}(x)=x^{3}-\frac{3}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2}x\qquad(0\leq x\leq 1), 
 
 
 
 and let B ~ 2 , B ~ 3 \widetilde{B}_{2},\widetilde{B}_{3} be their 1 1 -periodic extensions to ℝ \mathbb{R} . Thus B ~ 2 ​ ( x ) = B 2 ​ ( { x } ) \widetilde{B}_{2}(x)=B_{2}(\{x\}) and B ~ 3 ​ ( x ) = B 3 ​ ( { x } ) \widetilde{B}_{3}(x)=B_{3}(\{x\}) . Away from the points where n ​ u ∈ ℤ nu\in\mathbb{Z} , we have 

 

 
 
 d d ​ u ​ B ~ 2 ​ ( n ​ u ) = 2 ​ n ​ ψ ​ ( n ​ u ) , d d ​ u ​ B ~ 3 ​ ( n ​ u ) = 3 ​ n ​ B ~ 2 ​ ( n ​ u ) . \frac{d}{du}\widetilde{B}_{2}(nu)=2n\psi(nu),\qquad\frac{d}{du}\widetilde{B}_{3}(nu)=3n\widetilde{B}_{2}(nu). 
 
 
 


 
 We partition [ 0 , ρ ] [0,\rho] at the finitely many points u u for which
 n ​ u ∈ ℤ nu\in\mathbb{Z} . On each resulting open subinterval, the functions
 B ~ 2 ​ ( n ​ u ) \widetilde{B}_{2}(nu) and B ~ 3 ​ ( n ​ u ) \widetilde{B}_{3}(nu) are ordinary smooth
functions of u u , since there { n ​ u } = n ​ u − k \{nu\}=nu-k for a fixed integer k k . Since B ~ 2 \widetilde{B}_{2} and B ~ 3 \widetilde{B}_{3} are
continuous, the boundary terms at the interior partition points cancel after
summing over all subintervals. Therefore 

 

 
 
 J n ​ ( ρ ) \displaystyle J_{n}(\rho) 
 = 1 2 ​ n ​ ∫ 0 ρ w ρ ​ ( u ) ​ ( B ~ 2 ​ ( n ​ u ) ) ′ ​ 𝑑 u \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{\rho}w_{\rho}(u)(\widetilde{B}_{2}(nu))^{\prime}\,du 
 
 
 
 
 
 = 1 2 ​ n ​ [ w ρ ​ ( u ) ​ B ~ 2 ​ ( n ​ u ) ] 0 ρ − 1 2 ​ n ​ ∫ 0 ρ w ρ ′ ​ ( u ) ​ B ~ 2 ​ ( n ​ u ) ​ 𝑑 u \displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n}[w_{\rho}(u)\widetilde{B}_{2}(nu)]_{0}^{\rho}-\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{\rho}w_{\rho}^{\prime}(u)\widetilde{B}_{2}(nu)\,du 
 
 
 
 
 
 = − 1 2 ​ n ∫ 0 ρ w ρ ′ ( u ) B ~ 2 ( n u ) d u \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2n}\int_{0}^{\rho}w_{\rho}^{\prime}(u)\widetilde{B}_{2}(nu)\,du 
 
 
 
 
 
 = − 1 6 ​ n 2 ∫ 0 ρ w ρ ′ ( u ) ( B ~ 3 ( n u ) ) ′ d u \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6n^{2}}\int_{0}^{\rho}w_{\rho}^{\prime}(u)(\widetilde{B}_{3}(nu))^{\prime}\,du 
 
 
 
 
 
 = − 1 6 ​ n 2 ​ [ w ρ ′ ​ ( u ) ​ B ~ 3 ​ ( n ​ u ) ] 0 ρ + 1 6 ​ n 2 ​ ∫ 0 ρ B ~ 3 ​ ( n ​ u ) ​ w ρ ′′ ​ ( u ) ​ d u . \displaystyle=-\frac{1}{6n^{2}}[w_{\rho}^{\prime}(u)\widetilde{B}_{3}(nu)]_{0}^{\rho}+\frac{1}{6n^{2}}\int_{0}^{\rho}\widetilde{B}_{3}(nu)w_{\rho}^{\prime\prime}(u)\,du. 
 
 
 
 The first boundary term vanishes because w ρ ​ ( 0 ) = w ρ ​ ( ρ ) = 0 w_{\rho}(0)=w_{\rho}(\rho)=0 . Also B ~ 3 ​ ( 0 ) = 0 \widetilde{B}_{3}(0)=0 , and a direct calculation gives ‖ B ~ 3 ‖ ∞ = 3 36 \|\widetilde{B}_{3}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{36} . Hence 

 

 
 
 | J n ​ ( ρ ) | ≤ 3 216 ​ n 2 ​ ( | w ρ ′ ​ ( ρ ) | + ∫ 0 ρ | w ρ ′′ ​ ( u ) | ​ 𝑑 u ) . |J_{n}(\rho)|\leq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{216n^{2}}\left(|w_{\rho}^{\prime}(\rho)|+\int_{0}^{\rho}|w_{\rho}^{\prime\prime}(u)|\,du\right). 
 
 
 


 
 Since ρ ∈ [ 1 / 6 , 1 / 2 ] \rho\in[1/6,1/2] , we have
 cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ∈ [ − 1 , 1 / 2 ] \cos(2\pi\rho)\in[-1,1/2] . Writing z = cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) z=\cos(2\pi u) , direct differentiation gives 

 

 
 
 w ρ ′ ​ ( u ) = 32 ​ π 2 ​ ( 2 ​ z 2 − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ​ z − 1 ) , w_{\rho}^{\prime}(u)=32\pi^{2}\left(2z^{2}-\cos(2\pi\rho)z-1\right), 
 
 
 
 and therefore 

 

 
 
 | w ρ ′ ​ ( ρ ) | = 32 ​ π 2 ​ ( 1 − cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ) . |w_{\rho}^{\prime}(\rho)|=32\pi^{2}(1-\cos^{2}(2\pi\rho)). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, 

 

 
 
 w ρ ′′ ​ ( u ) = 64 ​ π 3 ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ​ ( cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) − 4 ​ cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ) . w_{\rho}^{\prime\prime}(u)=64\pi^{3}\sin(2\pi u)\left(\cos(2\pi\rho)-4\cos(2\pi u)\right). 
 
 
 
 Since 0 ≤ u ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 0\leq u\leq\rho\leq 1/2 , the substitution z = cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) z=\cos(2\pi u) gives 

 

 
 
 ∫ 0 ρ | w ρ ′′ ​ ( u ) | ​ 𝑑 u = 32 ​ π 2 ​ ∫ cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) 1 | cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) − 4 ​ z | ​ 𝑑 z . \int_{0}^{\rho}|w_{\rho}^{\prime\prime}(u)|\,du=32\pi^{2}\int_{\cos(2\pi\rho)}^{1}|\cos(2\pi\rho)-4z|\,dz. 
 
 
 
 Thus 

 

 
 
 | w ρ ′ ​ ( ρ ) | + ∫ 0 ρ | w ρ ′′ ​ ( u ) | ​ 𝑑 u = 32 ​ π 2 ​ ( 1 − cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) + ∫ cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) 1 | cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) − 4 ​ z | ​ 𝑑 z ) . |w_{\rho}^{\prime}(\rho)|+\int_{0}^{\rho}|w_{\rho}^{\prime\prime}(u)|\,du=32\pi^{2}\left(1-\cos^{2}(2\pi\rho)+\int_{\cos(2\pi\rho)}^{1}|\cos(2\pi\rho)-4z|\,dz\right). 
 
 
 
 If cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 / 2 ] \cos(2\pi\rho)\in[0,1/2] , then the expression in parentheses is 

 

 
 
 3 − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) − 2 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ≤ 3 . 3-\cos(2\pi\rho)-2\cos^{2}(2\pi\rho)\leq 3. 
 
 
 
 If cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ∈ [ − 1 , 0 ] \cos(2\pi\rho)\in[-1,0] , then it is 

 

 
 
 3 − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) + cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) 4 ≤ 17 4 . 3-\cos(2\pi\rho)+\frac{\cos^{2}(2\pi\rho)}{4}\leq\frac{17}{4}. 
 
 
 
 Consequently, 

 

 
 
 | w ρ ′ ​ ( ρ ) | + ∫ 0 ρ | w ρ ′′ ​ ( u ) | ​ 𝑑 u ≤ 136 ​ π 2 . |w_{\rho}^{\prime}(\rho)|+\int_{0}^{\rho}|w_{\rho}^{\prime\prime}(u)|\,du\leq 136\pi^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 | J n ​ ( ρ ) | ≤ 3 216 ​ n 2 ⋅ 136 ​ π 2 = 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ​ n 2 < 11 n 2 . ∎ |J_{n}(\rho)|\leq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{216n^{2}}\cdot 136\pi^{2}=\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27n^{2}}<\frac{11}{n^{2}}.\qed 
 
 
 




 
 We now prove the path-splicing estimate needed in the deletion step of the second-order stop-loss induction. If X X is a set, we write 𝟏 X \mathbf{1}_{X} for its indicator function.



 
 Theorem 4.14 . 

 
 For all integers a , b ≥ 1 a,b\geq 1 and all t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 (20) 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P a + b ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P a ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P b ) ≤ ( 4 − t ) + 2 − ( 3 − t ) + 2 . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{a+b})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{a})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{b})\leq(4-t)_{+}^{2}-(3-t)_{+}^{2}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 We use the standard formula for the nonzero squared singular values of a path: 

 

 
 
 μ i ( P m ) = 4 cos 2 π ​ i m + 1 , i = 1 , … , ⌊ m 2 ⌋ . \mu_{i}(P_{m})=4\cos^{2}\frac{\pi i}{m+1},\qquad i=1,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\right\rfloor. 
 
 
 
 Put 

 

 
 
 p = a + 1 , q = b + 1 , L = p + q − 1 = a + b + 1 . p=a+1,\qquad q=b+1,\qquad L=p+q-1=a+b+1. 
 
 
 
 If t > 4 t>4 , then all squared singular values of paths are at most 4 4 , and
both sides of the desired inequality are zero. Thus we may assume
 0 ≤ t ≤ 4 0\leq t\leq 4 , and write 

 

 
 
 t = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( π ​ ρ ) , 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 2 . t=4\cos^{2}(\pi\rho),\qquad 0\leq\rho\leq\frac{1}{2}. 
 
 
 
 Let K ⁡ ( t ) K(t) be the right-hand side minus the left-hand side of the inequality ( 20 ), and set K p , q ​ ( ρ ) := K ⁡ ( 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( π ​ ρ ) ) K_{p,q}(\rho):=K(4\cos^{2}(\pi\rho)) . Define g ρ ​ ( u ) := ( 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( π ​ u ) − 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( π ​ ρ ) ) + 2 g_{\rho}(u):=\left(4\cos^{2}(\pi u)-4\cos^{2}(\pi\rho)\right)_{+}^{2} . Then the path spectrum formula gives 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) = g ρ ​ ( 0 ) − g ρ ​ ( 1 / 6 ) + ∑ i = 1 ⌊ p / 2 ⌋ g ρ ​ ( i / p ) + ∑ j = 1 ⌊ q / 2 ⌋ g ρ ​ ( j / q ) − ∑ k = 1 ⌊ L / 2 ⌋ g ρ ​ ( k / L ) . K_{p,q}(\rho)=g_{\rho}(0)-g_{\rho}(1/6)+\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor p/2\rfloor}g_{\rho}(i/p)+\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor q/2\rfloor}g_{\rho}(j/q)-\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor L/2\rfloor}g_{\rho}(k/L). 
 
 
 


 
 We use the following elementary summation-by-parts identity. Let { ( α , η α ) } α ∈ A \{(\alpha,\eta_{\alpha})\}_{\alpha\in A} be a finite set of pairs with
 α ∈ [ 0 , ρ ] \alpha\in[0,\rho] , and let g g be C 1 C^{1} on [ 0 , ρ ] [0,\rho] . If
 g ⁡ ( ρ ) = 0 g(\rho)=0 and w ​ ( u ) = − g ′ ​ ( u ) w(u)=-g^{\prime}(u) , then we have 

 

 
 
 g ⁡ ( α ) = ∫ α ρ w ⁡ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u = ∫ 0 ρ 𝟏 α ≤ u ​ w ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . g(\alpha)=\int_{\alpha}^{\rho}w(u)\,du=\int_{0}^{\rho}\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\leq u}w(u)\,du. 
 
 
 
 Therefore, using that the sum over α \alpha is finite, 

 

 
 
 ∑ α η α ​ g ​ ( α ) = ∫ 0 ρ ( ∑ α η α ​ 𝟏 α ≤ u ) ​ w ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u = ∫ 0 ρ ( ∑ α ≤ u η α ) ​ w ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . \sum_{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha}g(\alpha)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\left(\sum_{\alpha}\eta_{\alpha}\mathbf{1}_{\alpha\leq u}\right)w(u)\,du=\int_{0}^{\rho}\left(\sum_{\alpha\leq u}\eta_{\alpha}\right)w(u)\,du. 
 
 
 


 
 Discarding the terms with α > ρ \alpha>\rho and applying this identity to the remaining signed atoms 

 

 
 
 ( 0 , + 1 ) , ( 1 / 6 , − 1 ) , ( i / p , + 1 ) , ( j / q , + 1 ) , ( k / L , − 1 ) , (0,+1),\qquad(1/6,-1),\qquad(i/p,+1),\qquad(j/q,+1),\qquad(k/L,-1), 
 
 
 
 we obtain 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) = ∫ 0 ρ A p , q ​ ( u ) ​ w ρ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u , K_{p,q}(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}A_{p,q}(u)w_{\rho}(u)\,du, 
 
 
 
 where the values of A p , q A_{p,q} at the finitely many discontinuity points are irrelevant for the integral, and 

 

 
 
 A p , q ​ ( u ) = 1 − 𝟏 u ≥ 1 / 6 + ⌊ p ​ u ⌋ + ⌊ q ​ u ⌋ − ⌊ L ​ u ⌋ = 𝟏 u < 1 / 6 + ⌊ p ​ u ⌋ + ⌊ q ​ u ⌋ − ⌊ L ​ u ⌋ . A_{p,q}(u)=1-\mathbf{1}_{u\geq 1/6}+\lfloor pu\rfloor+\lfloor qu\rfloor-\lfloor Lu\rfloor=\mathbf{1}_{u<1/6}+\lfloor pu\rfloor+\lfloor qu\rfloor-\lfloor Lu\rfloor. 
 
 
 
 Here 

 

 
 
 w ρ ​ ( u ) = − g ρ ′ ​ ( u ) = 16 ​ π ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ​ ( cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ) , 0 ≤ u ≤ ρ . w_{\rho}(u)=-g_{\rho}^{\prime}(u)=16\pi\sin(2\pi u)\bigl(\cos(2\pi u)-\cos(2\pi\rho)\bigr),\qquad 0\leq u\leq\rho. 
 
 
 
 Since 0 ≤ u ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 0\leq u\leq\rho\leq 1/2 , we have sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ≥ 0 \sin(2\pi u)\geq 0 and cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ≥ 0 \cos(2\pi u)-\cos(2\pi\rho)\geq 0 . Thus w ρ ​ ( u ) ≥ 0 w_{\rho}(u)\geq 0 on [ 0 , ρ ] [0,\rho] . 



 
 Because L = p + q − 1 L=p+q-1 , we have L ​ u = p ​ u + q ​ u − u Lu=pu+qu-u . Hence 

 

 
 
 ⌊ L ​ u ⌋ − ⌊ p ​ u ⌋ − ⌊ q ​ u ⌋ = ⌊ { p ​ u } + { q ​ u } − u ⌋ ≤ 1 . \lfloor Lu\rfloor-\lfloor pu\rfloor-\lfloor qu\rfloor=\bigl\lfloor\{pu\}+\{qu\}-u\bigr\rfloor\leq 1. 
 
 
 
 Therefore, if 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 6 0\leq\rho\leq 1/6 , then u < 1 / 6 u<1/6 for all
 0 ≤ u < ρ 0\leq u<\rho , and hence 

 

 
 
 A p , q ​ ( u ) ≥ 0 ( 0 ≤ u ≤ ρ ) A_{p,q}(u)\geq 0\qquad(0\leq u\leq\rho) 
 
 
 
 up to the irrelevant endpoint convention. Since w ρ ≥ 0 w_{\rho}\geq 0 , the integral
representation gives 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) ≥ 0 ( 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 6 ) . K_{p,q}(\rho)\geq 0\qquad(0\leq\rho\leq 1/6). 
 
 
 


 
 It remains to consider ρ ∈ [ 1 / 6 , 1 / 2 ] \rho\in[1/6,1/2] . Using 

 

 
 
 ⌊ p ​ u ⌋ + ⌊ q ​ u ⌋ − ⌊ L ​ u ⌋ = u + { L ​ u } − { p ​ u } − { q ​ u } , \lfloor pu\rfloor+\lfloor qu\rfloor-\lfloor Lu\rfloor=u+\{Lu\}-\{pu\}-\{qu\}, 
 
 
 
 and the notation ψ ⁡ ( x ) = { x } − 1 / 2 \psi(x)=\{x\}-1/2 , we obtain the exact decomposition 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) = M ⁡ ( ρ ) + J L ​ ( ρ ) − J p ​ ( ρ ) − J q ​ ( ρ ) , K_{p,q}(\rho)=M(\rho)+J_{L}(\rho)-J_{p}(\rho)-J_{q}(\rho), 
 
 
 
 where 

 

 
 
 M ⁡ ( ρ ) = ∫ 0 ρ ( 𝟏 u < 1 / 6 + u − 1 2 ) ​ w ρ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u . M(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\left(\mathbf{1}_{u<1/6}+u-\frac{1}{2}\right)w_{\rho}(u)\,du. 
 
 
 
 With R = 2 ​ π ​ ρ R=2\pi\rho , direct integration gives 

 

 
 
 M ⁡ ( ρ ) = R ​ cos ⁡ 2 ​ R + 2 ​ R − 3 2 ​ sin ⁡ 2 ​ R − π ​ cos ⁡ 2 ​ R π . M(\rho)=\frac{R\cos 2R+2R-\frac{3}{2}\sin 2R-\pi\cos 2R}{\pi}. 
 
 
 
 Moreover M M is increasing on [ 1 / 6 , 1 / 2 ] [1/6,1/2] . Indeed, 

 

 
 
 d ​ M d ​ R = 2 π ​ ( 1 − cos ⁡ 2 ​ R + ( π − R ) ​ sin ⁡ 2 ​ R ) ≥ 0 . \frac{dM}{dR}=\frac{2}{\pi}\left(1-\cos 2R+(\pi-R)\sin 2R\right)\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 Hence M ⁡ ( ρ ) ≥ M ⁡ ( 1 / 6 ) = m 0 M(\rho)\geq M(1/6)=m_{0} , where m 0 = 1 − 3 ​ 3 4 ​ π m_{0}=1-\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4\pi} . 



 
 By Lemma  4.13 , 

 

 
 
 | J n ​ ( ρ ) | ≤ 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ⋅ 1 n 2 . |J_{n}(\rho)|\leq\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{2}}. 
 
 
 
 If p , q ≥ 7 p,q\geq 7 , then L ≥ 13 L\geq 13 , and therefore 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) ≥ m 0 − 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ​ ( 2 7 2 + 1 13 2 ) > 0 . K_{p,q}(\rho)\geq m_{0}-\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27}\left(\frac{2}{7^{2}}+\frac{1}{13^{2}}\right)>0. 
 
 
 


 
 It remains to consider the cases in which min ⁡ ( p , q ) ≤ 6 \min(p,q)\leq 6 . By symmetry we
may assume p ≤ q p\leq q . If 2 ≤ p ≤ 6 < q 2\leq p\leq 6<q , set D p ​ ( ρ ) := M ⁡ ( ρ ) − J p ​ ( ρ ) D_{p}(\rho):=M(\rho)-J_{p}(\rho) . The finite certificate in Lemma  A.1 (i), proved in
Appendix  A , gives 

 

 
 
 D p ( ρ ) − 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ( 1 7 2 + 1 ( p + 6 ) 2 ) ≥ 10 − 4 ( p = 2 , … , 6 ,  1 / 6 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 ) . D_{p}(\rho)-\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27}\left(\frac{1}{7^{2}}+\frac{1}{(p+6)^{2}}\right)\geq 10^{-4}\qquad(p=2,\ldots,6,\;1/6\leq\rho\leq 1/2). 
 
 
 
 Since q ≥ 7 q\geq 7 and p + q − 1 ≥ p + 6 p+q-1\geq p+6 , this implies 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) \displaystyle K_{p,q}(\rho) 
 ≥ D p ​ ( ρ ) − 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ​ ( 1 q 2 + 1 ( p + q − 1 ) 2 ) \displaystyle\geq D_{p}(\rho)-\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27}\left(\frac{1}{q^{2}}+\frac{1}{(p+q-1)^{2}}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≥ D p ​ ( ρ ) − 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ​ ( 1 7 2 + 1 ( p + 6 ) 2 ) ≥ 10 − 4 > 0 . \displaystyle\geq D_{p}(\rho)-\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27}\left(\frac{1}{7^{2}}+\frac{1}{(p+6)^{2}}\right)\geq 10^{-4}>0. 
 
 
 
 Finally, if 2 ≤ p ≤ q ≤ 6 2\leq p\leq q\leq 6 , Lemma  A.1 (ii)
gives 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) ≥ 10 − 4 ( 1 / 6 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 ) . K_{p,q}(\rho)\geq 10^{-4}\qquad(1/6\leq\rho\leq 1/2). 
 
 
 
 Thus K p , q ​ ( ρ ) ≥ 0 K_{p,q}(\rho)\geq 0 for all 0 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 0\leq\rho\leq 1/2 , which is precisely the
desired inequality.
∎ 







 
 4.3. Pure even cycles


 
 We first record a trigonometric pair inequality comparing two adjacent
entries from the squared-singular-value list of an even cycle with the
corresponding two entries from the path.



 
 Lemma 4.15 . 

 
 Let m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 and 1 ≤ h ≤ ⌊ m / 2 ⌋ 1\leq h\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor . Then 

 

 
 
 cos 2 ⁡ ( h − 1 ) ​ π m + cos 2 ⁡ h ​ π m ≥ cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ h − 1 ) ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 + cos 2 ⁡ 2 ​ h ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 . \cos^{2}\frac{(h-1)\pi}{m}+\cos^{2}\frac{h\pi}{m}\geq\cos^{2}\frac{(2h-1)\pi}{2m+1}+\cos^{2}\frac{2h\pi}{2m+1}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Put α = π / m \alpha=\pi/m , β = π / ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) \beta=\pi/(2m+1) , u = ( 2 ​ h − 1 ) ​ α u=(2h-1)\alpha and v = ( 4 ​ h − 1 ) ​ β v=(4h-1)\beta . Using 

 

 
 
 cos 2 ⁡ x + cos 2 ⁡ y = 1 + cos ⁡ ( x + y ) ​ cos ⁡ ( x − y ) , \cos^{2}x+\cos^{2}y=1+\cos(x+y)\cos(x-y), 
 
 
 
 the desired inequality is equivalent to 

 

 
 (21) 
 
 cos ⁡ u ​ cos ⁡ α ≥ cos ⁡ v ​ cos ⁡ β . \cos u\cos\alpha\geq\cos v\cos\beta. 
 
 
 
 We have 0 < β < α 0<\beta<\alpha , and 

 

 
 
 v − u = ( m + 1 − 2 ​ h ) ​ π m ⁡ ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) > 0 . v-u=\frac{(m+1-2h)\pi}{m(2m+1)}>0. 
 
 
 
 Set r = v − u r=v-u and s = α − β s=\alpha-\beta . Then 0 < r < s 0<r<s . 



 
 We prove ( 21 ) by considering the positions of
 u u and v v relative to π / 2 \pi/2 . First suppose that u ≥ π / 2 u\geq\pi/2 .
Since v = u + r v=u+r and α = β + s \alpha=\beta+s , we have 

 

 
 
 cos ⁡ u ​ cos ⁡ α − cos ⁡ v ​ cos ⁡ β \displaystyle\cos u\cos\alpha-\cos v\cos\beta 
 = sin ⁡ u ​ cos ⁡ β ​ sin ⁡ r − cos ⁡ u ​ sin ⁡ β ​ sin ⁡ s \displaystyle=\sin u\cos\beta\sin r-\cos u\sin\beta\sin s 
 
 
 
 
 
 − cos ⁡ u ​ cos ⁡ β ⁡ ( cos ⁡ r − cos ⁡ s ) . \displaystyle-\cos u\cos\beta(\cos r-\cos s). 
 
 
 
 Here sin ⁡ u ≥ 0 \sin u\geq 0 , cos ⁡ β > 0 \cos\beta>0 , sin ⁡ r > 0 \sin r>0 ,
 − cos ⁡ u ≥ 0 -\cos u\geq 0 , and cos ⁡ r − cos ⁡ s > 0 \cos r-\cos s>0 . Hence the right-hand side is
nonnegative. 



 
 Next suppose that u < π / 2 ≤ v u<\pi/2\leq v . Then
 cos ⁡ u ​ cos ⁡ α > 0 \cos u\cos\alpha>0 , while cos ⁡ v ​ cos ⁡ β ≤ 0 \cos v\cos\beta\leq 0 , so
( 21 ) is immediate. 



 
 It remains to consider the case v < π / 2 v<\pi/2 . Then all cosines in
( 21 ) are positive. Taking logarithms, the inequality
( 21 ) is equivalent to 

 

 
 (22) 
 
 ∫ u v tan ⁡ x ​ 𝑑 x ≥ ∫ β α tan ⁡ x ​ 𝑑 x . \int_{u}^{v}\tan x\,dx\geq\int_{\beta}^{\alpha}\tan x\,dx. 
 
 
 
 Let M = ( u + v ) / 2 M=(u+v)/2 . Since M > α > β M>\alpha>\beta and x ↦ tan ⁡ x / x x\mapsto\tan x/x is increasing on ( 0 , π / 2 ) (0,\pi/2) , we have 

 

 
 
 tan ⁡ M ≥ M α ​ tan ⁡ α , tan ⁡ β ≤ β α ​ tan ⁡ α . \tan M\geq\frac{M}{\alpha}\tan\alpha,\qquad\tan\beta\leq\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\tan\alpha. 
 
 
 
 By convexity of tan ⁡ x \tan x on ( 0 , π / 2 ) (0,\pi/2) , 

 

 
 
 ∫ u v tan ⁡ x ​ 𝑑 x ≥ r ​ tan ⁡ M , ∫ β α tan ⁡ x ​ 𝑑 x ≤ s 2 ​ ( tan ⁡ α + tan ⁡ β ) . \int_{u}^{v}\tan x\,dx\geq r\tan M,\qquad\int_{\beta}^{\alpha}\tan x\,dx\leq\frac{s}{2}(\tan\alpha+\tan\beta). 
 
 
 
 Therefore ( 22 ) follows once 

 

 
 (23) 
 
 r ​ M ≥ s ⁡ ( α + β ) 2 . rM\geq\frac{s(\alpha+\beta)}{2}. 
 
 
 
 Since 2 ​ r ​ M = v 2 − u 2 2rM=v^{2}-u^{2} and s ⁡ ( α + β ) = α 2 − β 2 s(\alpha+\beta)=\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2} , inequality
( 23 ) is equivalent to 

 

 
 
 Δ m , h := m 2 ​ ( ( 4 ​ h − 1 ) 2 + 1 ) − ( 2 ​ m + 1 ) 2 ​ ( ( 2 ​ h − 1 ) 2 + 1 ) ≥ 0 . \Delta_{m,h}:=m^{2}\bigl((4h-1)^{2}+1\bigr)-(2m+1)^{2}\bigl((2h-1)^{2}+1\bigr)\geq 0. 
 
 
 


 
 Write m = 2 ​ h + q m=2h+q . A direct expansion gives 

 

 
 
 Δ m , h = 2 ​ ( 8 ​ h 2 ​ q + 2 ​ h 2 + 4 ​ h ​ q 2 − 4 ​ h ​ q − 6 ​ h − 3 ​ q 2 − 4 ​ q − 1 ) . \Delta_{m,h}=2\left(8h^{2}q+2h^{2}+4hq^{2}-4hq-6h-3q^{2}-4q-1\right). 
 
 
 
 The assumption v < π / 2 v<\pi/2 implies m ≥ 4 ​ h − 1 m\geq 4h-1 , and hence
 q ≥ 2 ​ h − 1 q\geq 2h-1 . If h ≥ 2 h\geq 2 , the expression in parentheses is increasing in
 q q for q ≥ 2 ​ h − 1 q\geq 2h-1 . At q = 2 ​ h − 1 q=2h-1 , the expression equals 2 ​ h ​ ( 16 ​ h 2 − 21 ​ h + 3 ) > 0 2h(16h^{2}-21h+3)>0 . Thus Δ m , h ≥ 0 \Delta_{m,h}\geq 0 for h ≥ 2 h\geq 2 . 



 
 For h = 1 h=1 , the same expansion reduces to 

 

 
 
 Δ m , 1 = 2 ​ ( q 2 − 5 ) , q = m − 2 . \Delta_{m,1}=2(q^{2}-5),\qquad q=m-2. 
 
 
 
 This is nonnegative for q ≥ 3 q\geq 3 , equivalently m ≥ 5 m\geq 5 . The only remaining cases in the present case v < π / 2 v<\pi/2 are ( m , h ) = ( 3 , 1 ) (m,h)=(3,1) and ( m , h ) = ( 4 , 1 ) (m,h)=(4,1) . A direct substitution into ( 21 ) gives a strict inequality in both cases. This proves the lemma.
∎ 





 
 The pure even-cycle comparison follows by grouping the squared singular values into pairs and applying weak majorization.



 
 Theorem 4.16 . 

 
 For every m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 and every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( C 2 ​ m ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P 2 ​ m ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(C_{2m})\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{2m}). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 The squared singular values are 

 

 
 
 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ π ​ j m , j = 0 , … , m − 1 , 4\cos^{2}\frac{\pi j}{m},\qquad j=0,\ldots,m-1, 
 
 
 
 for C 2 ​ m C_{2m} , and 

 

 
 
 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ j ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 , j = 1 , … , m , 4\cos^{2}\frac{j\pi}{2m+1},\qquad j=1,\ldots,m, 
 
 
 
 for P 2 ​ m P_{2m} . 



 
 Let c ↓ c^{\downarrow} and p ↓ p^{\downarrow} denote the decreasing
rearrangements of these two squared-singular-value vectors. We claim that
 c ↓ c^{\downarrow} weakly majorizes p ↓ p^{\downarrow} . Indeed, 

 

 
 
 c i ↓ = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ π ​ ⌊ i / 2 ⌋ m , p i ↓ = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ i ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 , 1 ≤ i ≤ m . c_{i}^{\downarrow}=4\cos^{2}\frac{\pi\lfloor i/2\rfloor}{m},\qquad p_{i}^{\downarrow}=4\cos^{2}\frac{i\pi}{2m+1},\qquad 1\leq i\leq m. 
 
 
 
 For 1 ≤ h ≤ ⌊ m / 2 ⌋ 1\leq h\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor , put 

 

 
 
 A h = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( h − 1 ) ​ π m , B h = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ h ​ π m , A_{h}=4\cos^{2}\frac{(h-1)\pi}{m},\qquad B_{h}=4\cos^{2}\frac{h\pi}{m}, 
 
 
 
 and 

 

 
 
 X h = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ h − 1 ) ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 , Y h = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ 2 ​ h ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 . X_{h}=4\cos^{2}\frac{(2h-1)\pi}{2m+1},\qquad Y_{h}=4\cos^{2}\frac{2h\pi}{2m+1}. 
 
 
 
 The angle comparison gives A h ≥ X h A_{h}\geq X_{h} . Moreover,
Lemma  4.15 , multiplied by 4 4 , gives 

 

 
 
 A h + B h ≥ X h + Y h . A_{h}+B_{h}\geq X_{h}+Y_{h}. 
 
 
 
 Thus each pair ( A h , B h ) (A_{h},B_{h}) weakly majorizes the pair ( X h , Y h ) (X_{h},Y_{h}) . Summing these pair inequalities gives all even partial sums: 

 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 2 ​ r c i ↓ = ∑ h = 1 r ( A h + B h ) ≥ ∑ h = 1 r ( X h + Y h ) = ∑ i = 1 2 ​ r p i ↓ , 1 ≤ r ≤ ⌊ m 2 ⌋ . \sum_{i=1}^{2r}c_{i}^{\downarrow}=\sum_{h=1}^{r}(A_{h}+B_{h})\geq\sum_{h=1}^{r}(X_{h}+Y_{h})=\sum_{i=1}^{2r}p_{i}^{\downarrow},\qquad 1\leq r\leq\left\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\right\rfloor. 
 
 
 


 
 It remains to check the odd partial sums. Let r ≥ 0 r\geq 0 and
 2 ​ r + 1 ≤ m 2r+1\leq m . Then 

 

 
 
 r m < 2 ​ r + 1 2 ​ m + 1 , \frac{r}{m}<\frac{2r+1}{2m+1}, 
 
 
 
 and hence, by the monotonicity of x ↦ cos 2 ⁡ ( π ​ x ) x\mapsto\cos^{2}(\pi x) on
 [ 0 , 1 / 2 ] [0,1/2] , 

 

 
 
 c 2 ​ r + 1 ↓ = 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ r ​ π m ≥ 4 ​ cos 2 ⁡ ( 2 ​ r + 1 ) ​ π 2 ​ m + 1 = p 2 ​ r + 1 ↓ . c_{2r+1}^{\downarrow}=4\cos^{2}\frac{r\pi}{m}\geq 4\cos^{2}\frac{(2r+1)\pi}{2m+1}=p_{2r+1}^{\downarrow}. 
 
 
 
 Adding this inequality to the even partial-sum inequality up to 2 ​ r 2r gives
the odd partial sums: 

 

 
 
 ∑ i = 1 2 ​ r + 1 c i ↓ ≥ ∑ i = 1 2 ​ r + 1 p i ↓ . \sum_{i=1}^{2r+1}c_{i}^{\downarrow}\geq\sum_{i=1}^{2r+1}p_{i}^{\downarrow}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore c ↓ c^{\downarrow} weakly majorizes p ↓ p^{\downarrow} . 



 
 For fixed t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , the function ϕ t ​ ( λ ) = ( λ − t ) + 2 \phi_{t}(\lambda)=(\lambda-t)_{+}^{2} is increasing and convex on [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) . By the standard characterization of weak submajorization by increasing convex functions [ 17 , Section A.4] , we have 

 

 
 
 ∑ i ϕ t ​ ( c i ↓ ) ≥ ∑ i ϕ t ​ ( p i ↓ ) . \sum_{i}\phi_{t}(c_{i}^{\downarrow})\geq\sum_{i}\phi_{t}(p_{i}^{\downarrow}). 
 
 
 
 This is exactly 𝖲 t ​ ( C 2 ​ m ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P 2 ​ m ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(C_{2m})\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{2m}) , and the proof is complete.
∎ 









 
 5. Deletion theory at the second-order stop-loss level


 
 We first turn the effect of adding back a deleted vertex into a
one-dimensional interval estimate. The rank-one trace formula is the bridge
from the spectral stop-loss functional to this interval estimate. The rest of
the section then converts the interval estimate into concrete deletion
criteria for the minimal-counterexample argument.



 
 We first record the rank-one trace formula for the stop-loss kernel. This is
a standard finite-dimensional trace derivative formula, but we include the
short proof for completeness.



 
 Lemma 5.1 . 

 
 Let M M be a real symmetric matrix, let b b be a vector of the same dimension, and set M θ = M + θ ​ b ​ b ⊤ M_{\theta}=M+\theta bb^{\top} for 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 . Then, for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 tr ⁡ ( M 1 − t ​ I ) + 2 − tr ⁡ ( M 0 − t ​ I ) + 2 = 2 ​ ∫ 0 1 b ⊤ ​ ( M θ − t ​ I ) + ​ b ​ 𝑑 θ . \tr(M_{1}-tI)_{+}^{2}-\tr(M_{0}-tI)_{+}^{2}=2\int_{0}^{1}b^{\top}(M_{\theta}-tI)_{+}b\,d\theta. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Put φ t ​ ( y ) = ( y − t ) + 2 \varphi_{t}(y)=(y-t)_{+}^{2} . Then
 φ t ∈ C 1 ​ ( ℝ ) \varphi_{t}\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}) and
 φ t ′ ​ ( y ) = 2 ​ ( y − t ) + \varphi_{t}^{\prime}(y)=2(y-t)_{+} . Choose R > 0 R>0 such that
 spec ⁡ ( M θ ) ⊂ [ − R , R ] \spec(M_{\theta})\subset[-R,R] for every 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 .
By Weierstrass approximation, there are polynomials q j q_{j} such that
 q j → φ t ′ q_{j}\to\varphi_{t}^{\prime} uniformly on [ − R , R ] [-R,R] . Define 

 

 
 
 p j ​ ( y ) = φ t ​ ( 0 ) + ∫ 0 y q j ​ ( s ) ​ 𝑑 s . p_{j}(y)=\varphi_{t}(0)+\int_{0}^{y}q_{j}(s)\,ds. 
 
 
 
 Then p j → φ t p_{j}\to\varphi_{t} and p j ′ → φ t ′ p_{j}^{\prime}\to\varphi_{t}^{\prime} uniformly on [ − R , R ] [-R,R] . 



 
 For a polynomial p p , cyclicity of trace gives 

 

 
 
 d d ​ θ ​ tr ⁡ p ⁡ ( M θ ) = tr ⁡ ( p ′ ​ ( M θ ) ​ b ​ b ⊤ ) = b ⊤ ​ p ′ ​ ( M θ ) ​ b . \frac{d}{d\theta}\tr p(M_{\theta})=\tr\bigl(p^{\prime}(M_{\theta})bb^{\top}\bigr)=b^{\top}p^{\prime}(M_{\theta})b. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 tr ⁡ p j ​ ( M 1 ) − tr ⁡ p j ​ ( M 0 ) = ∫ 0 1 b ⊤ ​ p j ′ ​ ( M θ ) ​ b ​ 𝑑 θ . \tr p_{j}(M_{1})-\tr p_{j}(M_{0})=\int_{0}^{1}b^{\top}p_{j}^{\prime}(M_{\theta})b\,d\theta. 
 
 
 
 Letting j → ∞ j\to\infty , using the uniform convergence on the common spectral
interval [ − R , R ] [-R,R] , gives the desired identity.
∎ 





 
 We next convert the trace formula into a local lower bound for the effect of adding back a deleted vertex. Throughout, N G ​ ( v ) N_{G}(v) denotes the open neighborhood of v v in G G , that is, the set of vertices adjacent to v v . For a vector x x , we write ‖ x ‖ 2 \|x\|_{2} for its Euclidean norm, so that ‖ x ‖ 2 2 = x ⊤ ​ x \|x\|_{2}^{2}=x^{\top}x .



 
 Lemma 5.2 . 

 
 Let G G be a bipartite graph and let v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) v\in V(G) be a non-isolated vertex. Choose the bipartition so that v v lies on the left side. Let B G − v B_{G-v} be the corresponding biadjacency matrix of G − v G-v . Let b b be the 0 0 - 1 1 indicator vector of N G ​ ( v ) N_{G}(v) (indexed by the right-side vertices of G − v G-v ), and set 

 

 
 
 M = B G − v ⊤ ​ B G − v , d = ‖ b ‖ 2 2 = d G ​ ( v ) > 0 , γ = b ⊤ ​ M ​ b . M=B_{G-v}^{\top}B_{G-v},\qquad d=\|b\|_{2}^{2}=d_{G}(v)>0,\qquad\gamma=b^{\top}Mb. 
 
 
 
 For 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 , put M θ = M + θ ​ b ​ b ⊤ M_{\theta}=M+\theta bb^{\top} . Then, for every
 t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ ∫ γ / d γ / d + d ( u − t ) + ​ 𝑑 u . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2\int_{\gamma/d}^{\gamma/d+d}(u-t)_{+}\,du. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Since v v lies on the left side, adding v v adds the row b ⊤ b^{\top} to the biadjacency matrix: 

 

 
 
 B G = ( B G − v b ⊤ ) . B_{G}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{G-v}\\
b^{\top}\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the matrix used to compute the squared singular values changes from 

 

 
 
 M 0 = B G − v ⊤ ​ B G − v = M to M 1 = B G − v ⊤ ​ B G − v + b ​ b ⊤ = M + b ​ b ⊤ . M_{0}=B_{G-v}^{\top}B_{G-v}=M\qquad\text{to}\qquad M_{1}=B_{G-v}^{\top}B_{G-v}+bb^{\top}=M+bb^{\top}. 
 
 
 
 Possible additional zero squared singular values do not contribute to
 𝖲 t \mathsf{S}_{t} . Hence 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) = tr ⁡ ( M 1 − t ) + 2 , 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) = tr ⁡ ( M 0 − t ) + 2 . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)=\tr(M_{1}-t)_{+}^{2},\qquad\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)=\tr(M_{0}-t)_{+}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 By Lemma  5.1 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) = 2 ​ ∫ 0 1 b ⊤ ​ ( M θ − t ) + ​ b ​ 𝑑 θ . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)=2\int_{0}^{1}b^{\top}(M_{\theta}-t)_{+}b\,d\theta. 
 
 
 


 
 Fix θ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \theta\in[0,1] . The matrix M θ M_{\theta} is real symmetric and
positive semidefinite. Hence, by the spectral theorem, there is a real
orthogonal matrix U U and numbers λ i ≥ 0 \lambda_{i}\geq 0 such that 

 

 
 
 M θ = U ​ diag ⁡ ( λ 1 , … , λ m ) ​ U ⊤ . M_{\theta}=U\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{m})U^{\top}. 
 
 
 
 Put c = U ⊤ ​ b c=U^{\top}b . Since U U is orthogonal, we know that b = U ​ c b=Uc , ∑ i c i 2 = ‖ b ‖ 2 2 = d \sum_{i}c_{i}^{2}=\|b\|_{2}^{2}=d , and 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ ( M θ − t ) + ​ b = ∑ i = 1 m c i 2 ​ ( λ i − t ) + . b^{\top}(M_{\theta}-t)_{+}b=\sum_{i=1}^{m}c_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{i}-t)_{+}. 
 
 
 
 The coefficients c i 2 / d c_{i}^{2}/d are non-negative and sum to 1 1 . Applying
Jensen’s inequality to the convex function λ ↦ ( λ − t ) + \lambda\mapsto(\lambda-t)_{+} 
gives 

 

 
 
 1 d ​ b ⊤ ​ ( M θ − t ) + ​ b = ∑ i = 1 m c i 2 d ​ ( λ i − t ) + ≥ ( ∑ i = 1 m c i 2 d ​ λ i − t ) + = ( b ⊤ ​ M θ ​ b d − t ) + . \frac{1}{d}b^{\top}(M_{\theta}-t)_{+}b=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{d}(\lambda_{i}-t)_{+}\geq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{c_{i}^{2}}{d}\lambda_{i}-t\right)_{+}=\left(\frac{b^{\top}M_{\theta}b}{d}-t\right)_{+}. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ M θ ​ b = b ⊤ ​ M ​ b + θ ​ b ⊤ ​ ( b ​ b ⊤ ) ​ b = γ + θ ​ d 2 , b^{\top}M_{\theta}b=b^{\top}Mb+\theta b^{\top}(bb^{\top})b=\gamma+\theta d^{2}, 
 
 
 
 hence 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ ( M θ − t ) + ​ b ≥ d ​ ( γ d + θ ​ d − t ) + . b^{\top}(M_{\theta}-t)_{+}b\geq d\left(\frac{\gamma}{d}+\theta d-t\right)_{+}. 
 
 
 
 Substituting this into the trace formula yields 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ ∫ 0 1 d ​ ( γ d + θ ​ d − t ) + ​ 𝑑 θ . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2\int_{0}^{1}d\left(\frac{\gamma}{d}+\theta d-t\right)_{+}\,d\theta. 
 
 
 
 Finally, with u = γ / d + θ ​ d u=\gamma/d+\theta d , we have d ​ u = d ​ d ​ θ du=d\,d\theta , and therefore 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ ∫ γ / d γ / d + d ( u − t ) + ​ 𝑑 u . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2\int_{\gamma/d}^{\gamma/d+d}(u-t)_{+}\,du. 
 
 
 
 This proves the lemma.
∎ 





 
 We shall use the following elementary lower bound on γ \gamma , obtained by counting the edges incident with N G ​ ( v ) N_{G}(v) after v v is deleted.



 
 Lemma 5.3 . 

 
 Let the notation be as in Lemma  5.2 . Let s s be the number of neighbors x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) x\in N_{G}(v) such that d G − v ​ ( x ) ≥ 1 d_{G-v}(x)\geq 1 . Then 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ ∑ x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) ( d G ​ ( x ) − 1 ) ≥ s . \gamma\geq\sum_{x\in N_{G}(v)}(d_{G}(x)-1)\geq s. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Choose the bipartition with v v on the left side. Then b b is the
 0 0 - 1 1 indicator vector of N G ​ ( v ) N_{G}(v) , indexed by the right-side vertices,
and M = B G − v ⊤ ​ B G − v M=B_{G-v}^{\top}B_{G-v} . Thus 

 

 
 
 γ = b ⊤ ​ B G − v ⊤ ​ B G − v ​ b = ‖ B G − v ​ b ‖ 2 2 . \gamma=b^{\top}B_{G-v}^{\top}B_{G-v}b=\|B_{G-v}b\|_{2}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 For a left-side vertex y y of G − v G-v , the y y -coordinate of
 B G − v ​ b B_{G-v}b is 

 

 
 
 ( B G − v ​ b ) y = ∑ x ( B G − v ) y ​ x ​ b x = | N G − v ​ ( y ) ∩ N G ​ ( v ) | . (B_{G-v}b)_{y}=\sum_{x}(B_{G-v})_{yx}b_{x}=|N_{G-v}(y)\cap N_{G}(v)|. 
 
 
 
 Hence 

 

 
 
 γ = ∑ y | N G − v ​ ( y ) ∩ N G ​ ( v ) | 2 , \gamma=\sum_{y}|N_{G-v}(y)\cap N_{G}(v)|^{2}, 
 
 
 
 where y y runs over the left-side vertices of G − v G-v . Since
 | N G − v ​ ( y ) ∩ N G ​ ( v ) | |N_{G-v}(y)\cap N_{G}(v)| is a non-negative integer, we know that 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ ∑ y | N G − v ​ ( y ) ∩ N G ​ ( v ) | = ∑ x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) d G − v ​ ( x ) = ∑ x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) ( d G ​ ( x ) − 1 ) . \gamma\geq\sum_{y}|N_{G-v}(y)\cap N_{G}(v)|=\sum_{x\in N_{G}(v)}d_{G-v}(x)=\sum_{x\in N_{G}(v)}(d_{G}(x)-1). 
 
 
 
 This proves the first inequality. 



 
 For the second inequality, by the definition of s s , exactly s s vertices
 x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) x\in N_{G}(v) satisfy d G − v ​ ( x ) ≥ 1 d_{G-v}(x)\geq 1 . Therefore 

 

 
 
 ∑ x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) ( d G ​ ( x ) − 1 ) = ∑ x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) d G − v ​ ( x ) ≥ s . \sum_{x\in N_{G}(v)}(d_{G}(x)-1)=\sum_{x\in N_{G}(v)}d_{G-v}(x)\geq s. 
 
 
 
 This completes the proof.
∎ 





 
 For an interval [ a , b ] ⊂ [ 0 , ∞ ) [a,b]\subset[0,\infty) , write

 

 
 
 ℐ [ a , b ] ​ ( t ) := ∫ a b ( u − t ) + ​ 𝑑 u . \mathcal{I}_{[a,b]}(t):=\int_{a}^{b}(u-t)_{+}\,du. 
 
 
 
 For fixed ℓ ≥ 0 \ell\geq 0 and t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , we shall refer to the trivial fact
that ℐ [ a , a + ℓ ] ​ ( t ) \mathcal{I}_{[a,a+\ell]}(t) is non-decreasing as a function of a a as the
 translation monotonicity of ℐ \mathcal{I} ; indeed, this is immediate
from the monotonicity of u ↦ ( u − t ) + u\mapsto(u-t)_{+} .



 
 The next elementary packing estimate says that, among all interval sets of a
fixed total length inside [ 0 , 4 ] [0,4] , the largest value of the stop-loss
integral is obtained by placing the whole set at the right endpoint.



 
 Lemma 5.4 . 

 
 Let E ⊂ [ 0 , 4 ] E\subset[0,4] be a finite disjoint union of intervals, and let
 ℓ = | E | \ell=|E| be its total length. Define 

 

 
 
 J E ​ ( t ) := ∫ E ( y − t ) + ​ 𝑑 y . J_{E}(t):=\int_{E}(y-t)_{+}\,dy. 
 
 
 
 Then, for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 J E ​ ( t ) ≤ ℐ [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . J_{E}(t)\leq\mathcal{I}_{[4-\ell,4]}(t). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Let f t ​ ( y ) = ( y − t ) + f_{t}(y)=(y-t)_{+} . Then f t f_{t} is non-decreasing. Since E E and [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] [4-\ell,4] have the same measure, the two sets 

 

 
 
 A = E ∖ [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] , B = [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] ∖ E A=E\setminus[4-\ell,4],\qquad B=[4-\ell,4]\setminus E 
 
 
 
 also have the same measure. Moreover, A ⊂ [ 0 , 4 − ℓ ] A\subset[0,4-\ell] and B ⊂ [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] B\subset[4-\ell,4] . Therefore 

 

 
 
 ∫ A f t ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 y ≤ | A | ​ f t ​ ( 4 − ℓ ) = | B | f t ​ ( 4 − ℓ ) ≤ ∫ B f t ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 y . \int_{A}f_{t}(y)\,dy\leq|A|f_{t}(4-\ell)=|B|f_{t}(4-\ell)\leq\int_{B}f_{t}(y)\,dy. 
 
 
 
 Adding the common contribution from E ∩ [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] E\cap[4-\ell,4] , we get 

 

 
 
 ∫ E f t ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 y ≤ ∫ [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] f t ​ ( y ) ​ 𝑑 y = ℐ [ 4 − ℓ , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \int_{E}f_{t}(y)\,dy\leq\int_{[4-\ell,4]}f_{t}(y)\,dy=\mathcal{I}_{[4-\ell,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 This is exactly the desired inequality.
∎ 





 
 We shall need two path-cost estimates: one for extending a path by one endpoint,
and one for splicing two paths through a new center.



 
 Lemma 5.5 . 

 
 For every n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 and every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P n − 1 ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n-1})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, for every a , b ≥ 1 a,b\geq 1 and every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P a + b + 1 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P a ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P b ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{a+b+1})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{a})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{b})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 We prove both estimates in the same way. Let G 0 G_{0} be the graph before the
update and G 1 G_{1} the graph after the update. In the first case,
 G 0 = P n − 1 G_{0}=P_{n-1} and G 1 = P n G_{1}=P_{n} . In the second case,
 G 0 = P a ⊔ P b G_{0}=P_{a}\sqcup P_{b} and G 1 = P a + b + 1 G_{1}=P_{a+b+1} . 



 
 Choose the bipartition so that the newly added vertex lies on the left side.
Thus the old right-side vertices remain the column set. Let B 0 B_{0} and
 B 1 B_{1} be the biadjacency matrices before and after the update, with this
common column set. Now we set M 0 := B 0 ⊤ ​ B 0 M_{0}:=B_{0}^{\top}B_{0} and M 1 := B 1 ⊤ ​ B 1 M_{1}:=B_{1}^{\top}B_{1} . Since 

 

 
 
 B 1 = ( B 0 b ⊤ ) , B_{1}=\begin{pmatrix}B_{0}\\
b^{\top}\end{pmatrix}, 
 
 
 
 we have M 1 = M 0 + b ​ b ⊤ M_{1}=M_{0}+bb^{\top} . 



 
 Let 

 

 
 
 α 1 ≥ α 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 , β 1 ≥ β 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 \alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\qquad\beta_{1}\geq\beta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0 
 
 
 
 be the eigenvalues of M 0 M_{0} and M 1 M_{1} , respectively, padded with zeros if
necessary. Since M 1 = M 0 + b ​ b ⊤ M_{1}=M_{0}+bb^{\top} is a rank-one positive semidefinite
perturbation of the Hermitian matrix M 0 M_{0} , the rank-one interlacing theorem
for Hermitian matrices gives 

 

 
 
 β 1 ≥ α 1 ≥ β 2 ≥ α 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 ; \beta_{1}\geq\alpha_{1}\geq\beta_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0; 
 
 
 
 see, for example, [ 12 , Corollary 4.3.9] .
Therefore the intervals [ α i , β i ] [\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}] are pairwise disjoint except possibly for common endpoints. 



 
 Let E := ⋃ i [ α i , β i ] E:=\bigcup_{i}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}] . Since endpoint overlaps have measure zero, we have 

 

 
 
 | E | = ∑ i ( β i − α i ) = tr ⁡ ( M 1 − M 0 ) = tr ⁡ ( b ​ b ⊤ ) = ‖ b ‖ 2 2 . |E|=\sum_{i}(\beta_{i}-\alpha_{i})=\tr(M_{1}-M_{0})=\tr(bb^{\top})=\|b\|_{2}^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Since these eigenvalues are precisely the squared singular
values involved in 𝖲 t \mathsf{S}_{t} , we have 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G 1 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G 0 ) \displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{t}(G_{1})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G_{0}) 
 = ∑ i ( ( β i − t ) + 2 − ( α i − t ) + 2 ) \displaystyle=\sum_{i}\Bigl((\beta_{i}-t)_{+}^{2}-(\alpha_{i}-t)_{+}^{2}\Bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 ​ ∑ i ∫ α i β i ( y − t ) + ​ d y = 2 ​ ∫ E ( y − t ) + ​ d y = 2 ​ J E ​ ( t ) . \displaystyle=2\sum_{i}\int_{\alpha_{i}}^{\beta_{i}}(y-t)_{+}\,dy=2\int_{E}(y-t)_{+}\,dy=2J_{E}(t). 
 
 
 
 All graphs appearing in the two updates are paths or disjoint unions of paths.
Hence all their squared singular values lie in [ 0 , 4 ] [0,4] , and so
 E ⊂ [ 0 , 4 ] E\subset[0,4] . 



 
 For the endpoint update P n − 1 ↝ P n P_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow P_{n} , the vector b b has
exactly one nonzero entry. Thus ‖ b ‖ 2 2 = 1 \|b\|_{2}^{2}=1 . By
Lemma  5.4 , we know that J E ​ ( t ) ≤ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) J_{E}(t)\leq\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t) . This proves 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P n − 1 ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n-1})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 


 
 For the two-arm update P a ⊔ P b ↝ P a + b + 1 P_{a}\sqcup P_{b}\rightsquigarrow P_{a+b+1} , we choose the two endpoints to be on the right side and insert a new left-side center adjacent to both of them. Then b b has exactly two nonzero entries, so ‖ b ‖ 2 2 = 2 \|b\|_{2}^{2}=2 . Again by Lemma  5.4 , we know that J E ​ ( t ) ≤ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) J_{E}(t)\leq\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t) , which proves 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P a + b + 1 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P a ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P b ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . ∎ \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{a+b+1})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{a})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{b})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t).\qed 
 
 
 




 
 Repeated applications of the preceding path-cost estimates give the following
two bounds.



 
 Corollary 5.6 . 

 
 For r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 , m 1 , … , m r ≥ 1 m_{1},\ldots,m_{r}\geq 1 , and t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P m 1 + ⋯ + m r ) − ∑ i = 1 r 𝖲 t ​ ( P m i ) ≤ 2 ​ ( r − 1 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{i}})\leq 2(r-1)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 If r ≥ 2 r\geq 2 , then 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 + m 1 + ⋯ + m r ) − ∑ i = 1 r 𝖲 t ​ ( P m i ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + 2 ​ ( r − 2 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{i}})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+2(r-2)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 We first prove the first estimate by induction on r r . The case r = 1 r=1 is
trivial. Put M = m 1 + ⋯ + m r − 1 M=m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r-1} . For r ≥ 2 r\geq 2 , Theorem  4.14 
gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P M + m r ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P M ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P m r ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{M+m_{r}})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{M})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{r}})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Adding the induction hypothesis applied to m 1 , … , m r − 1 m_{1},\ldots,m_{r-1} gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P m 1 + ⋯ + m r ) − ∑ i = 1 r 𝖲 t ​ ( P m i ) ≤ 2 ​ ( r − 1 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{i}})\leq 2(r-1)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 


 
 For the second estimate, first insert one new center between P m 1 P_{m_{1}} and P m 2 P_{m_{2}} . By Lemma  5.5 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 + m 1 + m 2 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P m 1 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P m 2 ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1+m_{1}+m_{2}})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{1}})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{2}})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 For each k = 3 , … , r k=3,\ldots,r , Theorem  4.14 gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 + m 1 + ⋯ + m k ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 + m 1 + ⋯ + m k − 1 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P m k ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k}})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{k-1}})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{k}})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Summing these inequalities for k = 3 , … , r k=3,\ldots,r together with the first-center estimate proves 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 + m 1 + ⋯ + m r ) − ∑ i = 1 r 𝖲 t ​ ( P m i ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + 2 ​ ( r − 2 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . ∎ \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{r}})-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{m_{i}})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+2(r-2)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t).\qed 
 
 
 




 
 We shall also need the following elementary interval domination estimates. They are used to compare the rank-one local gain with the path costs above.



 
 Lemma 5.7 . 

 
 Let d , r d,r be integers with d ≥ r + 2 d\geq r+2 and r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 . Then, for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 

 

 
 
 ℐ [ ( d − r + 1 ) / d , ( d − r + 1 ) / d + d ] ​ ( t ) ≥ r ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathcal{I}_{[(d-r+1)/d,(d-r+1)/d+d]}(t)\geq r\,\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 In the borderline case d = r + 2 d=r+2 , one has the sharper estimate 

 

 
 
 ℐ [ 3 / d , d + 3 / d ] ​ ( t ) ≥ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + ( d − 3 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathcal{I}_{[3/d,d+3/d]}(t)\geq\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+(d-3)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 The proof is elementary but slightly lengthy, and is deferred to
Appendix  B .
∎ 





 
 The next lemma is the deletion criterion used in the minimal-counterexample
argument. It says that a vertex whose degree is sufficiently large compared
with the number of components created by its deletion can be removed without
losing the desired stop-loss comparison. The borderline case d = q + 1 d=q+1 is
handled by a two-arm path-cost estimate. A non-isolated component means a component that is not a single isolated vertex.



 
 Lemma 5.8 . 

 
 Let G G be connected and bipartite, let v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) v\in V(G) , and put d = d G ​ ( v ) d=d_{G}(v) . Let G 1 , … , G q G_{1},\ldots,G_{q} be the connected components of G − v G-v . Assume that 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G i ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P | V ⁡ ( G i ) | ) ( i = 1 , … , q , t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G_{i})\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{|V(G_{i})|})\qquad(i=1,\ldots,q;\ t\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 If d ≥ q + 2 d\geq q+2 , then 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P | V ⁡ ( G ) | ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{|V(G)|})\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, if d = q + 1 d=q+1 , if G − v G-v has at least two non-isolated components,
and if one component of G − v G-v contains at least two neighbors of v v , then 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P | V ⁡ ( G ) | ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{|V(G)|})\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Choose the bipartition so that v v lies on the left side, and let
 M , b , γ M,b,\gamma be as in Lemma  5.2 . Put 

 

 
 
 n i = | V ⁡ ( G i ) | , n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | = 1 + ∑ i = 1 q n i . n_{i}=|V(G_{i})|,\qquad n=|V(G)|=1+\sum_{i=1}^{q}n_{i}. 
 
 
 


 
 We first prove the assertion under the hypothesis d ≥ q + 2 d\geq q+2 . Let s s be the number of neighbors x ∈ N G ​ ( v ) x\in N_{G}(v) such that d G − v ​ ( x ) ≥ 1 d_{G-v}(x)\geq 1 . 



 
 The remaining d − s d-s neighbors of v v are isolated vertices in G − v G-v , and
hence account for d − s d-s isolated components. Since G G is connected, every
component of G − v G-v contains at least one neighbor of v v . If all components
of G − v G-v were isolated, then q = d q=d , contradicting d ≥ q + 2 d\geq q+2 . Thus
 G − v G-v has at least one non-isolated component, and so q ≥ ( d − s ) + 1 q\geq(d-s)+1 . Hence s ≥ d − q + 1 s\geq d-q+1 . By Lemma  5.3 , 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ s ≥ d − q + 1 . \gamma\geq s\geq d-q+1. 
 
 
 
 Therefore γ / d ≥ ( d − q + 1 ) / d \gamma/d\geq(d-q+1)/d . Lemma  5.2 and translation monotonicity give 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ γ / d , γ / d + d ] ​ ( t ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ ( d − q + 1 ) / d , ( d − q + 1 ) / d + d ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[\gamma/d,\gamma/d+d]}(t)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[(d-q+1)/d,(d-q+1)/d+d]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Applying Lemma  5.7 with r = q r=q , we get 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ q ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2q\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 On the other hand, Corollary  5.6 , applied to the q + 1 q+1 paths P 1 , P n 1 , … , P n q P_{1},P_{n_{1}},\ldots,P_{n_{q}} , gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 ) − ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) ≤ 2 ​ q ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}})\leq 2q\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Since 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 ) = 0 \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1})=0 , it follows that 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ q ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2q\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}}). 
 
 
 
 Using additivity over the components of G − v G-v and the component hypotheses, 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) = ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( G i ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)=\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(G_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}}). 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) \displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{t}(G) 
 = 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) + ( 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ) \displaystyle=\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)+\bigl(\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\bigr) 
 
 
 
 
 
 ≥ ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) + ( 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) ) \displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}})+\left(\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}})\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) . \displaystyle=\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 
 This proves the first assertion. 



 
 We now prove the borderline assertion. Assume that d = q + 1 d=q+1 , that G − v G-v 
has at least two non-isolated components, and that one component of G − v G-v 
contains at least two neighbors of v v . Since G G is connected, every
non-isolated component of G − v G-v contains at least one neighbor of v v .
Therefore the number s s of neighbors of v v that remain non-isolated in
 G − v G-v satisfies 

 

 
 
 s ≥ 3 . s\geq 3. 
 
 
 
 Lemma  5.3 gives γ ≥ 3 \gamma\geq 3 . Hence, by
Lemma  5.2 and translation monotonicity, 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ γ / d , γ / d + d ] ​ ( t ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 / d , d + 3 / d ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[\gamma/d,\gamma/d+d]}(t)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3/d,d+3/d]}(t). 
 
 
 
 The sharper part of Lemma  5.7 gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − v ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + 2 ​ ( d − 3 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-v)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+2(d-3)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Since q = d − 1 q=d-1 , Corollary  5.6 gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) \displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}}) 
 ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + 2 ​ ( q − 2 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) \displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+2(q-2)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + 2 ​ ( d − 3 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \displaystyle=2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+2(d-3)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Thus the local gain again dominates the path deficit. Combining this with ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( G i ) ≥ ∑ i = 1 q 𝖲 t ​ ( P n i ) \sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(G_{i})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{q}\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n_{i}}) proves 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}) . This proves the borderline assertion and completes the proof.
∎ 





 
 We use the following terminology. In this paper, a sun graph means a
graph obtained from an even cycle by attaching any finite number of pendant
leaves to its cycle vertices. A cycle vertex is called loaded if at
least one pendant leaf is attached to it. Such a sun graph is called
 sparse if each cycle vertex supports at most one pendant leaf. In a
sparse sun graph, the loaded cycle vertices are called 3 3 -separated 
if their pairwise cyclic distances along the base cycle are at least 3 3 .



 
 The next proposition reduces a minimal counterexample to the terminal sparse-sun configuration.



 
 Proposition 5.9 . 

 
 Let G G be a connected bipartite graph of minimum order among all connected
bipartite graphs for which there exists t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 such that 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) < 𝖲 t ​ ( P | V ⁡ ( G ) | ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)<\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{|V(G)|}). 
 
 
 
 Then G G is a sparse sun graph whose loaded cycle vertices are
 3 3 -separated. 





 
 Proof.

 
 By minimality, every connected bipartite graph of order strictly smaller than
 | V ⁡ ( G ) | |V(G)| satisfies the desired comparison for all t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 . In particular,
every connected component arising after deleting a vertex of G G satisfies
the comparison. Therefore, whenever one of the local hypotheses in
Lemma  5.8 is met, the corresponding deletion criterion may
be applied. 



 
 By Corollary  4.12 , G G is not a tree. Since G G is connected, it therefore contains a cycle. A cycle is 2 2 -connected, and hence it is contained in a maximal 2 2 -connected subgraph, that is, in a 2 2 -connected block of G G . 



 
 Here and below, κ ⁡ ( H ) \kappa(H) denotes the number of connected components of a
graph H H . Clearly, every vertex satisfies 

 

 
 
 d G ​ ( v ) − κ ⁡ ( G − v ) ≤ 1 . d_{G}(v)-\kappa(G-v)\leq 1. 
 
 
 
 Indeed, if some vertex v v satisfied d G ​ ( v ) ≥ κ ⁡ ( G − v ) + 2 d_{G}(v)\geq\kappa(G-v)+2 , then the
first assertion of Lemma  5.8 , together with the
minimality of G G , would prove the comparison for G G at all t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 ,
contradicting the choice of G G . 



 
 Let B B be a 2 2 -connected block of G G . If v ∈ V ⁡ ( B ) v\in V(B) , then
 B − v B-v is connected. Let r v r_{v} be the number of neighbors of v v outside
 B B , and let c v c_{v} be the number of components of G − v G-v which do not meet
 B − v B-v . Each such component contains at least one neighbor of v v outside
 B B , so c v ≤ r v c_{v}\leq r_{v} . Since B − v B-v is connected, 

 

 
 
 κ ⁡ ( G − v ) ≤ 1 + c v , d G ​ ( v ) = d B ​ ( v ) + r v . \kappa(G-v)\leq 1+c_{v},\qquad d_{G}(v)=d_{B}(v)+r_{v}. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 d G ​ ( v ) − κ ⁡ ( G − v ) ≥ d B ​ ( v ) + r v − ( 1 + c v ) ≥ d B ​ ( v ) − 1 . d_{G}(v)-\kappa(G-v)\geq d_{B}(v)+r_{v}-(1+c_{v})\geq d_{B}(v)-1. 
 
 
 
 Together with d G ​ ( v ) − κ ⁡ ( G − v ) ≤ 1 d_{G}(v)-\kappa(G-v)\leq 1 , this gives d B ​ ( v ) ≤ 2 d_{B}(v)\leq 2 for every v ∈ V ⁡ ( B ) v\in V(B) . Since B B is 2 2 -connected, every vertex of B B has degree at least 2 2 inside B B , and therefore B B must be a cycle. Thus every 2 2 -connected block of G G is a cycle, so G G is a cactus. We now fix a cycle block C C of G G . Using the structure of cacti, after deleting all edges of C C , every connected component of G − E ⁡ ( C ) G-E(C) contains exactly one vertex of C C . 



 
 We next show that G G is a sun graph. Fix a cycle block C C of G G . 



 
 We claim first that, for every v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) v\in V(C) , the graph G − v G-v contains at most one non-isolated component. Suppose, to the contrary, that for some v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) v\in V(C) , the graph G − v G-v contains at least two non-isolated components. The component containing C − v C-v contains the two cycle neighbors of v v , and therefore one component of G − v G-v contains at least two neighbors of v v . Since G G is connected, every other component of G − v G-v contains at least one neighbor of v v . Hence 

 

 
 
 d G ​ ( v ) ≥ κ ⁡ ( G − v ) + 1 . d_{G}(v)\geq\kappa(G-v)+1. 
 
 
 
 Combining this with d G ​ ( v ) − κ ⁡ ( G − v ) ≤ 1 d_{G}(v)-\kappa(G-v)\leq 1 , we get 

 

 
 
 d G ​ ( v ) = κ ⁡ ( G − v ) + 1 . d_{G}(v)=\kappa(G-v)+1.
