 Thus the borderline part of Lemma  5.8 applies to v v , because G − v G-v has at least two non-isolated components and one of its components contains at least two neighbors of v v . This would make v v deletable, contradicting the minimality of G G . The claim follows. 



 
 Now fix v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) v\in V(C) , and let D v D_{v} be the connected component of
 G − E ⁡ ( C ) G-E(C) that contains v v . Since G G is a cactus, every component of
 G − E ⁡ ( C ) G-E(C) contains exactly one vertex of C C ; in particular, 

 

 
 
 V ⁡ ( D v ) ∩ V ⁡ ( C ) = { v } . V(D_{v})\cap V(C)=\{v\}. 
 
 
 
 We show that D v D_{v} is a star centered at v v . No vertex of D v − v D_{v}-v lies
in the same component of G − v G-v as C − v C-v . Indeed, if such a path existed in
 G − v G-v , then by taking the first vertex of C − v C-v reached by the path, we
would obtain in G − E ⁡ ( C ) G-E(C) a path from D v D_{v} to a second vertex of C C ,
contradicting V ⁡ ( D v ) ∩ V ⁡ ( C ) = { v } V(D_{v})\cap V(C)=\{v\} . 



 
 The component of G − v G-v containing C − v C-v is non-isolated. By the claim,
 G − v G-v has at most one non-isolated component. Therefore every component of
 D v − v D_{v}-v is isolated. Since D v D_{v} is connected, every vertex of D v − v D_{v}-v 
is adjacent only to v v . Hence D v D_{v} is a star centered at v v , with
possibly no leaves. 



 
 This holds for every v ∈ V ⁡ ( C ) v\in V(C) . Since every component of G − E ⁡ ( C ) G-E(C) contains exactly one vertex of C C , the graph G G is obtained from the cycle C C by attaching pendant leaves to cycle vertices. The cycle C C is even because G G is bipartite. Hence G G is a sun graph. 



 
 We now strengthen this to sparsity. Suppose that some cycle vertex v v 
supports at least two pendant leaves. Delete one such leaf ℓ \ell , and put
 H = G − ℓ H=G-\ell . Then H H is connected. When ℓ \ell is added back to H H ,
the added vertex has degree 1 1 . Choose the bipartition so that ℓ \ell 
lies on the left side. In the notation of Lemma  5.2 , we
have b = 𝟏 { v } b=\mathbf{1}_{\{v\}} , and Lemma  5.3 gives 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ ∑ w ∈ N G ​ ( ℓ ) ( d G ​ ( w ) − 1 ) = d G ​ ( v ) − 1 ≥ 3 , \gamma\geq\sum_{w\in N_{G}(\ell)}(d_{G}(w)-1)=d_{G}(v)-1\geq 3, 
 
 
 
 because v v is incident with its two cycle edges and with at least two pendant leaves. Hence Lemma  5.2 and translation monotonicity give 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ γ , γ + 1 ] ​ ( t ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[\gamma,\gamma+1]}(t)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 By minimality, H H satisfies the comparison with P n − 1 P_{n-1} . On the other
hand, Lemma  5.5 gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P n − 1 ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n-1})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Thus adding ℓ \ell back gives at least as much gain as the endpoint cost of
passing from P n − 1 P_{n-1} to P n P_{n} . Hence G G would satisfy the desired
comparison, contradicting the minimality of G G . Therefore G G is sparse. 



 
 It remains to prove that the loaded cycle vertices are 3 3 -separated. Since
 G G is now known to be a sparse sun graph, it suffices to exclude the cyclic
patterns 11 11 and 101 101 , where 1 1 denotes a loaded cycle vertex and
 0 0 denotes an unloaded cycle vertex. 



 
 First suppose that two adjacent cycle vertices x x and y y are loaded.
Since G G is sparse, x x supports exactly one pendant leaf; call it
 ℓ x \ell_{x} . Delete x x . Then 

 

 
 
 G − x = K ⊔ { ℓ x } , G-x=K\sqcup\{\ell_{x}\}, 
 
 
 
 where K K is connected. Choose the bipartition so that x x lies on the
left side, and let z z be the other cycle neighbor of x x . By
Lemma  5.3 , 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ ∑ w ∈ N G ​ ( x ) ( d G ​ ( w ) − 1 ) . \gamma\geq\sum_{w\in N_{G}(x)}(d_{G}(w)-1). 
 
 
 
 The pendant leaf ℓ x \ell_{x} contributes d G ​ ( ℓ x ) − 1 = 0 d_{G}(\ell_{x})-1=0 . Since y y is
loaded and G G is sparse, d G ​ ( y ) = 3 d_{G}(y)=3 , while d G ​ ( z ) ≥ 2 d_{G}(z)\geq 2 . Hence 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ ( d G ​ ( y ) − 1 ) + ( d G ​ ( z ) − 1 ) ≥ 2 + 1 = 3 . \gamma\geq(d_{G}(y)-1)+(d_{G}(z)-1)\geq 2+1=3. 
 
 
 
 Also d G ​ ( x ) = 3 d_{G}(x)=3 . Therefore Lemma  5.2 and translation
monotonicity give 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( G − x ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ γ / 3 , γ / 3 + 3 ] ​ ( t ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ 1 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(G-x)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[\gamma/3,\gamma/3+3]}(t)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[1,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 By minimality, K K satisfies the path comparison, and
 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 ) = 0 \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1})=0 . Moreover, Lemma  5.5 gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P | V ⁡ ( K ) | ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P 1 ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 1 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{|V(K)|})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{1})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[1,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Thus the local gain from adding x x back dominates the corresponding path
deficit. This would make x x deletable, contradicting the minimality of
 G G . Hence the pattern 11 11 cannot occur. 



 
 Next suppose that the pattern 101 101 occurs. Thus there are three consecutive
cycle vertices x , u , z x,u,z , where x x and z z are loaded and u u is
unloaded. Delete u u , and put H = G − u H=G-u . Then H H is connected. Choose
the bipartition so that u u lies on the left side. By
Lemma  5.3 , 

 

 
 
 γ ≥ ∑ w ∈ N G ​ ( u ) ( d G ​ ( w ) − 1 ) = ( d G ​ ( x ) − 1 ) + ( d G ​ ( z ) − 1 ) = 2 + 2 = 4 , \gamma\geq\sum_{w\in N_{G}(u)}(d_{G}(w)-1)=(d_{G}(x)-1)+(d_{G}(z)-1)=2+2=4, 
 
 
 
 because x x and z z are loaded in a sparse sun graph. Since u u is
unloaded, d G ​ ( u ) = 2 d_{G}(u)=2 . Hence Lemma  5.2 and translation
monotonicity give 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ γ / 2 , γ / 2 + 2 ] ​ ( t ) ≥ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[\gamma/2,\gamma/2+2]}(t)\geq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 By minimality, H H satisfies the path comparison with P n − 1 P_{n-1} . Meanwhile
Lemma  5.5 gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P n − 1 ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) ≤ 2 ​ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n-1})\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t)\leq 2\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Thus u u would be deletable, contradicting the minimality of G G . Hence
the pattern 101 101 cannot occur. 



 
 Therefore no two loaded cycle vertices are at cyclic distance 1 1 or 2 2 .
Equivalently, the loaded cycle vertices are 3 3 -separated.
∎ 







 
 6. The terminal 3 3 -separated sparse-sun case


 
 This section treats the terminal case left by Proposition  5.9 . Thus G G is a 3 3 -separated sparse sun graph with at least one pendant leaf. We shall delete an unloaded cycle neighbor of a loaded cycle vertex, rather than deleting the pendant leaf itself. The goal is to show that the rank-one spectral shift produced by adding this vertex back dominates the endpoint increment P n − 1 ↝ P n P_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow P_{n} .



 
 The proof is reduced to an envelope comparison on 0 ≤ t ≤ 4 0\leq t\leq 4 ; the finite one-variable inequalities used in that comparison are verified in Appendix  C . For t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 , the path-endpoint contribution vanishes by spectral support.



 
 6.1. Spectral-shift notation


 
 Recall the spectral-shift interval notation from the proof of Lemma  5.5 . Let M ⪰ 0 M\succeq 0 and b ≠ 0 b\neq 0 , and consider the
positive rank-one update

 

 
 
 M ↝ M + b ​ b ⊤ . M\rightsquigarrow M+bb^{\top}. 
 
 
 
 Let

 

 
 
 α 1 ≥ α 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 , β 1 ≥ β 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 \alpha_{1}\geq\alpha_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0,\qquad\beta_{1}\geq\beta_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0 
 
 
 
 be the eigenvalues of M M and M + b ​ b ⊤ M+bb^{\top} , respectively, with zero padding
if necessary. By the rank-one interlacing theorem, we know that

 

 
 
 β 1 ≥ α 1 ≥ β 2 ≥ α 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 . \beta_{1}\geq\alpha_{1}\geq\beta_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 We define the associated spectral-shift interval set by

 

 
 
 E ⁡ ( M , b ) := ⋃ i [ α i , β i ] , E(M,b):=\bigcup_{i}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}], 
 
 
 
 where zero-length intervals and endpoint overlaps are harmless. Thus
 E ⁡ ( M , b ) E(M,b) records the interlacing gaps created by the positive rank-one
update.



 
 For a finite interval set E ⊂ [ 0 , ∞ ) E\subset[0,\infty) , recall that

 

 
 
 J E ​ ( t ) = ∫ E ( y − t ) + ​ 𝑑 y , t ≥ 0 . J_{E}(t)=\int_{E}(y-t)_{+}\,dy,\qquad t\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 When E = E ⁡ ( M , b ) E=E(M,b) , this quantity is half of the increase of the second-order stop-loss functional:

 

 
 
 tr ⁡ ( M + b ​ b ⊤ − t ​ I ) + 2 − tr ⁡ ( M − t ​ I ) + 2 = 2 ​ J E ⁡ ( M , b ) ​ ( t ) . \tr(M+bb^{\top}-tI)_{+}^{2}-\tr(M-tI)_{+}^{2}=2J_{E(M,b)}(t). 
 
 
 
 If M θ = M + θ ​ b ​ b ⊤ M_{\theta}=M+\theta bb^{\top} , then combining this identity with Lemma  5.1 gives

 

 
 (24) 
 
 J E ⁡ ( M , b ) ​ ( t ) = ∫ 0 1 b ⊤ ​ ( M θ − t ) + ​ b ​ 𝑑 θ . J_{E(M,b)}(t)=\int_{0}^{1}b^{\top}(M_{\theta}-t)_{+}b\,d\theta. 
 
 
 


 
 For the path endpoint increment P n − 1 ↝ P n P_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow P_{n} , label the
vertices as 1 , … , n 1,\ldots,n , so that P n − 1 P_{n-1} is the path
 1 − ⋯ − ( n − 1 ) 1-\cdots-(n-1) and the new edge is ( n − 1 ) ​ n (n-1)n . Choose the bipartition so
that the new endpoint n n lies on the left side; then the old endpoint
 n − 1 n-1 lies on the right side. Let B P n − 1 B_{P_{n-1}} be the biadjacency matrix
of P n − 1 P_{n-1} with this bipartition, and put

 

 
 
 M 0 := B P n − 1 ⊤ ​ B P n − 1 . M_{0}:=B_{P_{n-1}}^{\top}B_{P_{n-1}}. 
 
 
 
 Let e e be the standard basis vector corresponding to the right-side vertex
 n − 1 n-1 . Adding the new endpoint appends the row e ⊤ e^{\top} to the
biadjacency matrix, and therefore

 

 
 
 B P n ⊤ ​ B P n = M 0 + e ​ e ⊤ . B_{P_{n}}^{\top}B_{P_{n}}=M_{0}+ee^{\top}. 
 
 
 
 We write

 

 
 
 E n P := E ⁡ ( M 0 , e ) E_{n}^{P}:=E(M_{0},e) 
 
 
 
 for the corresponding spectral-shift interval set.





 
 6.2. The support-neighbor configuration


 
 Let G G be a 3 3 -separated sparse sun graph with at least one pendant leaf.
Choose a loaded cycle vertex a a . Since the loaded cycle vertices are
 3 3 -separated, both cycle neighbors of a a are unloaded. Choose one of
them and call it u u . Let c c be the other cycle neighbor of u u , and put

 

 
 
 H = G − u , n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | . H=G-u,\qquad n=|V(G)|. 
 
 
 
 Deleting u u breaks the base cycle, while all pendant leaves remain attached
to the resulting path. Hence H H is a tree.



 
 Choose the bipartition so that u u lies on the left side; then a a and
 c c lie on the right side. Let B H B_{H} be the biadjacency matrix of H H ,
with columns indexed by the right side. Let e a e_{a} and e c e_{c} denote the
standard basis vectors corresponding to the right-side vertices a a and
 c c , and set

 

 
 
 M = B H ⊤ B H , b = e a + e c , M θ = M + θ b b ⊤ ( 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 ) . M=B_{H}^{\top}B_{H},\qquad b=e_{a}+e_{c},\qquad M_{\theta}=M+\theta bb^{\top}\quad(0\leq\theta\leq 1). 
 
 
 
 Adding u u back to H H appends to the biadjacency matrix one new row corresponding to u u . This row has nonzero entries precisely in the columns a a and c c . Therefore

 

 
 
 B G ⊤ ​ B G = M + b ​ b ⊤ . B_{G}^{\top}B_{G}=M+bb^{\top}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the rank-one update M ↝ M + b ​ b ⊤ M\rightsquigarrow M+bb^{\top} is exactly the
squared-singular-value update from H H to G G . Define

 

 
 
 E G , u := E ⁡ ( M , b ) . E_{G,u}:=E(M,b). 
 
 
 


 
 The next result is the final deletion step for the terminal sparse-sun case:
it shows that adding back the unloaded neighbor u u gives at least as much
second-order stop-loss gain as adding an endpoint to the path.



 
 Theorem 6.1 . 

 
 With the notation above, 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( t ) ≥ J E n P ​ ( t ) for every  ​ t ≥ 0 . J_{E_{G,u}}(t)\geq J_{E_{n}^{P}}(t)\qquad\text{for every }t\geq 0. 
 
 
 






 
 6.3. Local data for the support-neighbor update


 
 The next lemma records the local first moment, a fifth-moment lower bound, and
the spectral support needed for the envelope comparison.



 
 Lemma 6.2 . 

 
 Assume that G G is a 3 3 -separated sparse sun graph whose base cycle has length at least 6 6 . Then, for every
 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 , 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ M θ ​ b = 3 + 4 ​ θ , b^{\top}M_{\theta}b=3+4\theta, 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ M θ 5 ​ b ≥ q 5 ​ ( θ ) := 64 ​ θ 5 + 240 ​ θ 4 + 440 ​ θ 3 + 507 ​ θ 2 + 387 ​ θ + 174 . b^{\top}M_{\theta}^{5}b\geq q_{5}(\theta):=64\theta^{5}+240\theta^{4}+440\theta^{3}+507\theta^{2}+387\theta+174. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, 

 

 
 
 spec ⁡ ( M θ ) ⊂ [ 0 , 5 ] . \spec(M_{\theta})\subset[0,5]. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 We first compute b ⊤ ​ M θ ​ b b^{\top}M_{\theta}b . Since b = e a + e c b=e_{a}+e_{c} , we have ‖ b ‖ 2 = 2 \|b\|^{2}=2 , and hence 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ M θ ​ b = b ⊤ ​ M ​ b + θ ​ ‖ b ‖ 4 = b ⊤ ​ M ​ b + 4 ​ θ . b^{\top}M_{\theta}b=b^{\top}Mb+\theta\|b\|^{4}=b^{\top}Mb+4\theta. 
 
 
 
 It remains to compute b ⊤ ​ M ​ b = ‖ B H ​ b ‖ 2 2 b^{\top}Mb=\|B_{H}b\|_{2}^{2} . In H H , the vertex a a has two remaining left neighbors, namely its remaining cycle neighbor and its pendant leaf. The vertex c c is unloaded by 3 3 -separation, and after deleting u u it has only one remaining left neighbor. Since the cycle has length at least 6 6 , a a and c c have no common left neighbor in H H . Thus 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ M ​ b = ‖ B H ​ b ‖ 2 2 = 2 + 1 = 3 , b^{\top}Mb=\|B_{H}b\|_{2}^{2}=2+1=3, 
 
 
 
 and the claimed identity follows. 



 
 Let H 0 H_{0} be the subgraph of H H induced by the unique a a - c c path in H H , together with the pendant leaf attached to a a , and with all other pendant leaves removed.
Let B H 0 B_{H_{0}} be its biadjacency matrix, with rows indexed by the left side
and columns indexed by the right side, in the same convention as for B H B_{H} .
Let N N be the number of right-side vertices of H 0 H_{0} , ordered so that
 a a is the first vertex and c c is the last vertex. The Gram matrix on
the right side is therefore 

 

 
 
 T N := B H 0 ⊤ ​ B H 0 . T_{N}:=B_{H_{0}}^{\top}B_{H_{0}}. 
 
 
 
 If r 1 = a , r 2 , … , r N = c r_{1}=a,r_{2},\ldots,r_{N}=c are the right-side vertices in this order, then ( T N ) i ​ j = | N H 0 ​ ( r i ) ∩ N H 0 ​ ( r j ) | (T_{N})_{ij}=|N_{H_{0}}(r_{i})\cap N_{H_{0}}(r_{j})| . Hence the diagonal entries record the right-side degrees, so they are 2 , 2 , … , 2 , 1 2,2,\ldots,2,1 : the vertex a a has one path neighbor and its pendant leaf, each internal right-side path vertex has two path neighbors, and c c has only one remaining path neighbor after u u is deleted. Moreover, two consecutive right-side vertices along H 0 H_{0} have exactly one common left neighbor, while non-consecutive right-side vertices have no common left neighbor. Thus 

 

 
 
 T N = ( 2 1 0 ⋯ 0 1 2 1 ⋱ 0 1 2 ⋱ 0 ⋱ ⋱ ⋱ 1 0 ⋯ 0 1 1 ) . T_{N}=\begin{pmatrix}2&1&0&\cdots&0\\
1&2&1&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&1&2&\ddots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&1\\
0&\cdots&0&1&1\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 Since a = r 1 a=r_{1} and c = r N c=r_{N} , the vector corresponding to e a + e c e_{a}+e_{c} in this reduced model is b N = e 1 + e N b_{N}=e_{1}+e_{N} . Put the rank-one interpolation as 

 

 
 
 M N , θ := T N + θ ​ b N ​ b N ⊤ . M_{N,\theta}:=T_{N}+\theta b_{N}b_{N}^{\top}. 
 
 
 


 
 We first show that the reduced local model has its smallest fifth moment at
the stable value N = 7 N=7 : 

 

 
 (25) 
 
 b N ⊤ M N , θ 5 b N ≥ b 7 ⊤ M 7 , θ 5 b 7 ( N ≥ 3 ,  0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 ) . b_{N}^{\top}M_{N,\theta}^{5}b_{N}\geq b_{7}^{\top}M_{7,\theta}^{5}b_{7}\qquad(N\geq 3,\;0\leq\theta\leq 1). 
 
 
 
 Put 

 

 
 
 P N := b N b N ⊤ , s N ( r ) := b N ⊤ T N r b N ( r ≥ 0 ) . P_{N}:=b_{N}b_{N}^{\top},\qquad s_{N}(r):=b_{N}^{\top}T_{N}^{r}b_{N}\qquad(r\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 We first prove that 

 

 
 (26) 
 
 s N ( r ) ≥ s 7 ( r ) ( N ≥ 3 ,  0 ≤ r ≤ 5 ) . s_{N}(r)\geq s_{7}(r)\qquad(N\geq 3,\;0\leq r\leq 5). 
 
 
 


 
 View T N T_{N} as the adjacency matrix of a looped colored path on
 { 1 , … , N } \{1,\ldots,N\} : there is one edge between i i and i + 1 i+1 , each of the
vertices 1 , … , N − 1 1,\ldots,N-1 carries two loop-colors, and the endpoint N N 
carries one loop-color. With this convention, ( T N r ) i ​ j (T_{N}^{r})_{ij} counts
colored walks of length r r from i i to j j . Since
 b N = e 1 + e N b_{N}=e_{1}+e_{N} , the quantity s N ​ ( r ) s_{N}(r) counts colored walks of length r r 
whose initial and terminal vertices lie in the endpoint set { 1 , N } \{1,N\} . 



 
 We construct an injection from the colored walks counted by s 7 ​ ( r ) s_{7}(r) to
those counted by s N ​ ( r ) s_{N}(r) , for every N ≥ 3 N\geq 3 and 0 ≤ r ≤ 5 0\leq r\leq 5 . In the
 N = 7 N=7 model, the two endpoints are at distance 6 6 . Hence a walk of
length at most 5 5 cannot visit both endpoints. Every endpoint walk counted
by s 7 ​ ( r ) s_{7}(r) is therefore either a return walk at 1 1 or a return walk at
 7 7 . 



 
 Assume first that N ≥ 4 N\geq 4 . A return walk of length r ≤ 5 r\leq 5 at an endpoint can reach distance at most 2 2 from that endpoint. Thus a return walk at 1 1 in the N = 7 N=7 model only uses the vertices 1 , 2 , 3 1,2,3 . These vertices have the same loop-colors and edge structure in the N N -model, so
we map such a walk identically to a return walk at 1 1 . Similarly, a return
walk at 7 7 only uses the vertices 7 , 6 , 5 7,6,5 , and we translate these vertices
to N , N − 1 , N − 2 N,N-1,N-2 . This preserves all edge steps and loop-colors. The image
of a left-end return walk starts at 1 1 , while the image of a right-end
return walk starts at N N ; since N ≥ 4 N\geq 4 , these image families are
disjoint. Hence s N ​ ( r ) ≥ s 7 ​ ( r ) s_{N}(r)\geq s_{7}(r) for N ≥ 4 N\geq 4 . 



 
 It remains to handle N = 3 N=3 . The right-end return walks in the N = 7 N=7 
model translate exactly to right-end return walks in the N = 3 N=3 model by
sending 7 , 6 , 5 7,6,5 to 3 , 2 , 1 3,2,1 . For the left-end return walks, the identity
map works except for one missing colored walk. This can occur only when
 r = 5 r=5 : the walk reaches vertex 3 3 , uses the loop-color at vertex 3 3 
which is absent in the N = 3 N=3 model, and then returns: 

 

 
 
 1 → 2 → 3 → 3 → 2 → 1 . 1\to 2\to 3\to 3\to 2\to 1. 
 
 
 
 We map this single missing walk instead to the endpoint-crossing walk 

 

 
 
 1 → 2 → 3 → 3 → 3 → 3 . 1\to 2\to 3\to 3\to 3\to 3. 
 
 
 
 This image is not used by any left-end return walk, since it ends at 3 3 , and
it is not used by any right-end return walk, since it starts at 1 1 . Thus
the injection also exists for N = 3 N=3 , proving ( 26 ). 



 
 We now expand the noncommutative product ( T N + θ ​ P N ) 5 (T_{N}+\theta P_{N})^{5} . A word with
exactly m m copies of P N P_{N} and 5 − m 5-m copies of T N T_{N} can be written
uniquely in the form 

 

 
 
 T N r 0 P N T N r 1 P N ⋯ P N T N r m , r 0 + ⋯ + r m = 5 − m , T_{N}^{r_{0}}P_{N}T_{N}^{r_{1}}P_{N}\cdots P_{N}T_{N}^{r_{m}},\qquad r_{0}+\cdots+r_{m}=5-m, 
 
 
 
 where the exponents r i r_{i} are allowed to be zero. For such a word, using
 P N = b N ​ b N ⊤ P_{N}=b_{N}b_{N}^{\top} , its contribution to the quadratic form with b N b_{N} is 

 

 
 
 b N ⊤ T N r 0 P N T N r 1 P N ⋯ P N T N r m b N = ∏ ℓ = 0 m b N ⊤ T N r ℓ b N = ∏ ℓ = 0 m s N ( r ℓ ) . b_{N}^{\top}T_{N}^{r_{0}}P_{N}T_{N}^{r_{1}}P_{N}\cdots P_{N}T_{N}^{r_{m}}b_{N}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{m}b_{N}^{\top}T_{N}^{r_{\ell}}b_{N}=\prod_{\ell=0}^{m}s_{N}(r_{\ell}). 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 b N ⊤ ​ M N , θ 5 ​ b N = ∑ m = 0 5 θ m ​ ∑ r 0 , … , r m ≥ 0 r 0 + ⋯ + r m = 5 − m ∏ ℓ = 0 m s N ​ ( r ℓ ) . b_{N}^{\top}M_{N,\theta}^{5}b_{N}=\sum_{m=0}^{5}\theta^{m}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r_{0},\ldots,r_{m}\geq 0\\
r_{0}+\cdots+r_{m}=5-m\end{subarray}}\prod_{\ell=0}^{m}s_{N}(r_{\ell}). 
 
 
 
 By ( 26 ), each coefficient of this polynomial in θ \theta is at
least the corresponding coefficient for N = 7 N=7 . Since 0 ≤ θ ≤ 1 0\leq\theta\leq 1 ,
this coefficientwise comparison gives ( 25 ). 



 
 It remains to compute the stable N = 7 N=7 model. A direct exact multiplication in this 7 × 7 7\times 7 model gives 

 

 
 
 b 7 ⊤ ​ M 7 , θ 5 ​ b 7 = 64 ​ θ 5 + 240 ​ θ 4 + 440 ​ θ 3 + 507 ​ θ 2 + 387 ​ θ + 174 = q 5 ​ ( θ ) . b_{7}^{\top}M_{7,\theta}^{5}b_{7}=64\theta^{5}+240\theta^{4}+440\theta^{3}+507\theta^{2}+387\theta+174=q_{5}(\theta). 
 
 
 
 The exact rational verification of this 7 × 7 7\times 7 computation is recorded in Section  C.3 . Hence, by ( 25 ), the reduced local model satisfies 

 

 
 
 b N ⊤ ​ M N , θ 5 ​ b N ≥ q 5 ​ ( θ ) . b_{N}^{\top}M_{N,\theta}^{5}b_{N}\geq q_{5}(\theta). 
 
 
 


 
 Restoring the pendant leaves that were removed in the definition of H 0 H_{0} can only increase the fifth moment seen from b b . Let R 0 R_{0} be the set of right-side vertices of H 0 H_{0} , ordered as above, and let R 1 R_{1} be the set of remaining right-side vertices of H H . Order the columns of B H B_{H} as R 0 R_{0} followed by R 1 R_{1} , and write B H = ( C 0 ​ C 1 ) B_{H}=(C_{0}\ C_{1}) . Then 

 

 
 
 B H ⊤ ​ B H = ( C 0 ⊤ ​ C 0 C 0 ⊤ ​ C 1 C 1 ⊤ ​ C 0 C 1 ⊤ ​ C 1 ) . B_{H}^{\top}B_{H}=\begin{pmatrix}C_{0}^{\top}C_{0}&C_{0}^{\top}C_{1}\\
C_{1}^{\top}C_{0}&C_{1}^{\top}C_{1}\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 The rows of B H B_{H} coming from the subgraph H 0 H_{0} contribute exactly T N T_{N} to the upper-left block. All remaining rows contribute only entrywise non-negative terms. Hence C 0 ⊤ ​ C 0 = T N + Δ C_{0}^{\top}C_{0}=T_{N}+\Delta for some entrywise non-negative matrix Δ \Delta . 



 
 Moreover, b = e a + e c b=e_{a}+e_{c} is supported on R 0 R_{0} , and in these block coordinates b = ( b N 0 ) b=\binom{b_{N}}{0} . Since M N , θ = T N + θ ​ b N ​ b N ⊤ M_{N,\theta}=T_{N}+\theta b_{N}b_{N}^{\top} , we obtain 

 

 
 
 M θ \displaystyle M_{\theta} 
 = B H ⊤ ​ B H + θ ​ b ​ b ⊤ \displaystyle=B_{H}^{\top}B_{H}+\theta bb^{\top} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = ( T N + Δ C 0 ⊤ ​ C 1 C 1 ⊤ ​ C 0 C 1 ⊤ ​ C 1 ) + ( θ ​ b N ​ b N ⊤ 0 0 0 ) \displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}T_{N}+\Delta&C_{0}^{\top}C_{1}\\
C_{1}^{\top}C_{0}&C_{1}^{\top}C_{1}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\theta b_{N}b_{N}^{\top}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = ( M N , θ + Δ X X ⊤ Y ) , \displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}M_{N,\theta}+\Delta&X\\
X^{\top}&Y\end{pmatrix}, 
 
 
 
 where X = C 0 ⊤ ​ C 1 X=C_{0}^{\top}C_{1} , Y = C 1 ⊤ ​ C 1 Y=C_{1}^{\top}C_{1} , and X , Y X,Y are entrywise non-negative. 



 
 Now define 

 

 
 
 M ~ N , θ := ( M N , θ 0 0 0 ) . \widetilde{M}_{N,\theta}:=\begin{pmatrix}M_{N,\theta}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 The displayed block decomposition shows that M θ ≥ M ~ N , θ M_{\theta}\geq\widetilde{M}_{N,\theta} entrywise. Since both matrices are entrywise non-negative, entrywise domination is preserved under multiplication, and hence under taking positive integer powers. Therefore M θ 5 ≥ M ~ N , θ  5 M_{\theta}^{5}\geq\widetilde{M}_{N,\theta}^{\,5} entrywise and 

 

 
 
 b ⊤ ​ M θ 5 ​ b ≥ b ⊤ ​ M ~ N , θ  5 ​ b = b N ⊤ ​ M N , θ 5 ​ b N ≥ q 5 ​ ( θ ) . b^{\top}M_{\theta}^{5}b\geq b^{\top}\widetilde{M}_{N,\theta}^{\,5}b=b_{N}^{\top}M_{N,\theta}^{5}b_{N}\geq q_{5}(\theta). 
 
 
 


 
 It remains to prove the spectral support bound. We shall show that every row
sum of M θ M_{\theta} is at most 5 5 . Since M θ M_{\theta} is entrywise
non-negative, the row-sum bound for nonnegative matrices
 [ 12 , Theorem 8.1.22] gives 

 

 
 
 ρ ⁡ ( M θ ) ≤ max ⁡ ∑ z y ⁡ ( M θ ) y ​ z . \rho(M_{\theta})\leq\max_{y}\sum_{z}(M_{\theta})_{yz}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the desired spectral upper bound follows once all row sums are shown to be
at most 5 5 . 



 
 We first estimate the row sums of M = B H ⊤ ​ B H M=B_{H}^{\top}B_{H} . For a right-side vertex
 y y , with the sum over z z running over right-side vertices, we have 

 

 
 
 ∑ z M y ​ z \displaystyle\sum_{z}M_{yz} 
 = ∑ z ∑ ℓ ∈ L ( B H ) ℓ ​ y ​ ( B H ) ℓ ​ z \displaystyle=\sum_{z}\sum_{\ell\in L}(B_{H})_{\ell y}(B_{H})_{\ell z} 
 
 
 
 
 
 = ∑ ℓ ∈ N H ​ ( y ) ∩ L ∑ z ( B H ) ℓ ​ z = ∑ ℓ ∈ N H ​ ( y ) ∩ L d H ​ ( ℓ ) , \displaystyle=\sum_{\ell\in N_{H}(y)\cap L}\sum_{z}(B_{H})_{\ell z}=\sum_{\ell\in N_{H}(y)\cap L}d_{H}(\ell), 
 
 
 
 where L L is the left side of the bipartition. Thus the row sum at y y is
the sum of the degrees, in H H , of the left neighbors of y y . 



 
 We now check these sums case by case. If y ≠ a y\neq a is a loaded cycle vertex on the right side, then its two cycle neighbors are unloaded by 3 3 -separation, and its pendant leaf has degree 1 1 . Hence the row sum is at most 

 

 
 
 2 + 2 + 1 = 5 . 2+2+1=5. 
 
 
 
 For y = a y=a , the neighbor u u has been deleted. Thus the only remaining left neighbors of a a are its other cycle neighbor, of degree at most 2 2 , and its pendant leaf, of degree 1 1 . Hence the row sum is at most 

 

 
 
 2 + 1 = 3 . 2+1=3. 
 
 
 
 If y ≠ c y\neq c is an unloaded cycle vertex on the right side, then at most one of its two left cycle neighbors is loaded. Therefore the row sum is at most 

 

 
 
 3 + 2 = 5 . 3+2=5. 
 
 
 
 For y = c y=c , the deleted vertex u u removes one of the two cycle-neighbor
contributions, so c c has only one left neighbor in H H , of degree at most
 3 3 . Hence the row sum is at most 3 3 . Finally, if y y is a right-side
pendant leaf, then y y has a unique left neighbor, and that neighbor has
degree at most 3 3 . Hence the row sum is again at most 3 3 . 



 
 The rank-one term θ ​ b ​ b ⊤ \theta bb^{\top} , with b = e a + e c b=e_{a}+e_{c} , affects only the
rows a a and c c . In each of those two rows it adds 2 ​ θ ≤ 2 2\theta\leq 2 to the
row sum. Since the row sums of M M at a a and c c are at most 3 3 , and
all other row sums of M M are at most 5 5 , every row sum of M θ M_{\theta} is
at most 5 5 . Hence ρ ⁡ ( M θ ) ≤ 5 \rho(M_{\theta})\leq 5 . Since M θ ⪰ 0 M_{\theta}\succeq 0 , all
eigenvalues of M θ M_{\theta} are non-negative, and therefore 

 

 
 
 spec ⁡ ( M θ ) ⊂ [ 0 , 5 ] . ∎ \spec(M_{\theta})\subset[0,5].\qed 
 
 
 






 
 6.4. Moment data for the path endpoint increment


 
 We shall need the following moment bound for the spectral-shift interval set associated with adding one endpoint to a path.



 
 Lemma 6.3 . 

 
 For every n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , let E n P E_{n}^{P} be the path endpoint spectral-shift interval
set for P n − 1 ↝ P n P_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow P_{n} . Then, for every integer m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , 

 

 
 
 ∫ E n P y m − 1 ​ 𝑑 y ≤ 1 2 ​ m ​ ( 2 ​ m m ) . \int_{E_{n}^{P}}y^{m-1}\,dy\leq\frac{1}{2m}\binom{2m}{m}. 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Use the notation from Lemma  5.5 . Let 

 

 
 
 β 1 ≥ α 1 ≥ β 2 ≥ α 2 ≥ ⋯ ≥ 0 \beta_{1}\geq\alpha_{1}\geq\beta_{2}\geq\alpha_{2}\geq\cdots\geq 0 
 
 
 
 be the interlacing order for the squared singular values in the endpoint update P n − 1 ↝ P n P_{n-1}\rightsquigarrow P_{n} , after zero padding if necessary. Thus { β i } i \{\beta_{i}\}_{i} and { α i } i \{\alpha_{i}\}_{i} are the multisets { μ i ​ ( P n ) } i \{\mu_{i}(P_{n})\}_{i} and { μ i ​ ( P n − 1 ) } i \{\mu_{i}(P_{n-1})\}_{i} , respectively, and E n P = ⋃ i [ α i , β i ] E_{n}^{P}=\bigcup_{i}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}] . 



 
 Let A k A_{k} be the adjacency matrix of P k P_{k} . Since P k P_{k} is bipartite, its nonzero adjacency eigenvalues are the pairs ± μ i ​ ( P k ) \pm\sqrt{\mu_{i}(P_{k})} . Hence tr ⁡ A k 2 ​ m = 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( P k ) m \operatorname{tr}A_{k}^{2m}=2\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{k})^{m} . Therefore 

 

 
 
 ∫ E n P y m − 1 ​ 𝑑 y = ∑ i ∫ α i β i y m − 1 ​ 𝑑 y = 1 m ​ ∑ i ( β i m − α i m ) = tr ⁡ A n 2 ​ m − tr ⁡ A n − 1 2 ​ m 2 ​ m . \int_{E_{n}^{P}}y^{m-1}\,dy=\sum_{i}\int_{\alpha_{i}}^{\beta_{i}}y^{m-1}\,dy=\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i}(\beta_{i}^{m}-\alpha_{i}^{m})=\frac{\operatorname{tr}A_{n}^{2m}-\operatorname{tr}A_{n-1}^{2m}}{2m}. 
 
 
 
 It remains to prove 

 

 
 
 tr ⁡ A n 2 ​ m − tr ⁡ A n − 1 2 ​ m ≤ ( 2 ​ m m ) . \operatorname{tr}A_{n}^{2m}-\operatorname{tr}A_{n-1}^{2m}\leq\binom{2m}{m}. 
 
 
 


 
 The trace tr ⁡ A k 2 ​ m \operatorname{tr}A_{k}^{2m} counts closed walks of length 2 ​ m 2m in P k P_{k} . Therefore the difference tr ⁡ A n 2 ​ m − tr ⁡ A n − 1 2 ​ m \operatorname{tr}A_{n}^{2m}-\operatorname{tr}A_{n-1}^{2m} counts precisely those closed walks of length 2 ​ m 2m in P n P_{n} that visit the new endpoint n n . 



 
 We inject these walks into the set of balanced { ± 1 } \{\pm 1\} -sequences of length 2 ​ m 2m , where balanced means that the sequence contains exactly m m entries equal to + 1 +1 and exactly m m entries equal to − 1 -1 . Let 

 

 
 
 W = ( v 0 , v 1 , … , v 2 ​ m = v 0 ) W=(v_{0},v_{1},\ldots,v_{2m}=v_{0}) 
 
 
 
 be such a closed walk in P n P_{n} , and suppose that W W visits n n . Define 

 

 
 
 ε i := v i − v i − 1 ∈ { − 1 , + 1 } , i = 1 , … , 2 m . \varepsilon_{i}:=v_{i}-v_{i-1}\in\{-1,+1\},\qquad i=1,\ldots,2m. 
 
 
 
 Since W W is closed, ∑ i = 1 2 ​ m ε i = v 2 ​ m − v 0 = 0 \sum_{i=1}^{2m}\varepsilon_{i}=v_{2m}-v_{0}=0 . Thus the step sequence is balanced. 



 
 We claim that the map W ↦ ( ε 1 , … , ε 2 ​ m ) W\mapsto(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{2m}) is injective on the walks counted above. Put 

 

 
 
 S 0 = 0 , S j = ∑ i = 1 j ε i . S_{0}=0,\qquad S_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\varepsilon_{i}. 
 
 
 
 Then v j = v 0 + S j v_{j}=v_{0}+S_{j} . Since the walk stays in P n P_{n} , we have S j ≤ n − v 0 S_{j}\leq n-v_{0} for every j j . Since the walk visits n n , equality holds for at least one j j . Hence 

 

 
 
 n − v 0 = max 0 ≤ j ≤ 2 ​ m ⁡ S j . n-v_{0}=\max_{0\leq j\leq 2m}S_{j}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the initial vertex is determined uniquely by the step sequence: 

 

 
 
 v 0 = n − max 0 ≤ j ≤ 2 ​ m ⁡ S j . v_{0}=n-\max_{0\leq j\leq 2m}S_{j}. 
 
 
 
 Once v 0 v_{0} and the step sequence are known, the whole walk is determined. This proves injectivity. 



 
 There are exactly ( 2 ​ m m ) \binom{2m}{m} balanced { ± 1 } \{\pm 1\} -sequences of length
 2 ​ m 2m . Consequently, 

 

 
 
 tr ⁡ A n 2 ​ m − tr ⁡ A n − 1 2 ​ m ≤ ( 2 ​ m m ) , \operatorname{tr}A_{n}^{2m}-\operatorname{tr}A_{n-1}^{2m}\leq\binom{2m}{m}, 
 
 
 
 and the displayed moment bound follows.
∎ 







 
 6.5. Envelope comparison on 0 ≤ t ≤ 4 0\leq t\leq 4 


 
 We first obtain a low-threshold lower bound from the rank-one Jensen estimate.



 
 Lemma 6.4 . 

 
 Assume that G G is a 3 3 -separated sparse sun graph whose base cycle has length at least 6 6 . For 0 ≤ t ≤ 3 0\leq t\leq 3 , 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( t ) ≥ L av ​ ( t ) , J_{E_{G,u}}(t)\geq L_{\rm av}(t), 
 
 
 
 where 

 

 
 
 L av ​ ( t ) := 2 ​ ∫ 0 1 ( 3 2 + 2 ​ θ − t ) + ​ 𝑑 θ = { 5 − 2 ​ t , 0 ≤ t ≤ 3 / 2 , 1 2 ​ ( 7 2 − t ) 2 , 3 / 2 ≤ t ≤ 3 . L_{\rm av}(t):=2\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{3}{2}+2\theta-t\right)_{+}\,d\theta=\begin{cases}5-2t,&0\leq t\leq 3/2,\\[5.69054pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}\left(\dfrac{7}{2}-t\right)^{2},&3/2\leq t\leq 3.\end{cases} 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 Apply Lemma  5.2 to the update H ↝ G H\rightsquigarrow G , where the
added vertex is u u . In the notation of that lemma, 

 

 
 
 d = ‖ b ‖ 2 = d G ​ ( u ) = 2 . d=\|b\|^{2}=d_{G}(u)=2. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, Lemma  6.2 gives 

 

 
 
 γ = b ⊤ ​ M ​ b = 3 . \gamma=b^{\top}Mb=3. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) ≥ 2 ​ ∫ γ / d γ / d + d ( y − t ) + ​ 𝑑 y = 2 ​ ∫ 3 / 2 7 / 2 ( y − t ) + ​ 𝑑 y . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)\geq 2\int_{\gamma/d}^{\gamma/d+d}(y-t)_{+}\,dy=2\int_{3/2}^{7/2}(y-t)_{+}\,dy. 
 
 
 
 On the other hand, by the definition of the spectral-shift interval set, if α i \alpha_{i} and β i \beta_{i} are the eigenvalues of M M and M + b ​ b ⊤ M+bb^{\top} , respectively, paired by rank-one interlacing, then 

 

 
 
 E G , u = E ⁡ ( M , b ) = ⋃ i [ α i , β i ] . E_{G,u}=E(M,b)=\bigcup_{i}[\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}]. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) \displaystyle\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H) 
 = ∑ i ( ( β i − t ) + 2 − ( α i − t ) + 2 ) \displaystyle=\sum_{i}\left((\beta_{i}-t)_{+}^{2}-(\alpha_{i}-t)_{+}^{2}\right) 
 
 
 
 
 
 = 2 ​ ∑ i ∫ α i β i ( y − t ) + ​ d y = 2 ​ J E G , u ​ ( t ) . \displaystyle=2\sum_{i}\int_{\alpha_{i}}^{\beta_{i}}(y-t)_{+}\,dy=2J_{E_{G,u}}(t). 
 
 
 
 Hence 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( t ) ≥ ∫ 3 / 2 7 / 2 ( y − t ) + ​ 𝑑 y = 2 ​ ∫ 0 1 ( 3 2 + 2 ​ θ − t ) + ​ 𝑑 θ = L av ​ ( t ) . J_{E_{G,u}}(t)\geq\int_{3/2}^{7/2}(y-t)_{+}\,dy=2\int_{0}^{1}\left(\frac{3}{2}+2\theta-t\right)_{+}\,d\theta=L_{\rm av}(t). 
 
 
 
 The displayed piecewise formula follows by evaluating this elementary integral.
∎ 





 
 We next derive the high-threshold lower envelope from the fifth-moment information in Lemma  6.2 .

 (27)

 We shall use the following elementary moment envelope for the path endpoint interval set.



 
 Lemma 6.6 . 

 
 Let E ⊂ [ 0 , 4 ] E\subset[0,4] be a finite union of intervals. For every r > 1 r>1 and every 0 < t < 4 0<t<4 , 

 

 
 
 J E ​ ( t ) ≤ Γ r ​ ( t ) ​ ∫ E y r ​ 𝑑 y , J_{E}(t)\leq\Gamma_{r}(t)\int_{E}y^{r}\,dy, 
 
 
 
 where 

 

 
 
 Γ r ​ ( t ) := max t < y ≤ 4 ⁡ y − t y r = { ( r − 1 ) r − 1 r r ​ t r − 1 , 0 < t ≤ 4 ​ ( r − 1 ) r , 4 − t 4 r , 4 ​ ( r − 1 ) r ≤ t < 4 . \Gamma_{r}(t):=\max_{t<y\leq 4}\frac{y-t}{y^{r}}=\begin{cases}\dfrac{(r-1)^{r-1}}{r^{r}t^{r-1}},&0<t\leq\dfrac{4(r-1)}{r},\\[8.53581pt]
\dfrac{4-t}{4^{r}},&\dfrac{4(r-1)}{r}\leq t<4.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 For r = 1 r=1 , one has the simpler bound 

 

 
 
 J E ​ ( t ) ≤ ∫ E y ​ 𝑑 y ( t ≥ 0 ) . J_{E}(t)\leq\int_{E}y\,dy\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

 
 By the definition of Γ r ​ ( t ) \Gamma_{r}(t) , we have 

 

 
 
 ( y − t ) + ≤ Γ r ​ ( t ) ​ y r ( 0 ≤ y ≤ 4 ) . (y-t)_{+}\leq\Gamma_{r}(t)y^{r}\qquad(0\leq y\leq 4). 
 
 
 
 Integrating over E E gives 

 

 
 
 J E ​ ( t ) = ∫ E ( y − t ) + ​ 𝑑 y ≤ Γ r ​ ( t ) ​ ∫ E y r ​ 𝑑 y . J_{E}(t)=\int_{E}(y-t)_{+}\,dy\leq\Gamma_{r}(t)\int_{E}y^{r}\,dy. 
 
 
 


 
 It remains only to compute Γ r ​ ( t ) \Gamma_{r}(t) . For t < y ≤ 4 t<y\leq 4 , set ϕ ⁡ ( y ) = ( y − t ) / y r \phi(y)=(y-t)/y^{r} . Then 

 

 
 
 ϕ ′ ​ ( y ) = r ​ t − ( r − 1 ) ​ y y r + 1 . \phi^{\prime}(y)=\frac{rt-(r-1)y}{y^{r+1}}. 
 
 
 
 Thus the unconstrained maximizer is y ∗ = r ​ t / ( r − 1 ) y_{*}=rt/(r-1) . If y ∗ ≤ 4 y_{*}\leq 4 , equivalently t ≤ 4 ​ ( r − 1 ) / r t\leq 4(r-1)/r , then 

 

 
 
 Γ r ​ ( t ) = ϕ ⁡ ( y ∗ ) = ( r − 1 ) r − 1 r r ​ t r − 1 . \Gamma_{r}(t)=\phi(y_{*})=\frac{(r-1)^{r-1}}{r^{r}t^{r-1}}. 
 
 
 
 If y ∗ > 4 y_{*}>4 , equivalently t ≥ 4 ​ ( r − 1 ) / r t\geq 4(r-1)/r , then the maximum occurs at y = 4 y=4 , and 

 

 
 
 Γ r ​ ( t ) = ϕ ⁡ ( 4 ) = 4 − t 4 r . \Gamma_{r}(t)=\phi(4)=\frac{4-t}{4^{r}}. 
 
 
 
 The case r = 1 r=1 follows immediately from ( y − t ) + ≤ y (y-t)_{+}\leq y .
∎ 





 
 The following scalar certificate completes the comparison between the lower
envelopes and the path-side upper envelopes.

 With L av L_{\rm av} and L 5 L_{5} as in Lemmas  6.4 and  6.5 , one has 

 

 
 
 L av ​ ( t ) ≥ U 1 ​ ( t ) ( 0 ≤ t ≤ 3 ) , L_{\rm av}(t)\geq U_{1}(t)\qquad(0\leq t\leq 3), 
 
 
 
 and

 Proof.

the following five scalar inequalities:

L_{\rm av}(t)\geq\dfrac{1701}{640t^{3}},&2\leq t\leq 3,\\[5.69054pt]

 Using the explicit formula for L av L_{\rm av} , and clearing positive

 G 0 ​ ( t ) := 7 2 − 2 ​ t ≥ 0 ( 0 ≤ t ≤ 3 / 2 ) , G_{0}(t):=\frac{7}{2}-2t\geq 0\qquad(0\leq t\leq 3/2), 
 
 
 
 

 
 
 G 1 ​ ( t ) := 3 ​ t ​ ( 7 2 − t ) 2 − 5 ≥ 0 ( 3 / 2 ≤ t ≤ 2 ) , G_{1}(t):=3t\left(\frac{7}{2}-t\right)^{2}-5\geq 0\qquad(3/2\leq t\leq 2),

 We now clear denominators in the two inequalities involving L 5 L_{5} . Put

 Here deg ⁡ F 1 = 34 \deg F_{1}=34 and deg ⁡ F 2 = 16 \deg F_{2}=16 . 



 
 The remaining verification is a finite exact polynomial check. By the exact

 Since Bernstein basis functions are non-negative on the underlying interval,

∎ 





 
 We now combine the lower envelopes for the support-neighbor update with the upper envelopes for the path endpoint update on the compact interval 0 ≤ t ≤ 4 0\leq t\leq 4 .



 
 Lemma 6.8 . 

 
 Assume that G G is a 3 3 -separated sparse sun graph whose base cycle has length at least 6 6 . Then 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( t ) ≥ J E n P ​ ( t ) ( 0 ≤ t ≤ 4 ) . J_{E_{G,u}}(t)\geq J_{E_{n}^{P}}(t)\qquad(0\leq t\leq 4). 
 
 
 




 
 Proof.

L_{5}(t),&3\leq t\leq 4.\end{cases} 
 
 
 
 On the path side, Lemma  6.3 gives 

 

 
 
 ∫ E n P y ​ 𝑑 y ≤ 3 2 , ∫ E n P y 2 ​ 𝑑 y ≤ 10 3 , ∫ E n P y 4 ​ 𝑑 y ≤ 126 5 , ∫ E n P y 19 ​ 𝑑 y ≤ C 19 = 1 20 ​ ( 39 19 ) . \int_{E_{n}^{P}}y\,dy\leq\frac{3}{2},\qquad\int_{E_{n}^{P}}y^{2}\,dy\leq\frac{10}{3},\qquad\int_{E_{n}^{P}}y^{4}\,dy\leq\frac{126}{5},\qquad\int_{E_{n}^{P}}y^{19}\,dy\leq C_{19}=\frac{1}{20}\binom{39}{19}.

 while for 3 ≤ t ≤ 4 3\leq t\leq 4 ,

∎ 







 
 6.6. The exceptional C 4 C_{4} case


 
 It remains to handle the unique terminal case not covered by the compact
comparison above, namely the four-cycle with one attached leaf.



 
 Lemma 6.9 . 

 
 Let G G be obtained from C 4 C_{4} by attaching one pendant leaf to a cycle vertex. If u u is a cycle neighbor of this loaded vertex, then Theorem  6.1 holds. 





 
 Proof.

 
 In this case H = G − u H=G-u is isomorphic to P 4 P_{4} . The nonzero squared singular
values are 

 

 
 
 μ ⁡ ( G ) = { 5 + 17 2 , 5 − 17 2 } , μ ⁡ ( H ) = μ ⁡ ( P 4 ) = { 3 + 5 2 , 3 − 5 2 } . \mu(G)=\left\{\frac{5+\sqrt{17}}{2},\frac{5-\sqrt{17}}{2}\right\},\qquad\mu(H)=\mu(P_{4})=\left\{\frac{3+\sqrt{5}}{2},\frac{3-\sqrt{5}}{2}\right\}. 
 
 
 
 Moreover, μ ⁡ ( P 5 ) = { 3 , 1 } \mu(P_{5})=\{3,1\} . By the definition of the spectral-shift interval set, the update
 H ↝ G H\rightsquigarrow G satisfies 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) = 2 ​ J E G , u ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)=2J_{E_{G,u}}(t). 
 
 
 
 Similarly, for the path endpoint update P 4 ↝ P 5 P_{4}\rightsquigarrow P_{5} , we have 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( P 5 ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P 4 ) = 2 ​ J E 5 P ​ ( t ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{5})-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{4})=2J_{E_{5}^{P}}(t). 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 2 ​ ( J E G , u ​ ( t ) − J E 5 P ​ ( t ) ) = 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P 5 ) + 𝖲 t ​ ( P 4 ) . 2\bigl(J_{E_{G,u}}(t)-J_{E_{5}^{P}}(t)\bigr)=\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(H)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{5})+\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{4}). 
 
 
 
 Since H ≅ P 4 H\cong P_{4} , we have
 𝖲 t ​ ( H ) = 𝖲 t ​ ( P 4 ) \mathsf{S}_{t}(H)=\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{4}) . Hence 

 

 
 
 2 ​ ( J E G , u ​ ( t ) − J E 5 P ​ ( t ) ) = 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P 5 ) . 2\bigl(J_{E_{G,u}}(t)-J_{E_{5}^{P}}(t)\bigr)=\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{5}). 
 
 
 
 Using the squared singular values displayed above, this becomes 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( t ) − J E 5 P ​ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ [ ( 5 + 17 2 − t ) + 2 + ( 5 − 17 2 − t ) + 2 − ( 3 − t ) + 2 − ( 1 − t ) + 2 ] . J_{E_{G,u}}(t)-J_{E_{5}^{P}}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{5+\sqrt{17}}{2}-t\right)_{+}^{2}+\left(\frac{5-\sqrt{17}}{2}-t\right)_{+}^{2}-(3-t)_{+}^{2}-(1-t)_{+}^{2}\right]. 
 
 
 
 Set a := ( 5 − 17 ) / 2 a:=(5-\sqrt{17})/2 and b := ( 5 + 17 ) / 2 b:=(5+\sqrt{17})/2 . It remains to prove 

 

 
 
 Φ ⁡ ( t ) := 1 2 ​ [ ( b − t ) + 2 + ( a − t ) + 2 − ( 3 − t ) + 2 − ( 1 − t ) + 2 ] ≥ 0 . \Phi(t):=\frac{1}{2}\left[(b-t)_{+}^{2}+(a-t)_{+}^{2}-(3-t)_{+}^{2}-(1-t)_{+}^{2}\right]\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 The only breakpoints of this positive-part expression are a , 1 , 3 , b a,1,3,b , and they satisfy 0 < a < 1 < 3 < b 0<a<1<3<b . 



 
 If 0 ≤ t ≤ a 0\leq t\leq a , then Φ ⁡ ( t ) = 11 / 2 − t > 0 \Phi(t)=11/2-t>0 . If a ≤ t ≤ 1 a\leq t\leq 1 , then 

 

 
 
 Φ ⁡ ( t ) = − t 2 2 + 3 − 17 2 ​ t + 1 + 5 ​ 17 4 . \Phi(t)=-\frac{t^{2}}{2}+\frac{3-\sqrt{17}}{2}t+\frac{1+5\sqrt{17}}{4}. 
 
 
 
 This quadratic is decreasing on [ a , 1 ] [a,1] , and Φ ⁡ ( 1 ) = ( 5 + 3 ​ 17 ) / 4 > 0 \Phi(1)=(5+3\sqrt{17})/4>0 . If 1 ≤ t ≤ 3 1\leq t\leq 3 , then 

 

 
 
 Φ ⁡ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ [ ( b − t ) 2 − ( 3 − t ) 2 ] = 1 2 ​ ( b − 3 ) ​ ( b + 3 − 2 ​ t ) ≥ 0 , \Phi(t)=\frac{1}{2}\left[(b-t)^{2}-(3-t)^{2}\right]=\frac{1}{2}(b-3)(b+3-2t)\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 because b > 3 b>3 and b + 3 − 2 ​ t ≥ b − 3 b+3-2t\geq b-3 on this interval. If 3 ≤ t ≤ b 3\leq t\leq b , then Φ ⁡ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ ( b − t ) 2 ≥ 0 \Phi(t)=\frac{1}{2}(b-t)^{2}\geq 0 , and if
 t ≥ b t\geq b , then Φ ⁡ ( t ) = 0 \Phi(t)=0 . Therefore Φ ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 0 \Phi(t)\geq 0 for every
 t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 .
∎ 





 
 We are now ready to present the following.



 
 Proof of Theorem  6.1 .

 
 If the cycle length is 4 4 , the result is Lemma  6.9 . Assume that the cycle length is at least 6 6 . By Lemma  6.8 , 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( t ) ≥ J E n P ​ ( t ) ( 0 ≤ t ≤ 4 ) . J_{E_{G,u}}(t)\geq J_{E_{n}^{P}}(t)\qquad(0\leq t\leq 4). 
 
 
 
 For t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 , the path endpoint interval set is supported in [ 0 , 4 ] [0,4] , so J E n P ​ ( t ) = 0 J_{E_{n}^{P}}(t)=0 , while J E G , u ​ ( t ) ≥ 0 J_{E_{G,u}}(t)\geq 0 . Hence the inequality holds for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 .
∎ 









 
 7. Completion of the proof


 
 We first complete the proof of the bipartite stop-loss comparison.



 
 Proof of Theorem  4.5 .

 
 Suppose, for contradiction, that the second-order stop-loss comparison fails. Choose a connected bipartite counterexample G G of minimum order, and fix a threshold t 0 ≥ 0 t_{0}\geq 0 witnessing the failure: 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( G ) < 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( P n ) , n = | V ⁡ ( G ) | . \mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(G)<\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(P_{n}),\qquad n=|V(G)|. 
 
 
 
 By the minimality of G G , every connected bipartite graph of order strictly smaller than n n satisfies the comparison against the path of the same order for every threshold. In particular, every connected component produced by any vertex deletion from G G satisfies the comparison for all thresholds. 



 
 The tree case is excluded by Corollary  4.12 . Hence G G is not a tree, and Proposition  5.9 applies: G G is a sparse sun graph whose loaded cycle vertices are 3 3 -separated. 



 
 If this sparse sun has no pendant leaves, then it is just an even cycle, which is excluded by Theorem  4.16 . Thus G G has at least one pendant leaf. Choose a loaded cycle vertex and an unloaded cycle neighbor u u , and put H = G − u H=G-u . Deleting u u breaks the base cycle into a path, while all pendant leaves remain attached to this path. Hence H H is a tree on n − 1 n-1 vertices. By Corollary  4.12 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( H ) ≥ 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( P n − 1 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(H)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(P_{n-1}). 
 
 
 


 
 By Theorem  6.1 , 

 

 
 
 J E G , u ​ ( s ) ≥ J E n P ​ ( s ) ( s ≥ 0 ) . J_{E_{G,u}}(s)\geq J_{E_{n}^{P}}(s)\qquad(s\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 Evaluating at s = t 0 s=t_{0} and using the defining relation between spectral-shift intervals and second-order stop-loss increments, we obtain 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( H ) = 2 ​ J E G , u ​ ( t 0 ) ≥ 2 ​ J E n P ​ ( t 0 ) = 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( P n ) − 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( P n − 1 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(H)=2J_{E_{G,u}}(t_{0})\geq 2J_{E_{n}^{P}}(t_{0})=\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(P_{n})-\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(P_{n-1}). 
 
 
 
 Adding this inequality to
 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( H ) ≥ 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( P n − 1 ) \mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(H)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(P_{n-1}) gives 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t 0 ​ ( P n ) , \mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t_{0}}(P_{n}), 
 
 
 
 contradicting the choice of t 0 t_{0} . Therefore the second-order stop-loss
comparison holds for every connected bipartite graph and every threshold
 t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 .
∎ 





 
 We now prove the main p p -energy theorem.



 
 Proof of Theorem  1.3 .

 
 If n = 1 n=1 , the assertion is trivial. Hence assume n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 . 



 
 The case p = 2 p=2 follows from 

 

 
 
 ℰ 2 ​ ( G ) = tr ⁡ A ​ ( G ) 2 = 2 ​ | E ⁡ ( G ) | ≥ 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) = ℰ 2 ​ ( P n ) , \mathcal{E}_{2}(G)=\operatorname{tr}A(G)^{2}=2|E(G)|\geq 2(n-1)=\mathcal{E}_{2}(P_{n}), 
 
 
 
 because G G is connected. 



 
 We next record the equality case for p = 4 p=4 . For every simple graph, 

 

 
 
 ℰ 4 ​ ( G ) = tr ⁡ A ​ ( G ) 4 = 2 ​ m + 4 ​ ∑ v ∈ V ⁡ ( G ) ( d v 2 ) + 8 ​ C 4 ​ ( G ) , \mathcal{E}_{4}(G)=\operatorname{tr}A(G)^{4}=2m+4\sum_{v\in V(G)}\binom{d_{v}}{2}+8C_{4}(G), 
 
 
 
 where m = | E ⁡ ( G ) | m=|E(G)| , d v = d G ​ ( v ) d_{v}=d_{G}(v) , and C 4 ​ ( G ) C_{4}(G) is the number of
 4 4 -cycles in G G . For the path, 

 

 
 
 ℰ 4 ​ ( P n ) = 2 ​ ( n − 1 ) + 4 ​ ( n − 2 ) = 6 ​ n − 10 . \mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n})=2(n-1)+4(n-2)=6n-10. 
 
 
 
 If m ≥ n m\geq n , then, since G G is connected and n ≥ 2 n\geq 2 , every vertex has
positive degree and 

 

 
 
 ∑ v ( d v 2 ) ≥ ∑ v ( d v − 1 ) = 2 ​ m − n . \sum_{v}\binom{d_{v}}{2}\geq\sum_{v}(d_{v}-1)=2m-n. 
 
 
 
 Hence 

 

 
 
 ℰ 4 ​ ( G ) ≥ 2 ​ m + 4 ​ ( 2 ​ m − n ) = 10 ​ m − 4 ​ n ≥ 6 ​ n > 6 ​ n − 10 = ℰ 4 ​ ( P n ) . \mathcal{E}_{4}(G)\geq 2m+4(2m-n)=10m-4n\geq 6n>6n-10=\mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 
 If m = n − 1 m=n-1 , then G G is a tree. In this case 

 

 
 
 ∑ v ( d v 2 ) ≥ ∑ v ( d v − 1 ) = n − 2 , \sum_{v}\binom{d_{v}}{2}\geq\sum_{v}(d_{v}-1)=n-2, 
 
 
 
 with equality if and only if every vertex has degree at most 2 2 . A
connected tree with maximum degree at most 2 2 is a path. Thus 

 

 
 
 ℰ 4 ( G ) = ℰ 4 ( P n ) ⟺ G ≅ P n . \mathcal{E}_{4}(G)=\mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad G\cong P_{n}. 
 
 
 
 This proves the theorem, including the equality case, when p = 4 p=4 . 



 
 Now assume p > 2 p>2 . We first prove the strict equality statement in the
connected bipartite case. 



 
 Let G G be connected and bipartite. If 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , then by
Theorem  3.6 , we know that R 1 ​ ( G , x ) ≥ R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) R_{1}(G;x)\geq R_{1}(P_{n};x) for every x > 0 x>0 . Theorem  3.2 gives 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) . \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 
 Suppose equality holds. Put α = p / 2 ∈ ( 1 , 2 ) \alpha=p/2\in(1,2) . By the Mellin
representation used in Theorem  3.2 , 

 

 
 
 0 = ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) α − ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) α = c α ​ ∫ 0 ∞ ( R 1 ​ ( G , x ) − R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) ) ​ x − α − 1 ​ 𝑑 x . 0=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{\alpha}-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{\alpha}=c_{\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(R_{1}(G;x)-R_{1}(P_{n};x)\bigr)x^{-\alpha-1}\,dx. 
 
 
 
 The integrand is non-negative and continuous in x > 0 x>0 , while the weight is
strictly positive. Therefore 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) = R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) ( x > 0 ) . R_{1}(G;x)=R_{1}(P_{n};x)\qquad(x>0). 
 
 
 
 Since 

 

 
 
 r 1 ​ ( y ) = y − log ⁡ ( 1 + y ) = y 2 2 + O ⁡ ( y 3 ) ( y → 0 ) , r_{1}(y)=y-\log(1+y)=\frac{y^{2}}{2}+O(y^{3})\qquad(y\to 0), 
 
 
 
 we have 

 

 
 
 R 1 ​ ( G , x ) = x 2 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) 2 + O ⁡ ( x 3 ) , R 1 ​ ( P n , x ) = x 2 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) 2 + O ⁡ ( x 3 ) . R_{1}(G;x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{2}+O(x^{3}),\qquad R_{1}(P_{n};x)=\frac{x^{2}}{2}\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{2}+O(x^{3}). 
 
 
 
 Hence ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) 2 = ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) 2 \sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{2}=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{2} . Because both graphs are bipartite, 

 

 
 
 ℰ 4 ​ ( G ) = 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) 2 , ℰ 4 ​ ( P n ) = 2 ​ ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) 2 . \mathcal{E}_{4}(G)=2\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{2},\qquad\mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n})=2\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{2}. 
 
 
 
 Thus ℰ 4 ​ ( G ) = ℰ 4 ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{4}(G)=\mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n}) , and the p = 4 p=4 equality case
proved above gives G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . 



 
 If p > 4 p>4 , put α = p / 2 > 2 \alpha=p/2>2 . By Theorem  4.5 , 

 

 
 
 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) ≥ 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) ( t ≥ 0 ) . \mathsf{S}_{t}(G)\geq\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})\qquad(t\geq 0). 
 
 
 
 Using the integral representation 

 

 
 
 u α = α ​ ( α − 1 ) ​ ( α − 2 ) 2 ​ ∫ 0 ∞ ( u − t ) + 2 ​ t α − 3 ​ 𝑑 t ( u ≥ 0 ) , u^{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)(\alpha-2)}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}(u-t)_{+}^{2}t^{\alpha-3}\,dt\qquad(u\geq 0), 
 
 
 
 we obtain 

 

 
 
 ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) α − ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) α \displaystyle\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{\alpha}-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{\alpha} 
 = α ​ ( α − 1 ) ​ ( α − 2 ) 2 ​ ∫ 0 ∞ ( 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) ) ​ t α − 3 ​ d t ≥ 0 . \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)(\alpha-2)}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\bigl(\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n})\bigr)t^{\alpha-3}\,dt\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 Therefore 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) . \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 
 If G ≇ P n G\not\cong P_{n} , then the p = 4 p=4 equality case gives ℰ 4 ​ ( G ) > ℰ 4 ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{4}(G)>\mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n}) . 



 
 Equivalently, 

 

 
 
 𝖲 0 ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 0 ​ ( P n ) = ∑ i μ i ​ ( G ) 2 − ∑ i μ i ​ ( P n ) 2 = 1 2 ​ ( ℰ 4 ​ ( G ) − ℰ 4 ​ ( P n ) ) > 0 . \mathsf{S}_{0}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{0}(P_{n})=\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(G)^{2}-\sum_{i}\mu_{i}(P_{n})^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathcal{E}_{4}(G)-\mathcal{E}_{4}(P_{n})\bigr)>0. 
 
 
 
 By continuity of t ↦ 𝖲 t ​ ( G ) − 𝖲 t ​ ( P n ) t\mapsto\mathsf{S}_{t}(G)-\mathsf{S}_{t}(P_{n}) , the difference
is positive on some interval ( 0 , ε ) (0,\varepsilon) . Since
 t α − 3 > 0 t^{\alpha-3}>0 on ( 0 , ε ) (0,\varepsilon) , the above integral is strictly
positive. Hence 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) > ℰ p ​ ( P n ) ( p > 4 ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)>\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n})\qquad(p>4) 
 
 
 
 unless G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . 



 
 Combining the cases 2 < p < 4 2<p<4 , p = 4 p=4 , and p > 4 p>4 , we have proved that, for
every connected bipartite graph G G on n n vertices and every p > 2 p>2 , 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) , \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}), 
 
 
 
 with equality if and only if G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . 



 
 Finally let G G be an arbitrary connected graph on n n vertices, and let p > 2 p>2 . By Lemma  2.1 , there exists a connected bipartite spanning subgraph H H of G G such that ℰ p ​ ( H ) ≤ ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)\leq\mathcal{E}_{p}(G) . The connected bipartite case applied to H H gives ℰ p ​ ( H ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) , and therefore 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( G ) ≥ ℰ p ​ ( P n ) . \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)\geq\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}). 
 
 
 


 
 It remains to identify equality. Suppose ℰ p ​ ( G ) = ℰ p ​ ( P n ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(G)=\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}) . Then both inequalities above must be equalities. In particular, 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( H ) = ℰ p ​ ( P n ) , \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)=\mathcal{E}_{p}(P_{n}), 
 
 
 
 so the bipartite equality case gives H ≅ P n H\cong P_{n} . 



 
 We now use equality in the bipartite reduction. In the proof of
Lemma  2.1 , one has 

 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( H ) = A ⁡ ( G ) + ( − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ) 2 . A(H)=\frac{A(G)+(-DA(G)D)}{2}. 
 
 
 


 
 By the strict convexity of finite-dimensional Schatten p p -spaces for 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty   [ 3 , Corollary 1] , the Schatten norm has strictly convex unit ball. Consequently, for M ≠ N M\neq N , 

 

 
 
 ‖ M + N 2 ‖ S p p < ‖ M ‖ S p p + ‖ N ‖ S p p 2 . \left\|\frac{M+N}{2}\right\|_{S_{p}}^{p}<\frac{\|M\|_{S_{p}}^{p}+\|N\|_{S_{p}}^{p}}{2}. 
 
 
 
 Indeed, if ‖ M ‖ S p = ‖ N ‖ S p \|M\|_{S_{p}}=\|N\|_{S_{p}} , this follows directly from strict
convexity of the unit ball; if ‖ M ‖ S p ≠ ‖ N ‖ S p \|M\|_{S_{p}}\neq\|N\|_{S_{p}} , it follows from
the triangle inequality and the strict convexity of x ↦ x p x\mapsto x^{p} . Hence equality in 

 

 
 
 ℰ p ​ ( H ) = ‖ A ⁡ ( G ) + ( − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ) 2 ‖ S p p ≤ ‖ A ⁡ ( G ) ‖ S p p + ‖ − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D ‖ S p p 2 = ℰ p ​ ( G ) \mathcal{E}_{p}(H)=\left\|\frac{A(G)+(-DA(G)D)}{2}\right\|_{S_{p}}^{p}\leq\frac{\|A(G)\|_{S_{p}}^{p}+\|-DA(G)D\|_{S_{p}}^{p}}{2}=\mathcal{E}_{p}(G) 
 
 
 
 forces A ⁡ ( G ) = − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D A(G)=-DA(G)D . Writing A ⁡ ( G ) A(G) with respect to the bipartition
 V ⁡ ( G ) = X ⊔ Y V(G)=X\sqcup Y used in the construction of H H , we have 

 

 
 
 A ⁡ ( G ) = ( A X B B ⊤ A Y ) . A(G)=\begin{pmatrix}A_{X}&B\\
B^{\top}&A_{Y}\end{pmatrix}. 
 
 
 
 The identity A ⁡ ( G ) = − D ​ A ​ ( G ) ​ D A(G)=-DA(G)D forces A X = A Y = 0 A_{X}=A_{Y}=0 . Hence G G has no edges inside X X or inside Y Y . Therefore the bipartite spanning subgraph H H constructed in Lemma  2.1 contains all edges of G G , and so G = H G=H . Since H ≅ P n H\cong P_{n} , we get G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} . 



 
 The reverse implication is immediate: if G ≅ P n G\cong P_{n} , then equality holds for every p p . This completes the proof.
∎ 







 
 Appendix A Finite certificates for the path-splicing low range


 
 This appendix gives the finite verification used in the low-range part of Theorem  4.14 . The verification is not a sampling argument. It is
a rigorous interval computation with rational subdivision endpoints and outward-rounded ball arithmetic, implemented in Arb  [ 13 ] .
The computations are carried out in SageMath  [ 21 ] , using its
built-in RealBallField class as the Arb interface to Arb  [ 13 ] .



 
 We use the notation from the proof of Theorem  4.14 . For
 1 / 6 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 1/6\leq\rho\leq 1/2 ,

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) = M ⁡ ( ρ ) + J p + q − 1 ​ ( ρ ) − J p ​ ( ρ ) − J q ​ ( ρ ) , K_{p,q}(\rho)=M(\rho)+J_{p+q-1}(\rho)-J_{p}(\rho)-J_{q}(\rho), 
 
 
 
 and

 

 
 
 D p ​ ( ρ ) = M ⁡ ( ρ ) − J p ​ ( ρ ) . D_{p}(\rho)=M(\rho)-J_{p}(\rho). 
 
 
 


 
 Lemma A.1 . 

 
 The following inequalities hold. 

 
 (i) 
 
 For each p = 2 , … , 6 p=2,\ldots,6 and every 1 / 6 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 1/6\leq\rho\leq 1/2 , 

 

 
 
 D p ​ ( ρ ) − 17 ​ 3 ​ π 2 27 ​ ( 1 7 2 + 1 ( p + 6 ) 2 ) ≥ 10 − 4 . D_{p}(\rho)-\frac{17\sqrt{3}\pi^{2}}{27}\left(\frac{1}{7^{2}}+\frac{1}{(p+6)^{2}}\right)\geq 10^{-4}. 
 
 
 



 (ii) 
 
 For each 2 ≤ p ≤ q ≤ 6 2\leq p\leq q\leq 6 and every 1 / 6 ≤ ρ ≤ 1 / 2 1/6\leq\rho\leq 1/2 , 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) ≥ 10 − 4 . K_{p,q}(\rho)\geq 10^{-4}. 
 
 
 



 




 
 Proof.

 
 The proof is a finite rigorous interval computation. We describe the
computation precisely. 



 
 For the strip inequalities in (i), we use 

 

 
 
 D p ​ ( ρ ) = ∫ 0 ρ ( 𝟏 u < 1 / 6 + u − { p ​ u } ) ​ w ρ ​ ( u ) ​ 𝑑 u , D_{p}(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\bigl(\mathbf{1}_{u<1/6}+u-\{pu\}\bigr)w_{\rho}(u)\,du, 
 
 
 
 where w ρ ​ ( u ) = 16 ​ π ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ​ ( cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ) w_{\rho}(u)=16\pi\sin(2\pi u)\bigl(\cos(2\pi u)-\cos(2\pi\rho)\bigr) . On each interval cut out by the rational breakpoints 

 

 
 
 0 , 1 6 , 1 2 , k p ( 0 ≤ k ≤ p ) , 0,\quad\frac{1}{6},\quad\frac{1}{2},\quad\frac{k}{p}\quad(0\leq k\leq p), 
 
 
 
 the coefficient 𝟏 u < 1 / 6 + u − { p ​ u } \mathbf{1}_{u<1/6}+u-\{pu\} is affine in u u . Therefore D p ​ ( ρ ) D_{p}(\rho) is evaluated on each such cell using the elementary antiderivatives of 

 

 
 
 ( α + β ​ u ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) ​ cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) , ( α + β ​ u ) ​ sin ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ u ) , (\alpha+\beta u)\sin(2\pi u)\cos(2\pi u),\qquad(\alpha+\beta u)\sin(2\pi u), 
 
 
 
 with ρ \rho treated as an interval variable. 



 
 For the small-box inequalities in (ii), the active set is constant between consecutive rational breakpoints 

 

 
 
 1 6 , 1 2 , i p , j q , k p + q − 1 . \frac{1}{6},\quad\frac{1}{2},\quad\frac{i}{p},\quad\frac{j}{q},\quad\frac{k}{p+q-1}. 
 
 
 
 More explicitly, the signed atoms are 

 

 
 
 ( 0 , + 1 ) , ( 1 / 6 , − 1 ) , ( i / p , + 1 ) , ( j / q , + 1 ) , ( k / ( p + q − 1 ) , − 1 ) , (0,+1),\qquad(1/6,-1),\qquad(i/p,+1),\qquad(j/q,+1),\qquad(k/(p+q-1),-1), 
 
 
 
 restricted to those atoms with α ≤ ρ \alpha\leq\rho . On any subinterval not containing a breakpoint, this active set is fixed, and one has the exact formula 

 

 
 
 K p , q ​ ( ρ ) = 4 ​ ∑ α ∈ 𝒜 ω α ​ ( cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ α ) − cos ⁡ ( 2 ​ π ​ ρ ) ) 2 , K_{p,q}(\rho)=4\sum_{\alpha\in\mathcal{A}}\omega_{\alpha}\bigl(\cos(2\pi\alpha)-\cos(2\pi\rho)\bigr)^{2}, 
 
 
 
 where 𝒜 \mathcal{A} is the fixed active set on that subinterval and ω α ∈ { 1 , − 1 } \omega_{\alpha}\in\{1,-1\} is the corresponding sign. Endpoint conventions are harmless, since a newly active atom contributes zero at the breakpoint. 



 
 The interval certification is implemented in the SageMath script 

 

 
 
 core/verify_path_splicing.sage 
 
 
 
 available in the public GitHub repository  [ 15 ] . The script uses exact
rational subdivision endpoints and Sage’s RealBallField(256) , which
is an interface to Arb ball arithmetic. On each box the program computes a
ball enclosure for the residual 

 

 
 
 left-hand side − right-hand side − 10 − 4 . \text{left-hand side}-\text{right-hand side}-10^{-4}. 
 
 
 
 The box is accepted only if the lower endpoint of this residual ball is
strictly positive. Otherwise the box is bisected. Thus every accepted box
certifies the required inequality on the whole box, not merely at sample
points. The corresponding output log is 

 

 
 
 core/verify_path_splicing_output.txt . \texttt{core/verify\_path\_splicing\_output.txt}. 
 
 
 
 The script was run with SageMath 10.8.
∎ 







 
 Appendix B Elementary interval domination inequalities


 
 For convenience, we restate Lemma  5.7 here.



 
 Restatement of Lemma  5.7 . 
Let d , r d,r be integers with d ≥ r + 2 d\geq r+2 and r ≥ 1 r\geq 1 . Put

 

 
 
 a = d − r + 1 d , B = a + d . a=\frac{d-r+1}{d},\qquad B=a+d. 
 
 
 
 Then for every t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 ,

 

 
 
 ℐ [ a , B ] ​ ( t ) ≥ r ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathcal{I}_{[a,B]}(t)\geq r\,\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 Moreover, if d = r + 2 d=r+2 , equivalently a = 3 / d a=3/d and B = d + 3 / d B=d+3/d , then for every
 t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 ,

 

 
 
 ℐ [ a , B ] ​ ( t ) ≥ ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + ( d − 3 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . \mathcal{I}_{[a,B]}(t)\geq\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+(d-3)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 


 
 Proof of Lemma  5.7 .

 
 We shall repeatedly use the elementary formula 

 

 
 
 ℐ [ u , v ] ​ ( t ) = { 0 , t ≥ v , 1 2 ​ ( v − t ) 2 , u ≤ t ≤ v , ( v − u ) ​ ( u + v 2 − t ) , t ≤ u . \mathcal{I}_{[u,v]}(t)=\begin{cases}0,&t\geq v,\\[2.84526pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}(v-t)^{2},&u\leq t\leq v,\\[5.69054pt]
(v-u)\left(\dfrac{u+v}{2}-t\right),&t\leq u.\end{cases} 
 
 
 


 
 We first prove the main estimate and then the moreover clause. 



 
 For the main estimate, set m = d − r ≥ 2 m=d-r\geq 2 . Then a = ( m + 1 ) / d a=(m+1)/d and
 B = d + a ≥ d + 3 d ≥ 4 B=d+a\geq d+\frac{3}{d}\geq 4 . Let 

 

 
 
 H ⁡ ( t ) = ℐ [ a , B ] ​ ( t ) − r ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . H(t)=\mathcal{I}_{[a,B]}(t)-r\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 For t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 , ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) = 0 \mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t)=0 and H ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 0 H(t)\geq 0 . For
 3 ≤ t ≤ 4 3\leq t\leq 4 , 

 

 
 
 H ⁡ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ { ( B − t ) 2 − r ​ ( 4 − t ) 2 } . H(t)=\frac{1}{2}\{(B-t)^{2}-r(4-t)^{2}\}. 
 
 
 
 The ratio ( B − t ) / ( 4 − t ) (B-t)/(4-t) is increasing with respect to t t on [ 3 , 4 ) [3,4) . Hence it is enough to check t = 3 t=3 and we need to prove B − 3 ≥ r B-3\geq\sqrt{r} . Since m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , 

 

 
 
 B − 3 = r + m − 3 + m + 1 r + m ≥ r − 1 + 3 r + 2 , B-3=r+m-3+\frac{m+1}{r+m}\geq r-1+\frac{3}{r+2}, 
 
 
 
 and 

 

 
 
 ( r − 1 + 3 r + 2 ) 2 − r = ( r − 1 ) ​ ( r 3 + 2 ​ r 2 + r − 1 ) ( r + 2 ) 2 ≥ 0 . \left(r-1+\frac{3}{r+2}\right)^{2}-r=\frac{(r-1)(r^{3}+2r^{2}+r-1)}{(r+2)^{2}}\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 Thus H ≥ 0 H\geq 0 on [ 3 , 4 ] [3,4] . 



 
 For a ≤ t ≤ 3 a\leq t\leq 3 , 

 

 
 
 H ⁡ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ ( B − t ) 2 − r ⁡ ( 7 2 − t ) . H(t)=\frac{1}{2}(B-t)^{2}-r\left(\frac{7}{2}-t\right). 
 
 
 
 This is a convex quadratic with unique minimizer at t = a + m t=a+m . If m ≥ 3 m\geq 3 , then a + m > 3 a+m>3 , so the minimum on [ a , 3 ] [a,3] is attained at t = 3 t=3 , already handled. If m = 2 m=2 , then the critical point lies in the interval and 

 

 
 
 H ⁡ ( a + m ) = r ⁡ ( r 2 − 3 2 + 3 r + 2 ) ≥ 0 , H(a+m)=r\left(\frac{r}{2}-\frac{3}{2}+\frac{3}{r+2}\right)\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 with equality only at r = 1 r=1 . Hence H ≥ 0 H\geq 0 on [ a , 3 ] [a,3] . 



 
 For t ≤ a t\leq a , the function H H is affine with slope r − d < 0 r-d<0 . Therefore its minimum on ( − ∞ , a ] (-\infty,a] is attained at t = a t=a , which was covered in the previous paragraph. This proves the main estimate. 



 
 For the moreover clause, assume in addition that d = r + 2 d=r+2 . Then a = 3 / d ≤ 1 a=3/d\leq 1 and B = d + a B=d+a , so we may define 

 

 
 
 H d ​ ( t ) = ℐ [ a , B ] ​ ( t ) − ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) − ( d − 3 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) . H_{d}(t)=\mathcal{I}_{[a,B]}(t)-\mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)-(d-3)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t). 
 
 
 
 For t ≥ 4 t\geq 4 , ℐ [ 2 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) + ( d − 3 ) ​ ℐ [ 3 , 4 ] ​ ( t ) = 0 \mathcal{I}_{[2,4]}(t)+(d-3)\mathcal{I}_{[3,4]}(t)=0 , so H d ​ ( t ) ≥ 0 H_{d}(t)\geq 0 . For
 3 ≤ t ≤ 4 3\leq t\leq 4 , 

 

 
 
 H d ​ ( t ) = 1 2 ​ { ( B − t ) 2 − ( d − 2 ) ​ ( 4 − t ) 2 } . H_{d}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\{(B-t)^{2}-(d-2)(4-t)^{2}\}. 
 
 
 
 As above, ( B − t ) / ( 4 − t ) (B-t)/(4-t) is increasing on [ 3 , 4 ) [3,4) , so it suffices to check the endpoint t = 3 t=3 , namely B − 3 ≥ d − 2 B-3\geq\sqrt{d-2} . This follows from 

 

 
 
 ( d − 3 + 3 d ) 2 − ( d − 2 ) = ( d − 3 ) ​ ( d 3 − 4 ​ d 2 + 5 ​ d − 3 ) d 2 ≥ 0 . \left(d-3+\frac{3}{d}\right)^{2}-(d-2)=\frac{(d-3)(d^{3}-4d^{2}+5d-3)}{d^{2}}\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 For 2 ≤ t ≤ 3 2\leq t\leq 3 , H d ​ ( t ) H_{d}(t) is affine and direct differentiation gives 

 

 
 
 H d ′ ​ ( t ) = 1 − a ≥ 0 . H_{d}^{\prime}(t)=1-a\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 Thus the minimum on this interval is at t = 2 t=2 , where 

 

 
 
 H d ​ ( 2 ) = 1 2 ​ ( B − 2 ) 2 − 2 − 3 2 ​ ( d − 3 ) = ( d − 3 ) ​ ( d 3 − 4 ​ d 2 + 3 ​ d − 3 ) 2 ​ d 2 ≥ 0 . H_{d}(2)=\frac{1}{2}(B-2)^{2}-2-\frac{3}{2}(d-3)=\frac{(d-3)(d^{3}-4d^{2}+3d-3)}{2d^{2}}\geq 0. 
 
 
 
 For a ≤ t ≤ 2 a\leq t\leq 2 , we have
 H d ′ ​ ( t ) = t − a − 1 . H_{d}^{\prime}(t)=t-a-1. 
Since a = 3 / d ≤ 1 a=3/d\leq 1 , the point t = a + 1 t=a+1 lies in [ a , 2 ] [a,2] and H d H_{d} attains its minimum on [ a , 2 ] [a,2] at t = a + 1 t=a+1 . At this point, 

 

 
 
 H d ​ ( a + 1 ) = ( d − 3 ) ​ ( d 2 − 4 ​ d + 2 ) 2 ​ d ≥ 0 , H_{d}(a+1)=\frac{(d-3)(d^{2}-4d+2)}{2d}\geq 0, 
 
 
 
 because the expression is 0 0 when d = 3 d=3 , and d 2 − 4 ​ d + 2 > 0 d^{2}-4d+2>0 for
 d ≥ 4 d\geq 4 . Hence H d ​ ( t ) ≥ 0 H_{d}(t)\geq 0 for all t ∈ [ a , 2 ] t\in[a,2] . 



 
 Finally, for t ≤ a t\leq a , H d H_{d} is affine with negative slope, so its minimum
on ( − ∞ , a ] (-\infty,a] is attained at t = a t=a . Since t = a t=a belongs to the
previous interval, H d ​ ( a ) ≥ 0 H_{d}(a)\geq 0 . This proves the claim.
∎ 







 
 Appendix C Exact verification of finite auxiliary inequalities


 
 This appendix records the finite algebraic checks used above. All computations are over the rational field ℚ \mathbb{Q} . No floating-point approximation is used.



 
 C.1. Bernstein positivity criterion


 
 We use the following elementary Bernstein-basis certificate for polynomial
non-negativity on a rational interval. Let P ∈ ℚ ⁡ [ t ] P\in\mathbb{Q}[t] , let
 α < β \alpha<\beta be rational, and let m ≥ deg ⁡ P m\geq\deg P . The degree- m m 
Bernstein coefficients of P P on [ α , β ] [\alpha,\beta] are the rational numbers
 b 0 , … , b m b_{0},\ldots,b_{m} defined by

 

 
 
 P ⁡ ( ( 1 − x ) ​ α + x ​ β ) = ∑ k = 0 m b k ​ ( m k ) ​ x k ​ ( 1 − x ) m − k . P((1-x)\alpha+x\beta)=\sum_{k=0}^{m}b_{k}\binom{m}{k}x^{k}(1-x)^{m-k}. 
 
 
 
 If b k ≥ 0 b_{k}\geq 0 for every k k , then P ⁡ ( t ) ≥ 0 P(t)\geq 0 for every
 t ∈ [ α , β ] t\in[\alpha,\beta] . Indeed, for such t t we may write
 t = ( 1 − x ) ​ α + x ​ β t=(1-x)\alpha+x\beta with x = ( t − α ) / ( β − α ) ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] x=(t-\alpha)/(\beta-\alpha)\in[0,1] , and
each Bernstein basis function

 

 
 
 ( m k ) ​ x k ​ ( 1 − x ) m − k \binom{m}{k}x^{k}(1-x)^{m-k} 
 
 
 
 is non-negative on [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] .



 
 For reproducibility, we compute the coefficients by the standard conversion formula from the power basis to the Bernstein basis; see, for example, [ 6 , Eq. (2)] . If P ⁡ ( ( 1 − x ) ​ α + x ​ β ) = ∑ j = 0 m c j ​ x j P((1-x)\alpha+x\beta)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}c_{j}x^{j} , where the coefficients are padded by c j = 0 c_{j}=0 when necessary, then the degree- m m Bernstein coefficients are

 

 
 
 b k = ∑ j = 0 k c j ​ ( k j ) ( m j ) , 0 ≤ k ≤ m . b_{k}=\sum_{j=0}^{k}c_{j}\frac{\binom{k}{j}}{\binom{m}{j}},\qquad 0\leq k\leq m. 
 
 
 
 This is the formula used in the exact rational checks below.





 
 C.2. Scalar envelope certificates


 
 The polynomials used in Lemma  6.7 are

 

 
 
 G 0 ​ ( t ) = 7 2 − 2 ​ t , G 1 ​ ( t ) = 3 ​ t ​ ( 7 2 − t ) 2 − 5 , G 2 ​ ( t ) = 320 ​ t 3 ​ ( 7 2 − t ) 2 − 1701 , G_{0}(t)=\frac{7}{2}-2t,\qquad G_{1}(t)=3t\left(\frac{7}{2}-t\right)^{2}-5,\qquad G_{2}(t)=320t^{3}\left(\frac{7}{2}-t\right)^{2}-1701,

 where

 They are verified by exact rational arithmetic in the SageMath script

 C.3. Local fifth-moment and envelope algebra checks


 
 The exact polynomial and rational identities used in Lemmas  6.2 and  6.5 are verified as follows. Besides
the stable N = 7 N=7 moment polynomial q 5 q_{5} , we also check the expansion of
 P t ​ ( 1 − z ) P_{t}(1-z) , the numerator identity underlying the derivative formula for
 h ⁡ ( t ) = A ⁡ ( t ) / B ⁡ ( t ) h(t)=A(t)/B(t) , the exact value h ⁡ ( 3 ) = 1245 / 3677 h(3)=1245/3677 , and the rational
lower bound used for D ⁡ ( t ) D(t) .



 
 Exact SageMath algebra check. 
The exact rational verification is implemented in the SageMath script

 

 
 
 core/verify_local_moment.sage 
 
 
 
 in the public GitHub repository  [ 15 ] . The corresponding output log is

 

 
 
 core/verify_local_moment_output.txt . \texttt{core/verify\_local\_moment\_output.txt}. 
 
 
 
 The script checks the stable N = 7 N=7 moment polynomial q 5 q_{5} , the
coefficientwise non-negativity of the differences for 3 ≤ N ≤ 6 3\leq N\leq 6 , the
expansion of P t ​ ( 1 − z ) P_{t}(1-z) , the numerator identity for the derivative of
 h ⁡ ( t ) = A ⁡ ( t ) / B ⁡ ( t ) h(t)=A(t)/B(t) , the value h ⁡ ( 3 ) = 1245 / 3677 h(3)=1245/3677 , and the rational lower

 Instructions for reporting errors

 We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
 support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
 methods listed below:

 
 Click the "Report Issue" ( 
 
 ) button, located in the page header.

 
 Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.

 Our team has already identified the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
 may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
 should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
 all.

 Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a list of packages that need conversion , and welcome developer contributions .
